H_{t}\in\{0,1\}^{n^{\otimes r-1}}
for each time
t
as

(5.7)

\displaystyle H_{t}:=\sum_{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle j_{2},\dots,j_{r}\in[n]^{r-1}}}{{\text{indices span $\geq 2$ of $\text{supp}(P^{t}),\text{supp}({\mathcal{D}}_{t}),\text{supp}({\mathcal{D}}^{*}_{t})$}}}}e_{j_{2}}\otimes\dots\otimes e_{j_{r}}

Equivalently, we can define
H_{t}
as

(5.8)

\displaystyle H_{t}:=\mathbf{1}^{\otimes r-1}-(P^{t})^{\otimes r-1}-({\mathcal{D}}_{t})^{\otimes r-1}-({\mathcal{D}}_{t}^{*})^{\otimes r-1}

(i.e. the tensor product consisting of cross terms of
P^{t}
,
{\mathcal{D}}_{t}
, and
{\mathcal{D}}_{t}^{*}
) so that we may use the following identity for any
i\in[k]
(which holds true because
P^{t}+{\mathcal{D}}_{t}+{\mathcal{D}}_{t}^{*}=\mathbf{1}
by definition):

(5.9)

\displaystyle\langle e_{i}\otimes(P^{t})^{\otimes r-1},Q\rangle

(5.10)

\displaystyle=\langle e_{i}\otimes\mathbf{1}^{\otimes r-1},Q\rangle-\langle e_{i}\otimes({\mathcal{D}}^{t})^{\otimes r-1},Q\rangle-\langle e_{i}\otimes({\mathcal{D}}_{*}^{t})^{\otimes r-1},Q\rangle-\langle e_{i}\otimes H_{t},Q\rangle

When
r=2
, we define
H_{t}=\mathbf{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

5.2.
Proof of Theorem
5.1

In order to prove Theorem
5.1
, we will use four key results, Lemmas
5.2
,
5.3
,
5.4
, and
5.5
. Their proofs are given in Section
5.3
.

Lemma 5.2
.

Fix any
\varepsilon\in(0,\frac{1}{2})
. For any
\kappa>0
(where we allow
\kappa
to grow with
n
), define
{\mathcal{A}}(\kappa)
as the following random subset of
[k]
:

\begin{split}\mathcal{A}(\kappa)=\bigg\{i\in[k]:&\langle e_{i}\otimes(P^{t})^{\otimes r-1},Q\rangle\\
\geq&\langle e_{i}\otimes\mathbf{1}^{\otimes r-1},Q\rangle-(2\pi)^{-1/2}\left(\lVert{\mathcal{D}}_{t}\rVert_{0}^{r-1}+{\mathcal{H}}_{1,t}\right)-m_{\lambda,k}\left(\lVert{\mathcal{D}}_{t}^{*}\rVert_{0}^{r-1}+{\mathcal{H}}_{2,t}\right)\\
&-\frac{\kappa}{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}\left(2n^{(r-1)/2}+k^{(r-1)/2}\right)\\
&\text{ for all }t<T_{i}\bigg\},\end{split}

where
T_{i}
is defined in (
5.6
), and

(5.11)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{H}}_{1,t}:=n^{r-1}-k^{r-1}-\lVert{\mathcal{D}}_{t}\rVert_{0}^{r-1}-\lVert P_{t}\rVert_{0}^{r-1}+(k-\lVert{\mathcal{D}}_{t}^{*}\rVert_{0})^{r-1}

counts the number of choices of
(j_{2},\dots,j_{r})
such that at least one coordinate is not in the support of
\theta

and
each
(j_{2},\dots,j_{r})
spans at least two of
(P^{t},{\mathcal{D}}_{t},{\mathcal{D}}_{t}^{*})
. Further,

(5.12)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{H}}_{2,t}:=k^{r-1}-(k-\lVert{\mathcal{D}}_{t}^{*}\rVert_{0})^{r-1}-\lVert{\mathcal{D}}_{t}^{*}\rVert_{0}^{r-1}

counts the number of choices of
(j_{2},\dots,j_{r})
such that all coordinates are in the support of
\theta

and
each
(j_{2},\dots,j_{r})
spans both
P^{t}
and
{\mathcal{D}}_{t}^{*}
. Then,

(5.13)

{\mathbb{P}}\left(|{\mathcal{A}}(\kappa)|\geq(1-\varepsilon)k\right)\geq 1-\frac{3}{\kappa^{2}}

Lemma 5.3
.

The following holds with probability at least
1-\frac{4k}{n^{2}}
: every
i\in[k]
satisfies

(5.14)

\displaystyle\langle e_{i}\otimes\mathbf{1}^{\otimes r-1},Q\rangle\geq m_{\lambda,k}k^{r-1}+(2\pi)^{-1/2}\left(n^{r-1}-k^{r-1}\right)-2\sqrt{\frac{2}{c}}(\log n)^{1/2}n^{(r-1)/2}

where
c>0
is a universal constant.

Lemma 5.4
.

Let
C>0
be any constant. Then there exists a large enough constant
C_{\lambda}>0
such that if
\lambda\geq C_{\lambda}\frac{n^{\frac{r-1}{2}}}{k^{\frac{r}{2}-1}}\sqrt{\log n}
, then

(5.15)

\displaystyle\left(m_{\lambda,k}-(2\pi)^{-1/2}\right)k^{r-1}\geq C\sqrt{\log n}n^{(r-1)/2}

Lemma 5.5
.

There exists a constant
C=C(r)>0
such that with probability at least
1-e^{-n}
, the following holds for every
t
such that
0\leq t\leq n-\frac{3}{2}k-1
:

(5.16)

\displaystyle\min_{i:(P^{t})_{i}=1,\theta_{i}=0}\langle e_{i}\otimes(P^{t})^{\otimes r-1},Q\rangle\leq(2\pi)^{-1/2}\left(n-\lVert{\mathcal{D}}_{t}\rVert_{0}+\lVert{\mathcal{D}}_{t}^{*}\rVert_{0}\right)^{r-1}+Cn^{(r-1)/2}

We are now ready to prove Theorem
5.1
.

Proof of Theorem
5.1
.

We will use Lemmas
5.2
,
5.3
and
5.4
to obtain a relatively large lower bound on the value of
\langle e_{i}\otimes(P^{t})^{\otimes r-1},Q\rangle
for most coordinates
i
in the support of
\theta
, at all times before they are removed.

We will then use Lemma
5.5
to upper bound the value of
\langle e_{i}\otimes(P^{t})^{\otimes r-1},Q\rangle
when
i
is not in the support of
\theta
. Consequently, we will find that the minimizer of
\langle e_{i}\otimes(P^{t})^{\otimes r-1},Q\rangle
is “usually” outside the support of
\theta
, so most of the “good” coordinates are retained by Algorithm
3
.

Let
\varepsilon\in(0,\frac{1}{2})
be a constant, and let
\kappa>0
be a parameter to be determined (possibly growing to infinity). Using Lemmas
5.2
,
5.3
and
5.5
, we have via a union bound that the following events hold simultaneously with probability at least
1-3/\kappa^{2}-4k/n^{2}-e^{-n}
: first,

(5.18)

|{\mathcal{A}}(\kappa)|\geq(1-\varepsilon)k

where
{\mathcal{A}}(\kappa)
is defined in (
5.2
). Secondly, every
i\in[k]
satisfies

(5.19)

\displaystyle\langle e_{i}\otimes\mathbf{1}^{\otimes r-1},Q\rangle\geq m_{\lambda,k}k^{r-1}+(2\pi)^{-1/2}\left(n^{r-1}-k^{r-1}\right)-2\sqrt{\frac{2}{c}}(\log n)^{1/2}n^{(r-1)/2},

where
c>0
is the universal constant in (
5.3
). Finally, for every
0\leq t\leq n-\frac{3}{2}k-1
,

(5.20)

\displaystyle\min_{i:P^{t}_{i}=1,\theta_{i}=0}\langle e_{i}\otimes(P^{t})^{\otimes r-1},Q\rangle\leq(2\pi)^{-1/2}\left(n-\lVert{\mathcal{D}}_{t}\rVert_{0}+\lVert{\mathcal{D}}_{t}^{*}\rVert_{0}\right)^{r-1}+Cn^{(r-1)/2}

where
C=C(r)>0
is the sufficiently large constant in Lemma
5.5
.
Conditional on the events (
5.18
), (
5.19
) and (
5.20
), we will prove inductively that with probability 1,
{\mathcal{A}}(\kappa)\subseteq\text{supp}(P^{t})
for every
t\leq n-\frac{3}{2}k
, and therefore, for a sufficiently small choice of
\varepsilon
, we have that
P^{n-\frac{3}{2}k}
satisfies
\langle P^{n-\frac{3}{2}k},\theta\rangle\geq\frac{1}{8}k
, as desired. If we then choose
\kappa=\frac{1}{3}(\varepsilon\log n)^{1/2}
so that
1-3/\kappa^{2}-4k/n^{2}-e^{-n}=1-o(1)
, the theorem then follows.

Clearly we have
{\mathcal{A}}(\kappa)\subseteq P^{0}=\mathbf{1}
. Assume that
{\mathcal{A}}(\kappa)\subseteq\text{supp}(P^{t})
. Then
by definition of
{\mathcal{A}}(\kappa)
(see (
5.2
)), it holds for every
i\in{\mathcal{A}}(\kappa)
that

(5.21)

\displaystyle\langle e_{i}\otimes(P^{t})^{\otimes r-1},Q\rangle

(5.22)

\displaystyle\geq\langle e_{i}\otimes\mathbf{1}^{\otimes r-1},Q\rangle-(2\pi)^{-1/2}\left(\lVert{\mathcal{D}}_{t}\rVert_{0}^{r-1}+{\mathcal{H}}_{1,t}\right)-m_{\lambda,k}\left(\lVert{\mathcal{D}}_{t}^{*}\rVert_{0}^{r-1}+{\mathcal{H}}_{2,t}\right)

(5.23)

\displaystyle-\frac{\kappa}{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}\left(2n^{(r-1)/2}+k^{(r-1)/2}\right)

and by (
5.19
), we can lower bound this by

(5.24)

\displaystyle\geq m_{\lambda,k}k^{r-1}+(2\pi)^{-1/2}\left(n^{r-1}-k^{r-1}\right)-2\sqrt{\frac{2}{c}}(\log n)^{1/2}n^{(r-1)/2}

(5.25)

\displaystyle-(2\pi)^{-1/2}\left(\lVert{\mathcal{D}}_{t}\rVert_{0}^{r-1}+{\mathcal{H}}_{1,t}\right)-m_{\lambda,k}\left(\lVert{\mathcal{D}}_{t}^{*}\rVert_{0}^{r-1}+{\mathcal{H}}_{2,t}\right)

(5.26)

\displaystyle-\frac{\kappa}{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}\left(2n^{(r-1)/2}+k^{(r-1)/2}\right)

Next apply the trivial upper bound that
2n^{(r-1)/2}+k^{(r-1)/2}\leq 3n^{(r-1)/2}
, so this is at least

(5.27)

\displaystyle\geq m_{\lambda,k}k^{r-1}+(2\pi)^{-1/2}\left(n^{r-1}-k^{r-1}\right)-2\sqrt{\frac{2}{c}}(\log n)^{1/2}n^{(r-1)/2}

(5.28)

\displaystyle-(2\pi)^{-1/2}\left(\lVert{\mathcal{D}}_{t}\rVert_{0}^{r-1}+{\mathcal{H}}_{1,t}\right)-m_{\lambda,k}\left(\lVert{\mathcal{D}}_{t}^{*}\rVert_{0}^{r-1}+{\mathcal{H}}_{2,t}\right)-\frac{3\kappa n^{(r-1)/2}}{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}

Applying the definition of
{\mathcal{H}}_{1,t}
and
{\mathcal{H}}_{2,t}
and rearranging yields that this is equal to

(5.29)

\displaystyle=\left(m_{\lambda,k}-(2\pi)^{-1/2}\right)\left(k-\lVert{\mathcal{D}}_{t}^{*}\rVert_{0}\right)^{r-1}+\left(2\pi\right)^{-1/2}\left(n-\lVert{\mathcal{D}}_{t}\rVert_{0}-\lVert{\mathcal{D}}_{t}^{*}\rVert_{0}\right)^{r-1}

(5.30)

\displaystyle-2\sqrt{\frac{2}{c}}(\log n)^{1/2}n^{(r-1)/2}-\frac{3\kappa n^{(r-1)/2}}{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}

Recall that
{\mathcal{A}}(\kappa)\subseteq\text{supp}(P^{t})
by inductive hypothesis and
|{\mathcal{A}}(\kappa)|\geq(1-\varepsilon)k
by (
5.18
), and so
\lVert{\mathcal{D}}_{t}^{*}\rVert_{0}\leq\varepsilon k
, and thus this is at least

(5.31)

\displaystyle=(1-\varepsilon)^{r-1}\left(m_{\lambda,k}-(2\pi)^{-1/2}\right)k^{r-1}+\left(2\pi\right)^{-1/2}\left(n-\lVert{\mathcal{D}}_{t}\rVert_{0}-\lVert{\mathcal{D}}_{t}^{*}\rVert_{0}\right)^{r-1}

(5.32)

\displaystyle-2\sqrt{\frac{2}{c}}(\log n)^{1/2}n^{(r-1)/2}-\frac{3\kappa n^{(r-1)/2}}{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}

Now we apply Lemma
5.4
with
C=(2+2\sqrt{\frac{2}{c}})2^{-(r-1)}
and the bound
\varepsilon<\frac{1}{2}
, to get a bound of

(5.33)

\displaystyle\geq 2\sqrt{\log n}n^{(r-1)/2}+\left(2\pi\right)^{-1/2}\left(n-\lVert{\mathcal{D}}_{t}\rVert_{0}-\lVert{\mathcal{D}}_{t}^{*}\rVert_{0}\right)^{r-1}-\frac{3\kappa n^{(r-1)/2}}{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}

Finally, with the choice
\kappa=\frac{1}{3}(\varepsilon\log n)^{1/2}
, since
\varepsilon
is constant, we see that for large enough
n
this is lower bounded by

(5.34)

\displaystyle\geq\sqrt{\log n}n^{(r-1)/2}+\left(2\pi\right)^{-1/2}\left(n-\lVert{\mathcal{D}}_{t}\rVert_{0}-\lVert{\mathcal{D}}_{t}^{*}\rVert_{0}\right)^{r-1}

Combining all the above inequalities, we therefore have that for every
i\in{\mathcal{A}}(\kappa)
,

(5.36)

\displaystyle\sqrt{\log n}n^{(r-1)/2}+\left(2\pi\right)^{-1/2}\left(n-\lVert{\mathcal{D}}_{t}\rVert_{0}-\lVert{\mathcal{D}}_{t}^{*}\rVert_{0}\right)^{r-1}\leq\langle e_{i}\otimes(P^{t})^{\otimes r-1},Q\rangle

By (
5.20
), we have the upper bound

(5.37)

\displaystyle\min_{i:P^{t}_{i}=1,\theta_{i}=0}\langle e_{i}\otimes(P^{t})^{\otimes r-1},Q\rangle

\displaystyle\leq(2\pi)^{-1/2}\left(n-\lVert{\mathcal{D}}_{t}\rVert_{0}+\lVert{\mathcal{D}}_{t}^{*}\rVert_{0}\right)^{r-1}+Cn^{(r-1)/2}

(5.38)

\displaystyle<\left(2\pi\right)^{-1/2}\left(n-\lVert{\mathcal{D}}_{t}\rVert_{0}-\lVert{\mathcal{D}}_{t}^{*}\rVert_{0}\right)^{r-1}+\sqrt{\log n}n^{(r-1)/2}

(5.39)

\displaystyle\leq\min_{i\in{\mathcal{A}}(\kappa)}\langle e_{i}\otimes(P^{t})^{\otimes r-1},Q\rangle

where the second inequality holds for large enough
n
and the last inequality holds by (
5.36
). Thus,
{\mathcal{A}}(\kappa)\subseteq\text{supp}(P^{t+1})
, as desired, completing the proof.

∎

5.3.
Proofs of Key Lemmas

Towards proving Lemma
5.2
, we introduce Lemmas
5.6
and
5.7
. Their proofs are given in Section
5.4
. The arguments used are inspired by those in
[
GJX23
]
and
[
FR10
]
for the setting of detecting a planted clique in a random graph.

Lemma 5.6
.

For every
i\in[k]
, there exist stochastic processes
\{b_{t,1}(i)\}_{t\leq n}
and
\{b_{t,2}(i)\}_{t\leq n}
such that for each
t<T_{i}
(where
T_{i}
is defined in (
5.6
)), we have

(5.40)

\langle e_{i}\otimes({\mathcal{D}}_{t})^{\otimes r-1},Q\rangle\preceq_{\rm st}b_{t,1}(i),

(5.41)

\langle e_{i}\otimes({\mathcal{D}}_{t}^{*})^{\otimes r-1},Q\rangle\preceq_{\rm st}b_{t,2}(i),

and

(5.42)

\langle e_{i}\otimes H_{t},Q\rangle\preceq_{\rm st}b_{t,3}(i),

where
H_{t}
is defined in (
5.7
) (recall that
\preceq_{\rm st}
refers to stochastic domination). The process
\{b_{t,1}(i)\}_{t\leq n}
is given by

(5.43)

b_{t,1}(i)=\sum_{j=1}^{\lVert{\mathcal{D}}_{t}\rVert_{0}^{r-1}}\eta_{j,1}(i)

with initial value
\eta_{0,1}(i)=0
, where
\{\eta_{j,1}(i)\}_{j\geq 1}
are independent random variables such that for each
j
,
{\mathbb{P}}(\eta_{j,1}(i)=0)=\frac{1}{2}
, and with probability
\frac{1}{2}
,
\eta_{j,1}(i)
follows a
{\mathcal{N}}(0,1)
distribution truncated to the positive half of the real line.

In addition,
\{b_{t,2}(i)\}_{t<T_{i}}
is given by

(5.44)

b_{t,2}(i)=\sum_{j=1}^{\lVert{\mathcal{D}}^{*}_{t}\rVert_{0}^{r-1}}\eta_{j,2}(i)

with initial value
\eta_{0,2}(i)=0
, where
\{\eta_{j,2}(i)\}_{j\geq 1}
are independent random variables such that for each
j
,
{\mathbb{P}}(\eta_{j,2}(i)=0)=\Phi(-\frac{\lambda}{k^{r/2}})
, and with probability
1-\Phi\left(-\frac{\lambda}{k^{r/2}}\right)
,
\eta_{j,2}(i)
follows a
{\mathcal{N}}\left(\frac{\lambda}{k^{r/2}},1\right)
distribution truncated to the positive half of the real line.

Finally,
\{b_{t,3}(i)\}_{t<T_{i}}
is given by

(5.45)

b_{t,3}(i)=\sum_{j=1}^{{\mathcal{H}}_{1,t}}\eta_{j,3}(i)+\sum_{j=1}^{{\mathcal{H}}_{2,t}}\eta_{j,4}(i)

with initial values
\eta_{0,3}(i)=\eta_{0,4}(i)=0
, where
{\mathcal{H}}_{1,t}
and
{\mathcal{H}}_{2,t}
are defined in (
5.11
) and (
5.12
) respectively. Finally,
\{\eta_{j,3}(i)\}_{j\geq 1}
are i.i.d. with the same distribution as
\{\eta_{j,1}(i)\}_{j\geq 1}
, and
\{\eta_{j,4}(i)\}_{j\geq 1}
are i.i.d. with the same distribution as
\{\eta_{j,2}(i)\}_{j\geq 1}
.

Lemma 5.7
.

For each
i\in[k]
, let
b_{t,1}(i)
,
b_{t,2}(i)
and
b_{t,3}(i)
be the processes defined in Lemma
5.6
. Then for each
t
,

(5.46)

{\mathbb{E}}(b_{t,1}(i)|\lVert{\mathcal{D}}_{t}\rVert_{0})=(2\pi)^{-1/2}\lVert{\mathcal{D}}_{t}\rVert_{0}^{r-1},

(5.47)

{\mathbb{E}}(b_{t,2}(i)|\lVert{\mathcal{D}}_{t}^{*}\rVert_{0})=m_{\lambda,k}\lVert{\mathcal{D}}_{t}^{*}\rVert_{0}^{r-1}

and

(5.48)

{\mathbb{E}}(b_{t,3}(i)|{\mathcal{H}}_{1,t},{\mathcal{H}}_{2,t})=(2\pi)^{-1/2}{\mathcal{H}}_{1,t}+m_{\lambda,k}{\mathcal{H}}_{2,t}

It further holds that for each
t
that,

(5.49)

\mathrm{Var}(b_{t,1}(i))\leq n^{r-1},

(5.50)

\mathrm{Var}(b_{t,2}(i))\leq k^{r-1},

and

(5.51)

\mathrm{Var}(b_{t,3}(i))\leq n^{r-1}.

Proof of Lemma
5.2
.

Fix
i\in[k]
and
\kappa>0
. Let
b_{t,1}(i)
be the process in Lemma
5.6
that stochastically dominates
\langle e_{i}\otimes({\mathcal{D}}_{t})^{\otimes r-1},Q\rangle
. Observe that

(5.52)

b_{t,1}(i)-\frac{\lVert{\mathcal{D}}_{t}\rVert_{0}^{r-1}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}

is a martingale, and
T_{i}\leq n
is a bounded stopping time. For the remainder of this proof, we consider all probabilistic statements as conditional on the values of
\{{\mathcal{D}}_{t}\}_{t<T_{i}}
and
\{{\mathcal{D}}_{t}^{*}\}_{t<T_{i}}
. Thus we have by Lemma
5.6
and Doob’s martingale inequality that

(5.53)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}\left(\sup_{t<T_{i}}\left(\langle e_{i}\otimes({\mathcal{D}}^{t})^{\otimes r-1},Q\rangle-\frac{\lVert{\mathcal{D}}_{t}\rVert_{0}^{r-1}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\right)\geq\kappa\sqrt{\frac{n^{r-1}}{\varepsilon}}\right)

(5.54)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{Lemma \ref{lem:StochDomProcess}}}}{{\leq}}

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}\left(\sup_{t<T_{i}}\left(b_{t,1}(i)-\frac{\lVert{\mathcal{D}}_{t}\rVert_{0}^{r-1}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\right)\geq\kappa\sqrt{\frac{n^{r-1}}{\varepsilon}}\right)

(5.55)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}\left(\sup_{t\leq n-\frac{3}{2}k}\left(b_{t,1}(i)-\frac{\lVert{\mathcal{D}}_{t}\rVert_{0}^{r-1}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\right)\geq\kappa\sqrt{\frac{n^{r-1}}{\varepsilon}}\right)

(5.56)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}\left(\sup_{t\leq n-\frac{3}{2}k}\left(b_{t,1}(i)-\frac{\lVert{\mathcal{D}}_{t}\rVert_{0}^{r-1}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\right)^{2}\geq\left(\kappa\sqrt{\frac{n^{r-1}}{\varepsilon}}\right)^{2}\right)

(5.57)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{Doob}}}{{\leq}}

\displaystyle\frac{{\mathbb{E}}\left(\left(b_{n,1}(i)-\frac{\lVert{\mathcal{D}}_{n}\rVert_{0}^{r-1}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\right)^{2}\right)}{\left(\kappa\sqrt{\frac{n^{r-1}}{\varepsilon}}\right)^{2}}

and since the numerator is just the variance of
\mathrm{Var}\left(b_{n-\frac{3}{2}k,1}(i)\right)\leq n^{r-1}
by Lemma
5.7
, we get that this is at most

(5.58)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{n^{r-1}}{\left(\kappa\sqrt{\frac{n^{r-1}}{\varepsilon}}\right)^{2}}=\frac{\varepsilon}{\kappa^{2}}

Next, let
b_{t,2}(i)
be the process in Lemma
5.6
that stochastically dominates
\langle e_{i}\otimes({\mathcal{D}}_{t}^{*})^{\otimes r-1},Q\rangle
. Recall that
m_{\lambda,k}=\frac{\lambda}{k^{r/2}}\Phi\left(\frac{\lambda}{k^{r/2}}\right)+\phi\left(\frac{\lambda}{k^{r/2}}\right)
, and notice that

(5.59)

\displaystyle b_{t,2}(i)-m_{\lambda,k}\lVert{\mathcal{D}}_{t}^{*}\rVert_{0}^{r-1}

is also a martingale. Then by an identical argument, we have that

(5.60)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}\left(\sup_{t<T_{i}}\left\langle e_{i}\otimes({\mathcal{D}}^{*}_{t})^{\otimes r-1},Q\right\rangle-m_{\lambda,k}\lVert{\mathcal{D}}_{t}^{*}\rVert_{0}^{r-1}\geq\kappa\sqrt{\frac{k^{r-1}}{\varepsilon}}\right)

(5.61)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{{\mathbb{E}}\left(\left(b_{n-\frac{3}{2}k,2}(i)-m_{\lambda,k}\lVert{\mathcal{D}}_{n-\frac{3}{2}k}^{*}\rVert_{0}^{r-1}\right)^{2}\right)}{\left(\kappa\sqrt{\frac{k^{r-1}}{\varepsilon}}\right)^{2}}

Again since the numerator is
\mathrm{Var}\left(b_{n-\frac{3}{2}k,2}(i)\right)\leq k^{r-1}
by Lemma
5.7
, we get that

(5.63)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}\left(\sup_{t<T_{i}}\left\langle e_{i}\otimes({\mathcal{D}}^{*}_{t})^{\otimes r-1},Q\right\rangle-m_{\lambda,k}\lVert{\mathcal{D}}_{t}^{*}\rVert_{0}^{r-1}\geq\kappa\sqrt{\frac{k^{r-1}}{\varepsilon}}\right)

(5.64)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{\kappa^{2}}

Now let
b_{t,3}(i)
be the process in Lemma
5.6
that stochastically dominates
\langle e_{i}\otimes H_{t},Q\rangle
. Then notice that

(5.65)

b_{t,3}(i)-(2\pi)^{-1/2}{\mathcal{H}}_{1,t}-m_{\lambda,k}{\mathcal{H}}_{2,t}

is also a martingale, and so by an identical argument to the above two cases, we have

(5.66)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\sup_{t<T_{i}}\left\langle e_{i}\otimes H_{t},Q\right\rangle-(2\pi)^{-1/2}{\mathcal{H}}_{1,t}-m_{\lambda,k}{\mathcal{H}}_{2,t}\geq\kappa\sqrt{\frac{n^{r-1}}{\varepsilon}}\right)

(5.67)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{\kappa^{2}}

Next, we note that by (
5.9
), we have the identity

(5.68)

\displaystyle\langle e_{i}\otimes(P^{t})^{\otimes r-1},Q\rangle=\langle e_{i}\otimes\mathbf{1}^{\otimes r-1},Q\rangle-\langle e_{i}\otimes({\mathcal{D}}^{t})^{\otimes r-1},Q\rangle-\langle e_{i}\otimes({\mathcal{D}}_{*}^{t})^{\otimes r-1},Q\rangle-\langle e_{i}\otimes H_{t},Q\rangle

Thus by (
5.58
), (
5.64
), and (
5.67
), we get that with probability at most
\frac{3\varepsilon}{\kappa^{2}}
, it holds that

(5.69)

\displaystyle\inf_{t<T_{i}}\langle e_{i}\otimes(P^{t})^{\otimes r-1},Q\rangle

(5.70)

\displaystyle<\langle e_{i}\otimes\mathbf{1}^{\otimes r-1},Q\rangle-(2\pi)^{-1/2}\left(\lVert{\mathcal{D}}_{t}\rVert_{0}^{r-1}+{\mathcal{H}}_{1,t}\right)-m_{\lambda,k}\left(\lVert{\mathcal{D}}_{t}^{*}\rVert_{0}^{r-1}+{\mathcal{H}}_{2,t}\right)

(5.71)

\displaystyle-\frac{\kappa}{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}\left(2n^{(r-1)/2}+k^{(r-1)/2}\right)

This holds for each
i\in[k]
, and therefore by Markov’s inequality we have

(5.72)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(|[k]\setminus{\mathcal{A}}(\kappa)|\geq\varepsilon k\right)\leq\frac{k\left(\frac{3\varepsilon}{\kappa^{2}}\right)}{\varepsilon k}=\frac{3}{\kappa^{2}}

as desired.
∎

Proof of Lemma
5.3
.

Fix
i\in[k]
. Note that we can decompose
\langle e_{i}\otimes\mathbf{1}^{\otimes r-1},Q\rangle
as

(5.73)

\displaystyle\langle e_{i}\otimes\mathbf{1}^{\otimes r-1},Q\rangle=\sum_{\underline{h}\in[k]^{r-1}}\max\left\{\frac{\lambda}{k^{r/2}}+W_{i\underline{h}},0\right\}+\sum_{\underline{h}\in[n]^{r-1}\setminus[k]^{r-1}]}\max\left\{W_{i\underline{h}},0\right\}

For each
\underline{h}\in[k]^{r-1}
,
\max\left\{\frac{\lambda}{k^{r/2}}+W_{i\underline{h}},0\right\}
is a subgaussian random variable with mean
m_{\lambda,k}
and variance at most
1
. This can be seen by noting that
\frac{\lambda}{k^{r/2}}+W_{i\underline{h}}\sim\mathcal{N}\left(\frac{\lambda}{k^{r/2}},0\right)
, and then applying the formula for the mean and variance of a truncated Gaussian random variable (see e.g. page 156 of
[
JKB94
]
). Then by Hoeffding’s inequality, it holds for some universal constant
c>0
that for any
t>0
,

(5.74)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}\left(\left|\sum_{\underline{h}\in[k]^{r-1}}\left(\max\left\{\frac{\lambda}{k^{r/2}}+W_{i\underline{h}},0\right\}-m_{\lambda,k}\right)\right|\geq\sqrt{t}\right)\leq 2\exp\left(\frac{-ct}{k^{r-1}}\right)

and so by taking
t=\frac{2}{c}k^{r-1}\log n
, we have that with probability at least
1-2n^{-2}
,

(5.75)

\displaystyle\sum_{\underline{h}\in[k]^{r-1}}\max\left\{\frac{\lambda}{k^{r/2}}+W_{i\underline{h}},0\right\}\geq m_{\lambda,k}k^{r-1}-\sqrt{\frac{2}{c}}k^{(r-1)/2}(\log n)^{1/2}.

By a similar argument with
t=\frac{2}{c}(n^{r-1}-k^{r-1})\log n
, it holds that

(5.76)

\displaystyle\sum_{\underline{h}\in[n]^{r-1}\setminus[k]^{r-1}]}\max\left\{W_{i\underline{h}},0\right\}

\displaystyle\geq(2\pi)^{-1/2}(n^{r-1}-k^{r-1})-\sqrt{\frac{2}{c}}\left(n^{r-1}-k^{r-1}\right)^{1/2}(\log n)^{1/2}

with probability at least
1-2n^{-2}
. We therefore see by (
5.73
) that with probability at least
1-4n^{-2}
,

(5.77)

\displaystyle\langle e_{i}\otimes\mathbf{1}^{\otimes r-1},Q\rangle

\displaystyle\geq m_{\lambda,k}k^{r-1}+(2\pi)^{-1/2}\left(n^{r-1}-k^{r-1}\right)-\sqrt{\frac{2}{c}}(\log n)^{1/2}\left(k^{(r-1)/2}+\left(n^{r-1}-k^{r-1}\right)^{1/2}\right)

(5.78)

\displaystyle\geq m_{\lambda,k}k^{r-1}+(2\pi)^{-1/2}\left(n^{r-1}-k^{r-1}\right)-2\sqrt{\frac{2}{c}}(\log n)^{1/2}n^{(r-1)/2}

Then by taking a union bound over all
i\in[k]
, the probability that this inequality holds for all
i
is at least

(5.79)

1-\frac{4k}{n^{2}}

as desired
∎

Proof of Lemma
5.4
.

Define the function
f(x)=\frac{1}{2}x+\phi(x)-(2\pi)^{-1/2}
. Then we claim that first that for all
x\geq 0
, it holds that
f(x)\geq\frac{1}{4}x
. To see this, note we have equality at 0, and that
f^{\prime}(x)=\frac{1}{2}-\frac{xe^{-x^{2}/2}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\geq\frac{1}{4}
for all
x>0
.
We have
\Phi(x)\geq\frac{1}{2}
for all
x>0
, and therefore it holds that

(5.80)

\displaystyle m_{\lambda,k}-(2\pi)^{-1/2}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\lambda}{2k^{r/2}}+\phi\left(\frac{\lambda}{k^{r/2}}\right)-(2\pi)^{-1/2}

(5.81)

\displaystyle=f\left(\frac{\lambda}{k^{r/2}}\right)\geq\frac{\lambda}{4k^{r/2}}

and therefore

(5.82)

\displaystyle\left(m_{\lambda,k}-(2\pi)^{-1/2}\right)k^{r-1}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{4}\lambda k^{\frac{r}{2}-1}

(5.83)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{4}C_{\lambda}\sqrt{\log n}n^{(r-1)/2}

(5.84)

\displaystyle\geq C\sqrt{\log n}n^{(r-1)/2}

for a large enough choice of
C_{\lambda}
.

∎

We introduce Lemma
5.8
towards proving Lemma
5.5
.

Lemma 5.8
.

Let
n\geq 7
. Let
G
be a tensor in
{\mathbb{R}}^{n^{\otimes r}}
with independent subgaussian entries, each with variance at most 1. For each
\sigma\in B_{\sigma}:=\left\{\sigma\in\{0,1\}^{n}:|i\in[n]:\sigma_{i}=1,\theta_{i}=0|\geq\frac{1}{2}k\right\}
, define the random variable
Z_{\sigma}
:

(5.85)

Z_{\sigma}=\frac{1}{|\{i\in[n]:\sigma_{i}=1,\theta_{i}=0\}|}\sum_{i:\sigma_{i}=1,\theta_{i}=0}\langle e_{i}\otimes\sigma^{\otimes r-1},G\rangle

Then, there exists a constant
C=C(r)>0
depending only on
r
such that the following holds with probability at least
1-e^{-n}
for large enough
n
:

(5.86)

\min_{i:\sigma_{i}=1,\theta_{i}=0}\langle e_{i}\otimes\sigma^{\otimes r-1},G\rangle\leq{\mathbb{E}}(Z_{\sigma})+Cn^{(r-1)/2}

Proof of Lemma
5.8
.

For each
\sigma\in\{0,1\}^{n}
, let
b_{\sigma}=|\{i\in[n]:\sigma_{i}=1,\theta_{i}=0\}|
. Consider a choice of

\sigma
with
b_{\sigma}\geq k/2
. Observe that
Z_{\sigma}
is a subgaussian random variable, whose variance is upper bounded by
\frac{(b_{\sigma}+k)^{r-1}}{b_{\sigma}}
. This is because the inner product
\langle e_{i}\otimes\sigma^{\otimes r-1},G\rangle
contains at most
\lVert\sigma\rVert_{0}^{r-1}\leq(b_{\sigma}+k)^{r-1}
nonzero terms, each of variance at most 1. Therefore,
\sum_{i:\sigma_{i}=1,\theta_{i}=0}\langle e_{i}\otimes\sigma^{\otimes r-1},G\rangle
has variance at most
b_{\sigma}(b_{\sigma}+k)^{r-1}
, so
Z_{\sigma}
has variance at most
\frac{(b_{\sigma}+k)^{r-1}}{b_{\sigma}}
. It therefore holds by Hoeffding’s inequality that for some universal constant
c>0
,

(5.87)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}(\left|Z_{\sigma}(j)-{\mathbb{E}}(Z_{\sigma}(j))\right|\geq\sqrt{t})\leq 2

\displaystyle\exp\left(-\frac{cb_{\sigma}t}{(b_{\sigma}+k)^{r-1}}\right)

(5.88)

\displaystyle\leq 2

\displaystyle\exp\left(-\frac{ct}{(3^{r-1})b_{\sigma}^{r-2}}\right)

where the second inequality holds because
b_{\sigma}\geq\frac{1}{2}k
by assumption. Let
t=\frac{3^{r-1}}{c}n^{r-1}
so that this probability is at most
2e^{-2n}
(because
b_{\sigma}\leq n
). As there are trivially less than
2^{n}
such choices of
\sigma
, by a union bound, the complement of event (
5.86
) has probability at most

(5.89)

2\cdot 2^{n}\cdot e^{-2n}\leq e^{-n}

for
n\geq 7
, completing the proof.
∎

Proof of Lemma
5.5
.

We first note that since
t\leq n-\frac{3}{2}k
, the conditions of Lemma
5.8
are satisfied with
G=Q
. It therefore holds that for some
r
-dependent constant
C>0
that, for every
t
, with probability at least
1-e^{-n}
that

(5.90)

\displaystyle\min_{i:(P^{t})_{i}=1,\theta_{i}=0}\langle e_{i}\otimes(P^{t})^{\otimes r-1},Q\rangle

(5.91)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left(\frac{1}{|\{i\in[n]:P^{t}_{i}=1,\theta_{i}=0\}|}\sum_{i:P^{t}_{i}=1,\theta_{i}=0}\langle e_{i}\otimes(P^{t})^{\otimes r-1},Q\rangle\right)+Cn^{(r-1)/2}

(5.92)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left(\langle e_{i}\otimes(P^{t})^{\otimes r-1},Q\rangle\right)+Cn^{(r-1)/2}

where
i
is any coordinate such that
P^{t}_{i}=1
and
\theta_{i}=0
. The inner product contains at most
\lVert P^{t}\rVert_{0}^{r-1}=(n-\lVert{\mathcal{D}}_{t}\rVert_{0}-\lVert{\mathcal{D}}_{t}^{*}\rVert_{0})^{r-1}
nonzero terms. Since
\theta_{i}=0
, each of these terms follows the distribution
X=\max\{Z,0\}
, where
Z\sim{\mathcal{N}}(0,1)
. A simple calculation yields that
\mathbb{E}(X)=(2\pi)^{-1/2}
, completing the proof.
∎

5.4.
Proofs of Auxiliary Lemmas

Here we prove Lemmas
5.6
and
5.7
.

Proof of Lemma
5.6
.

Here we follow the edge-exposure argument of
[
GJX23
]
and
[
FR10
]
, but applied to the case of sparse tensor PCA instead of planted clique. We demonstrate the construction of
b_{t,1}(i)
, as the arguments for
b_{t,2}(i)
and
b_{t,3}(i)
are similar, but with a shift in the mean to account for those coordinates lying in the support of
\theta
.

We introduce Algorithm
11
as a proof device. We briefly give an informal description of its operation. We generate
Y
, and apply the operation
\max\{\cdot,0\}
elementwise, similarly to Algorithm
3
. We then generate a masked tensor
\Gamma
where we conceal what we call the “connectivity” of every coordinate to
i
(where this terminology is used because these arguments are inspired by graph-theoretic results). We do this by erasing the coordinates of
Q
involving
i
in their first index.

For the current revealed state of the tensor
\Gamma^{t}
, and the current set of coordinates being considered
M^{t}
, Algorithm
11
then proceeds in a similar manner to Algorithm
3
, by finding the coordinate
s
that minimizes
\langle e_{s}\otimes(M^{t})^{\otimes r-1},\Gamma^{t}\rangle
, which we can view as as proxy for the “connectivity” of a coordinate
s
to the current set
M^{t}
. If at time
t
, coordinate
s
minimizes
\langle e_{s}\otimes(M^{t})^{\otimes r-1},\Gamma^{t}\rangle
, Algorithm
11
then updates the revealed information in the tensor
\Gamma
by revealing all the coordinates of
Q
that both have
i
as the first index and involve
s
. It then recalculates the minimizer. If a coordinate
s
is still the minimizer after having its “connectivity” revealed, the algorithm removes it from consideration by subtracting it from
M
. If not, it keeps looping until such a minimizer is found. After such a minimizer is found, Algorithm
11
then repeats this process until
n-\frac{3}{2}k
coordinates have been removed from
M
, similar to Algorithm
3
.

Our result is based on the intuitive idea that the revealed values
Q
for coordinates that are dropped should be smaller than the values for those that are not dropped, because the dropped coordinates were still the minimizers even after their connectivity was revealed.

Algorithm 11

Tentative Peeling Process for coordinate
i
.

0:

Y\in\mathbb{R}^{n^{\otimes r}}
,
i\in[k]

1:

Q\leftarrow\max\{Y,0\}
(elementwise)

2:

Let
\Gamma^{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{n^{\otimes r}}
.

3:

for

(j_{2},\dots,j_{r})\in[n]^{r-1}

do

4:

\Gamma^{0}_{i,j_{2},\dots,j_{r}}\leftarrow 0

5:

end

for

6:

Let
M^{0}\leftarrow\mathbf{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}

7:

for

t=0
to
n-3k/2-1

do

8:

\ell\leftarrow\operatorname*{arg\,min}\limits_{s:(M^{t})_{s}=1}\langle e_{s}\otimes(M^{t})^{\otimes r-1},\Gamma^{t}\rangle

9:

\tilde{\Gamma}^{t+1}\leftarrow\Gamma^{t}

10:

while

\exists(j_{2},\dots,j_{r})
containing
\ell
such that
\tilde{\Gamma}^{t+1}_{i,j_{2},\dots,j_{r}}\neq Q_{i,j_{2},\dots,j_{r}}

do

11:

for

(j_{2},\dots,j_{r})\in[n]^{r-1}

do

12:

\tilde{\Gamma}^{t+1}_{i,j_{2},\dots,j_{r}}\leftarrow Q_{i,j_{2},\dots,j_{r}}

13:

end

for

14:

\ell\leftarrow\operatorname*{arg\,min}\limits_{s:(M^{t})_{s}=1}\langle e_{s}\otimes(M^{t})^{\otimes r-1},\tilde{\Gamma}^{t+1}\rangle

15:

end

while

16:

\Gamma^{t+1}\leftarrow\tilde{\Gamma}^{t+1}

17:

\ell\leftarrow\operatorname*{arg\,min}\limits_{s:(M^{t})_{s}=1}\langle e_{s}\otimes(M^{t})^{\otimes r-1},\Gamma^{t+1}\rangle

18:

M^{t+1}\leftarrow M^{t}-e_{\ell}

19:

end

for

20:

return

M^{n-\frac{3}{2}k}

We now proceed with the proof. Fix a choice of
i\in[k]
. Let
\Gamma^{t}
and
M^{t}
be the iterates of Algorithm
11
with input
Y
and
i\in[k]
, and let
P^{t}
be the iterates of Algorithm
3
with input
Y
. The first step of our proof is to show that for each
t<T_{i}
,
M^{t}=P^{t}
with probability 1, where
T_{i}=\min\{t:P^{t}_{i}=0\}
is the time that
i
is first dropped by Algorithm
3
.

We proceed by induction. The base case is trivial as
M^{0}=P^{0}=\mathbf{1}
. Suppose that for some
t
,
M^{t}=P^{t}
. Let
\ell\in[n]
be the coordinate removed from
M
by Algorithm
11
at time
t
, i.e. we have
M^{t}_{\ell}=1
and
M^{t+1}_{\ell}=0
. By the condition in the while loop of Algorithm
11
, this implies that the connectivity of
i
and
\ell
has been revealed at this time (i.e.
\Gamma^{t+1}_{i,j_{2},\dots,j_{r}}=Q_{1,j_{2},\dots,j_{r}}
for every
(j_{2},\dots,j_{r})
containing
\ell
). Thus,

(5.94)

\displaystyle\langle e_{\ell}\otimes(M^{t})^{\otimes r-1},\Gamma^{t+1}\rangle=\langle e_{\ell}\otimes(M^{t})^{\otimes r-1},Q\rangle=\langle e_{\ell}\otimes(P^{t})^{\otimes r-1},Q\rangle

Next, we note that by construction, for every
(j_{1},\dots,j_{r})
, it holds that
\Gamma^{t+1}_{j_{1},\dots,j_{r}}\leq Q_{j_{1},\dots,j_{r}}
. Therefore, since
M^{t}=P^{t}
by inductive hypothesis, we have for every
s\in[n]
that

(5.95)

\displaystyle\langle e_{s}\otimes(M^{t})^{\otimes r-1},\Gamma^{t+1}\rangle\leq\langle e_{s}\otimes(P^{t})^{\otimes r-1},Q\rangle

We then note that since
\ell=\operatorname*{arg\,min}\limits_{s:(M^{t})_{s}=1}\langle e_{s}\otimes(M^{t})^{\otimes r-1},\Gamma^{t+1}\rangle
by definition (and as
Q
’s elements are truncated normal random variables, the
\operatorname*{arg\,min}
is unique with probability 1), it therefore holds by (
5.94
) and (
5.95
) that

(5.96)

\ell=\operatorname*{arg\,min}\limits_{s:(M^{t})_{s}=1}\langle e_{s}\otimes(M^{t})^{\otimes r-1},\Gamma^{t+1}\rangle=\operatorname*{arg\,min}\limits_{s:(P^{t})_{s}=1}\langle e_{s}\otimes(P^{t})^{\otimes r-1},Q\rangle

(informally, if connectivity within
M
lower bounds connectivity within
P
, and
\ell
is the minimizer for
M
, and connectivity for
\ell
in
M
equals that for
\ell
in
P
, then
\ell
must also be the minimizer for
P
). Therefore,
M^{t+1}=P^{t+1}
with probability 1, which completes the induction.

Next, define the following two sets for each
t<T_{i}
:

(5.97)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{R}}_{t,i}:=\{s\in[n]\setminus[k]:\Gamma^{t+1}_{i,j_{2},\dots,j_{r}}=Q_{i,j_{2},\dots,j_{r}}\text{ for all }(j_{2},\dots,j_{r})\text{ containing $s$}\}

and

(5.98)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{R}}^{*}_{t,i}:=\{s\in[k]:\Gamma^{t+1}_{i,j_{2},\dots,j_{r}}=Q_{i,j_{2},\dots,j_{r}}\text{ for all }(j_{2},\dots,j_{r})\text{ containing $s$}\}

{\mathcal{R}}_{t,i}
can be viewed as the set of coordinates outside the support of
\theta
whose connectivity to the coordinate
i\in[k]=\text{supp}(\theta)
has been revealed by time
t
.
{\mathcal{R}}^{*}_{t,i}
is similar but for coordinates in
\text{supp}(\theta)
. For any choice of
t
,
i
and
s
, we claim that for each
(j_{2},\dots,j_{r})
containing
s
, the law of
Q_{i,j_{2},\dots,j_{r}}
is independent of the event
\{s\in{\mathcal{R}}_{t,i}\}
. This is because
Q
is not changed at any stage by Algorithm
11
, and because the decision to reveal
s
’s connectivity (i.e. to set
\Gamma^{t+1}_{i,j_{2},\dots,j_{r}}=Q_{i,j_{2},\dots,j_{r}}
) is taken before observing
Q_{i,j_{2},\dots,j_{r}}
. Therefore, conditional that
s\in{\mathcal{R}}_{t,i}
,
\Gamma^{t+1}_{i,j_{2},\dots,j_{r}}
for any
(j_{2},\dots,j_{r})
containing
s
must have the same law as the marginal law of
Q_{i,j_{2},\dots,j_{r}}
- that is,
0
with probability
\frac{1}{2}
, and with probability
\frac{1}{2}
following a
{\mathcal{N}}(0,1)
distribution truncated to be nonnegative.

A similar argument applies to
{\mathcal{R}}^{*}_{t,i}
. Conditional that
s\in{\mathcal{R}}^{*}_{t,i}
,
\Gamma^{t+1}_{i,j_{2},\dots,j_{r}}
for any
(j_{2},\dots,j_{r})
containing
s
must have the same law as the marginal law of
Q_{i,j_{2},\dots,j_{r}}
. When at least one of
(j_{2},\dots,j_{r})
lies outside the support of
\theta
that marginal law is as above,
0
with probability
\frac{1}{2}
, and with probability
\frac{1}{2}
following a
{\mathcal{N}}(0,1)
distribution truncated to be nonnegative. On the other hand, when all of
(j_{2},\dots,j_{r})
are in
\text{supp}(\theta)
, then the marginal distribution is 0 with probability
\Phi(-\frac{\lambda}{k^{r/2}})
, and with probability
1-\Phi\left(-\frac{\lambda}{k^{r/2}}\right)
, follows a
{\mathcal{N}}\left(\frac{\lambda}{k^{r/2}},1\right)
distribution truncated to the positive half of the real line.

Now, suppose that
s\in[n]
has been dropped by time
t
(i.e. that
M^{t}_{s}=P^{t}_{s}=0
). Conditional on this event, we claim that for any
(j_{2},\dots,j_{r})
containing
s
, the distribution of
Q_{i,j_{2},\dots,j_{r}}
is stochastically dominated by its marginal law. That is, we claim that for any
x
it holds that

(5.99)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(Q_{i,j_{2},\dots,j_{r}}\geq x|M^{t}_{s}=0\right)\leq\mathbb{P}\left(Q_{i,j_{2},\dots,j_{r}}\geq x\right)

To see this, fix any
x\in\mathbb{R}
, any
s\in[n]
, and any
(j_{2},\dots,j_{r})
containing
s
. For any random variable
X
defined on the same probability space as
Q
and
M^{t}
, define

(5.100)

f(X)=\mathbf{1}\left\{X\geq x\right\}

Further, define the function
g
as

(5.101)

g(X)=\mathbb{P}\left(M_{s}^{t}=0|X\geq x\right).

Then we note that
f(Q_{i,j_{2},\dots,j_{r}})
is increasing with
Q_{i,j_{2},\dots,j_{r}}
, while
g(Q_{i,j_{2},\dots,j_{r}})
is decreasing with
Q_{i,j_{2},\dots,j_{r}}
(because if
Q_{i,j_{2},\dots,j_{r}}
is larger, the probability that
s
will be dropped is lower). Therefore, by Chebyshev’s sum inequality (see e.g. page 43 of
[
HLP34
]
), we have

(5.102)

\mathbb{E}\left[f(Q_{s,j_{2},\dots,j_{r}})g(Q_{s,j_{2},\dots,j_{r}})\right]\leq\mathbb{E}\left[f(Q_{s,j_{2},\dots,j_{r}})\right]\mathbb{E}\left[g(Q_{s,j_{2},\dots,j_{r}})\right]

or equivalently

(5.103)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbf{1}\left\{Q_{i,j_{2},\dots,j_{r}}\geq x\right\}\mathbb{P}\left(M_{s}^{t}=0|Q_{i,j_{2},\dots,j_{r}}\geq x\right)\right]

(5.104)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbf{1}\left\{Q_{i,j_{2},\dots,j_{r}}\geq x\right\}\right]\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{P}\left(M_{s}^{t}=0|Q_{i,j_{2},\dots,j_{r}}\geq x\right)\right]

and therefore by the law of iterated expectations,

(5.105)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left[Q_{i,j_{2},\dots,j_{r}}\geq x\text{ and }M_{s}^{t}=0\right]\leq\mathbb{P}\left[Q_{i,j_{2},\dots,j_{r}}\geq x\right]\mathbb{P}\left[M_{s}^{t}=0\right]

which implies that

(5.106)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(Q_{i,j_{2},\dots,j_{r}}\geq x|M^{t}_{s}=0\right)\leq\mathbb{P}\left(Q_{i,j_{2},\dots,j_{r}}\geq x\right)

and we obtain (
5.99
) as desired.

Now let
\{\eta_{j,1}(i)\}_{j\geq 1}
be a sequence of i.i.d. random variables such that for each
j
,
{\mathbb{P}}(\eta_{j,1}(i)=0)=\frac{1}{2}
, and with probability
\frac{1}{2}
,
\eta_{j,1}(i)
follows a
{\mathcal{N}}(0,1)
distribution truncated to the positive half of the real line. By the above argument, it therefore holds that

(5.107)

\displaystyle\langle e_{i}\otimes({\mathcal{D}}_{t})^{\otimes r-1},Q\rangle

(5.108)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{(j_{2},\dots,j_{r})}\Gamma^{t+1}_{i,j_{2},\dots,j_{r}}{\mathcal{D}}_{t}(j_{2})\dots{\mathcal{D}}_{t}(j_{r})

(5.109)

\displaystyle\preceq_{\rm st}

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{\lVert{\mathcal{D}}_{t}\rVert_{0}^{r-1}}\eta_{j,1}(i)

where the last line holds because there are
\lVert{\mathcal{D}}_{t}\rVert_{0}^{r-1}
nonzero terms in
\sum_{(j_{2},\dots,j_{r}}\Gamma^{t+1}_{i,j_{2},\dots,j_{r}}{\mathcal{D}}_{t}(j_{2})\dots{\mathcal{D}}_{t}(j_{r})
. Finally, taking

(5.110)

b_{t,1}(i)=\sum_{j=1}^{\lVert{\mathcal{D}}_{t}\rVert_{0}^{r-1}}\eta_{j,1}(i)

completes the proof.

Next, we construct
b_{t,2}(i)
. Let
\{\eta_{j,2}(i)\}_{j\geq 1}
be a sequence of i.i.d. random variables such that for each
j
,
{\mathbb{P}}(\eta_{j,2}(i)=0)=\Phi(-\frac{\lambda}{k^{r/2}})
, and with probability
1-\Phi(-\frac{\lambda}{k^{r/2}})
,
\eta_{j,2}(i)
follows a
{\mathcal{N}}\left(\frac{\lambda}{k^{r/2}},1\right)
distribution truncated to the positive half of the real line. By the above argument and (
5.99
), it thus holds that

(5.111)

\displaystyle\langle e_{i}\otimes({\mathcal{D}}^{*}_{t})^{\otimes r-1},Q\rangle

(5.112)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{(j_{2},\dots,j_{r})}\Gamma^{t+1}_{i,j_{2},\dots,j_{r}}{\mathcal{D}}_{t}^{*}(j_{2})\dots{\mathcal{D}}^{*}_{t}(j_{r})

(5.113)

\displaystyle\preceq_{\rm st}

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{\lVert{\mathcal{D}}^{*}_{t}\rVert_{0}^{r-1}}\eta_{j,2}(i)

We then let

(5.114)

b_{t,2}(i)=\sum_{j=1}^{\lVert{\mathcal{D}}_{t}\rVert_{0}^{r-1}}\eta_{j,1}(i)

Finally, we construct
b_{t,3}(i)
. Let
\{\eta_{j,3}(i)\}_{j\geq 1}
be a sequence i.i.d. random variables with the same distribution as
\{\eta_{j,1}(i)\}_{j\geq 1}
, and let
\{\eta_{j,4}(i)\}_{j\geq 1}
be i.i.d. with the same distribution as
\{\eta_{j,2}(i)\}_{j\geq 1}
. Then, the inner product
\langle e_{i}\otimes H_{t},Q\rangle
, where
H_{t}
is defined in (
5.7
) will have
{\mathcal{H}}_{1,t}
terms where at least one coordinate involved is not in the support of
\theta
, and
{\mathcal{H}}_{2,t}
terms where all coordinates are in the support of
\theta
. Then, by the above argument and (
5.99
),

(5.115)

\displaystyle\langle e_{i}\otimes H_{t},Q\rangle

(5.116)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle j_{2},\dots,j_{r}\in[n]^{r-1}}}{{\text{indices span $\geq 2$ of $\text{supp}(P^{t}),\text{supp}({\mathcal{D}}_{t}),\text{supp}({\mathcal{D}}^{*}_{t})$, not all in $\text{supp}(\theta)$}}}}Q_{i,j_{2},\dots,j_{r}}

(5.117)

\displaystyle+\sum_{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle j_{2},\dots,j_{r}\in[n]^{r-1}}}{{\text{indices span $\geq 2$ of $\text{supp}(P^{t}),\text{supp}({\mathcal{D}}_{t}),\text{supp}({\mathcal{D}}^{*}_{t})$, all in $\text{supp}(\theta)$}}}}Q_{i,j_{2},\dots,j_{r}}

(5.118)

\displaystyle\preceq_{\rm st}\sum_{j=1}^{{\mathcal{H}}_{1,t}}\eta_{j,3}(i)+\sum_{j=1}^{{\mathcal{H}}_{2,t}}\eta_{j,4}(i)

Finally, we let

(5.119)

b_{t,3}(i)=\sum_{j=1}^{{\mathcal{H}}_{1,t}}\eta_{j,3}(i)+\sum_{j=1}^{{\mathcal{H}}_{2,t}}\eta_{j,4}(i).

∎

Proof of Lemma
5.7
.

The mean follows directly by construction of
b_{t,1}(i)
,
b_{t,2}(i)
, and
b_{t,3}(i)
. The variance bound follows for fixed
t
because, for every
j
and every
i
,
\eta_{j,1}(i)
,
\eta_{j,2}(i)
,
\eta_{j,3}(i)
, and
\eta_{j,4}(i)
all have variance at most
1
.
∎

6.
Proof of Theorem
2.5

In this section we prove Theorem
6.1
, which directly implies Theorem
2.5
, but makes the dependence on constants and polylogarithmic factors more explicit.

Theorem 6.1
.

Assume that
\Omega(\sqrt{n})=k=o(n)
, any
r\geq 2
and
\theta\sim\mathrm{Unif}(\Theta_{k})
. Suppose
\sigma_{0}\in\{0,1\}^{n}
satisfies
\langle\sigma_{0},\theta\rangle\geq\frac{1}{8}k
and
\lVert\sigma\rVert_{0}\leq\frac{3}{2}k
. There exist sufficiently large constants
C_{\lambda}(r),C_{\gamma}(r)>0
such that if all the following hold:

•

\gamma=C_{\gamma}\sqrt{\log n}

•

\lambda\geq C_{\lambda}\frac{n^{\frac{r-1}{2}}}{k^{\frac{r}{2}-1}}\sqrt{\log n}

•

M=\Omega\left(nk^{(r+1)/2}\right)

•

\sigma_{0}\in\{0,1\}^{n}
satisfies
\langle\sigma_{0},\theta\rangle\geq\frac{1}{8}k
and
\lVert\sigma\rVert_{0}\leq\frac{3}{2}k

then, with probability at least
\frac{2}{3}-o(1)
, Algorithm
4
with input
(Y,\sigma_{0},M)
outputs
\theta
.

6.1.
Proof of Theorem
6.1

In order to prove Theorem
6.1
, we will need the following key lemmas. Their proofs are given in Section
6.2
. Lemma
6.2
is the main technical contribution of this section.

Lemma 6.2
.

Assume the conditions in Theorem
6.1
hold. For any constant
C>0
define the set

(6.1)

\tilde{\Omega}(C)=\left\{\sigma\in\{0,1\}^{n}\setminus\{\textbf{0}\}:\frac{\langle\sigma,\theta\rangle}{\lVert\sigma\rVert\lVert\theta\rVert}\geq C\left(\frac{\sqrt{k\log n}}{\lambda}\right)^{1/(r-1)}\text{, }\lVert\sigma\rVert_{0}\geq 2\right\}

Then there exists a large enough choice of
C
such that the following statements hold simultaneously with probability at least
1-3n^{-1}
:

(A)

For any
\sigma\in\tilde{\Omega}(C)\setminus\{\theta\}
there exists a
\sigma^{\prime}\in\tilde{\Omega}(C)
satisfying
d_{H}(\sigma^{\prime},\sigma)=1
and

(6.2)

\displaystyle H_{(r+1)/2,\gamma}(\sigma^{\prime})-H_{(r+1)/2,\gamma}(\sigma)\geq\|\sigma\|^{(r-1)/2}_{0}\sqrt{\log(n)}

(B)

Let
\hat{\Omega}(C)=\tilde{\Omega}(C)\cap\{\sigma\in\{0,1\}^{n}:\|\sigma\|_{0}\leq 3k/2,\langle\sigma,\theta\rangle\geq k/10\}
. Then, for each
\sigma\in\hat{\Omega}(C)
there are at least
\lfloor 2/3(k-\langle\sigma,\theta\rangle)\rfloor
distinct
\sigma^{\prime}\in\hat{\Omega}(C)
such that
d_{H}(\sigma^{\prime},\sigma)=1,d_{H}(\sigma^{\prime},\theta)=d_{H}(\sigma^{\prime},\theta)-1
and

(6.3)

H_{(r+1)/2,\gamma}(\sigma^{\prime})-H_{(r+1)/2,\gamma}(\sigma)\geq\frac{r}{2\cdot 10^{r-1}}\lambda k^{\frac{r}{2}-1}

We will also need the following two lemmas concerning the landscape of
H_{(r+1)/2,\gamma}
:

Lemma 6.3
.

The range
R_{H_{(r+1)/2,\gamma}}
of
H_{(r+1)/2,\gamma}
on the set
\{\sigma\in\{0,1\}^{n}:\lVert\sigma\rVert_{0}\leq\frac{3}{2}k\}
satisfies

(6.4)

R_{H_{(r+1)/2,\gamma}}=O\left(k^{\frac{r}{2}}\left(\lambda+k^{\frac{r}{2}}\sqrt{\log n}\right)\right)

with probability at least
1-n^{-1/2}
.

Lemma 6.4
.

For any
\sigma\in\{0,1\}^{n}
, we have the following identities:

•

For any
i
such that
\sigma_{i}=0
,

(6.5)

\displaystyle H_{(r+1)/2,\gamma}(\sigma+e_{i})-H_{(r+1)/2,\gamma}(\sigma)

(6.6)

\displaystyle=\frac{\lambda\theta_{i}}{k^{r/2}}\sum_{j=1}^{r}\binom{r}{j}\langle\sigma,\theta\rangle^{r-j}+\sum_{j=1}^{r}\binom{r}{j}\langle e_{i}^{\otimes j}\otimes\sigma^{\otimes r-j},W\rangle

(6.7)

\displaystyle-\gamma\left(\lVert\sigma\rVert_{0}+1\right)^{\frac{r+1}{2}}+\gamma\lVert\sigma\rVert_{0}^{\frac{r+1}{2}}

•

For any
i
such that
\sigma_{i}=1
,

(6.8)

\displaystyle H_{(r+1)/2,\gamma}(\sigma-e_{i})-H_{(r+1)/2,\gamma}(\sigma)

(6.9)

\displaystyle=\frac{\lambda\theta_{i}}{k^{r/2}}\sum_{j=1}^{r}\binom{r}{j}(-1)^{j}\langle\sigma,\theta\rangle^{r-j}+\sum_{j=1}^{r}\binom{r}{j}(-1)^{j}\langle e_{i}^{\otimes j}\otimes\sigma^{\otimes r-j},W\rangle

(6.10)

\displaystyle-\gamma\left(\lVert\sigma\rVert_{0}-1\right)^{\frac{r+1}{2}}+\gamma\lVert\sigma\rVert_{0}^{\frac{r+1}{2}}

Proof of Theorem
6.1
.

We will first prove the statement in Theorem
6.1
in the case where

(6.11)

\lambda=C_{\lambda}\frac{n^{\frac{r-1}{2}}}{k^{\frac{r}{2}-1}}\sqrt{\log n}

for a large enough choice of constant
C_{\lambda}>0
. Later, we will use a coupling argument to strengthen the result to all
\lambda
larger than this.

Let
S_{t}
denote the iterates of Algorithm
4
with input
(Y,\sigma_{0},M)
. We first define another process
\tilde{S}_{t}
in the following way: for
1\leq t\leq M
, let
\tilde{S}_{t}
be the iterates of Algorithm
12
with input
(Y,\sigma_{0},M)
. Note that Algorithm
12
is identical to Algorithm
4
, except that only transitions that maintain
\langle\tilde{S}_{t},\theta\rangle\geq\frac{k}{10}
are considered. This is not an implementable algorithm, because we do not have direct access to
\langle\tilde{S}_{t},\theta\rangle
. However, we will show that with probability at least
1-e^{-0.01k}
,
S_{t}=\tilde{S}_{t}
for the first
\Omega(e^{k})
many steps. We will also show that
\tilde{S}_{M}=\theta
with probability at least
\frac{2}{3}-o(1)
. Morever, since
\theta
is a local maximum of
H_{(r+1)/2,\gamma}
with probability
1-o(1)
, once
S_{t}
hits it, it is absorbed, completing the proof.

Algorithm 12

Norm-constrained Randomized Greedy Local Search on
H_{(r+1)/2,\gamma}
with high overlap maintained

0:

Y\in\mathbb{R}^{n^{\otimes r}}
,
\sigma_{0}\in\{\sigma\in\{0,1\}^{n}:\lVert\sigma\rVert_{0}\leq\frac{3}{2}k\}
,
M\in{\mathbb{N}}
.

1:

Let
t\leftarrow 1
and
S_{1}\leftarrow\sigma_{0}

2:

while

t<M

do

3:

Let
\mathcal{N}(\sigma)=\{\sigma^{\prime}\mid d_{H}(S_{t},\sigma^{\prime})=1\}\cap\{\sigma:\langle\sigma,\theta\rangle\geq\frac{k}{10},\lVert\sigma\rVert_{0}\leq\frac{3}{2}k\}

4:

Choose a random element
\sigma^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}(\sigma)
uniformly

5:

if

H_{(r+1)/2,\gamma}(\sigma^{\prime})-H_{(r+1)/2,\gamma}(S_{t})>0

then

6:

S_{t+1}\leftarrow\sigma^{\prime}

7:

t\leftarrow t+1

8:

end

if

9:

end

while

10:

return

S_{M}

First, to show that
\tilde{S}_{M}=\theta
, we will apply Theorem 2.3 of
[
CSZ24
]
. This theorem says that for a randomized greedy algorithm on a graph
G
with Hamiltonian
H
defined on
G
, if the maximum degree of
G
is at most
\Delta
, and if there exists a spanning tree of
G
rooted at a vertex
v^{*}
such that (i)
v^{*}
is the global maximizer of
H
, and (ii) moving up one step in the spanning tree increases
H
by at least
\delta
, then the randomized greedy algorithm from any initialization outputs
v^{*}
after
O\left(\frac{\Delta R_{H}}{\delta}\right)
iterations, where
R_{H}
is the range of
H
.

Consider the natural graph on
\{\sigma\in\{0,1\}^{n}:\langle\sigma,\theta\rangle\geq\frac{k}{10},\lVert\sigma\rVert_{0}\leq\frac{3}{2}k\}
where two elements
\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}
are connected if they have
d_{H}(\sigma,\sigma^{\prime})=1
. We will construct a spanning tree on this graph, rooted at
\theta
. Let
C>0
be a constant. We claim that whenever
\langle\sigma,\theta\rangle\geq\frac{1}{10}k
and
\lVert\sigma\rVert_{0}\leq\frac{3}{2}k
, it holds that
\sigma\in\tilde{\Omega}(C)
, where
\tilde{\Omega}(C)
is defined in (
6.1
). This holds because

(6.12)

\displaystyle\frac{\langle\sigma,\theta\rangle}{\lVert\sigma\rVert_{2}\lVert\theta\rVert_{2}}\geq\frac{\frac{k}{10}}{\sqrt{\frac{3k}{2}}\sqrt{k}}=\frac{\sqrt{2}}{10\sqrt{3}}

while since
\lambda\geq C_{\lambda}\frac{n^{\frac{r-1}{2}}}{k^{\frac{r}{2}-1}}\sqrt{\log n}
, we have

(6.13)

\displaystyle C\left(\frac{\sqrt{k\log n}}{\lambda}\right)^{1/(r-1)}\leq C\left(\frac{\sqrt{k\log n}}{C_{\lambda}\frac{n^{(r-1)/2}}{k^{r/2-1}}\sqrt{\log n}}\right)^{1/(r-1)}

\displaystyle=C\left(\frac{k^{(r-1)/2}}{C_{\lambda}n^{(r-1)/2}}\right)^{1/(r-1)}

(6.14)

\displaystyle=\frac{C}{C_{\lambda}^{1/(r-1)}}\sqrt{\frac{k}{n}}\leq\frac{\sqrt{2}}{10\sqrt{3}}

for a large enough choice of
C_{\lambda}
, since
k\leq n
. Thus,
\sigma\in\tilde{\Omega}(C)
. Then by Lemma
6.2
(A), if
\sigma\neq\theta
, then
\sigma
has a neighbor
\sigma^{\prime}
with
H_{(r+1)/2,\gamma}(\sigma^{\prime})-H_{(r+1)/2,\gamma}(\sigma)\geq\|\sigma\|^{(r-1)/2}_{0}\sqrt{\log(n)}=\Theta\left(k^{(r-1)/2}\sqrt{\log n}\right)
. Choose
\sigma^{\prime}
to be the parent of
\sigma
in the tree, and we obtain a spanning tree rooted at
\theta
with
\delta=\Theta\left(k^{(r-1)/2}\sqrt{\log n}\right)
.

Next, we note that the maximum degree of the graph
\Delta=n
, as each
\sigma
has exactly
n
neighbors.
Therefore, the assumptions of Theorem 2.3 of
[
CSZ24
]
are satisfied, with Hamiltonian
H_{(r+1)/2,\gamma}
,
\Delta=n
,
\delta=k^{\frac{r-1}{2}}\sqrt{\log n}
and
\beta=\infty
. By Lemma
6.3
, the range of
H_{(r+1)/2,\gamma}
,
R_{H_{(r+1)/2,\gamma}}
, satisfies
R_{H_{(r+1)/2,\gamma}}=O\left(k^{\frac{r}{2}}\left(\lambda+k^{\frac{r}{2}}\sqrt{\log n}\right)\right)
with high probability. Recall that for this part of the proof we assume that

(6.15)

\lambda=\Theta\left(\frac{\sqrt{\log n}n^{\frac{r-1}{2}}}{k^{\frac{r}{2}-1}}\right)

Therefore, Algorithm
12
with

(6.16)

\displaystyle M

\displaystyle=\Omega\left(\frac{nk^{\frac{r}{2}}\left(\lambda+k^{\frac{r}{2}}\sqrt{\log n}\right)}{k^{\frac{r-1}{2}}\sqrt{\log n}}\right)

(6.17)

\displaystyle=\Omega\left(\frac{nk^{1/2}\lambda}{\sqrt{\log n}}+nk^{\frac{r+1}{2}}\right)

(6.18)

\displaystyle=\Omega\left(\frac{n^{\frac{r+1}{2}}}{k^{\frac{r-3}{2}}}+nk^{\frac{r+1}{2}}\right)

(6.19)

\displaystyle=\Omega\left(nk^{\frac{r+1}{2}}\right)

outputs
\theta
with probability at least
2/3-o(1)
.

Next, we show that
S_{t}
and
\tilde{S}_{t}
are coupled up until this time. To do this, it suffices to show that
S_{t}
has
\langle S_{t},\theta\rangle\geq k/10
for all
t\leq M
with probability
1-o(1)
. Suppose that
\langle S_{t},\theta\rangle\in[\frac{k}{10},\frac{k}{8}]
. We first have the trivial upper bound

(6.20)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}\left(\langle S_{t+1},\theta\rangle=\langle S_{t},\theta\rangle-1|\langle S_{t},\theta\rangle\in[k/10,k/8]\right)\leq\frac{\langle S_{t},\theta\rangle}{n}\leq\frac{k}{8n}

because each of the
\langle S_{t},\theta\rangle
correct coordinates has probability
\frac{1}{n}
of being proposed to drop at each time
t
. Meanwhile, by Lemma
6.2
(
B
), we have the lower bound

(6.21)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}\left(\langle S_{t+1},\theta\rangle=\langle S_{t},\theta\rangle+1|\langle S_{t},\theta\rangle\in[k/10,k/8]\right)\geq\frac{2}{3}\frac{k-\langle S_{t},\theta\rangle}{n}\geq\frac{2}{3}\frac{7k}{8n}=\frac{7k}{12n}

Define a lazy random walk on the integers as follows: Let
a
be a positive integer, and let
X_{0}=\lfloor\frac{k}{8}\rfloor
. Then for all
t\geq 0
, let

(6.22)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}(X_{t+1}=X_{t}-1)=\frac{1}{8}

(6.23)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}(X_{t+1}=X_{t}+1)=\begin{cases}\frac{14}{24}\text{ if $X_{t}<a$}\\
0\text{ if $X_{t}=a$}\end{cases}

(6.24)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}(X_{t+1}=X_{t})=\begin{cases}\frac{7}{24}\text{ if $X_{t}<a$}\\
\frac{7}{8}\text{ if $X_{t}=a$}\end{cases}

i.e. a simple asymmetric random walk, but reflected downwards at
a
. Then with the choice of
a=\lfloor\frac{k}{8}\rfloor
, the random walk
\{X_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
is stochastically dominated by the process
\langle S_{t},\theta\rangle
. We will show that with probability at least
1-e^{-0.001k}
, the first time that
X_{t}=\lceil\frac{k}{10}\rceil
is at least
\Omega(e^{k})
, and thus with the same probability
\langle S_{t},\theta\rangle\geq\frac{k}{10}
for at least the first
\Omega(e^{k})
steps. Define
f:(-\infty,a]\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
as

(6.25)

\displaystyle f(x)=\begin{cases}\left(\frac{3}{14}\right)^{x}\text{ if $x<a$}\\
\left(\frac{3}{14}\right)^{a-1}\text{ if $x=a$}\end{cases}

Then we claim that
M_{t}:=f(X_{t})
is a martingale. To see this, if
X_{t}<a
, we have

(6.26)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\left(M_{t+1}|X_{t}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{8}\left(\frac{3}{14}\right)^{X_{t}-1}+\frac{14}{24}\left(\frac{3}{14}\right)^{X_{t}+1}+\frac{7}{24}\left(\frac{3}{14}\right)^{X_{t}}

(6.27)

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{3}{14}\right)^{X_{t}}=M_{t}

While if
X_{t}=a
:

(6.28)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\left(M_{t+1}|X_{t}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{8}\left(\frac{3}{14}\right)^{a-1}+\frac{7}{8}\left(\frac{3}{14}\right)^{a-1}

(6.29)

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{3}{14}\right)^{a-1}=M_{t}

so
M_{t}
is a martingale. Let
T
be any positive integer, let
\tau_{1}=\inf\left\{t>0:X_{t}=\lceil\frac{k}{10}\rceil\right\}
, and let
\tau=\min\left\{\tau_{1},T\right\}
. Then
\tau
is a bounded stopping time, and
M_{t}
is a non-negative martingale, so by the Optional Stopping Theorem, it holds that

(6.30)

{\mathbb{E}}(M_{\tau})={\mathbb{E}}(M_{0})=\left(\frac{3}{14}\right)^{\lfloor\frac{k}{8}\rfloor-1}

Decompose
{\mathbb{E}}(M_{\tau})
as

(6.31)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}(M_{\tau})={\mathbb{E}}(M_{\tau}|\tau_{1}\leq T){\mathbb{P}}(\tau_{1}\leq T)+{\mathbb{E}}(M_{\tau}|\tau_{1}>T){\mathbb{P}}(\tau_{1}>T)=\left(\frac{3}{14}\right)^{\lfloor\frac{k}{8}\rfloor-1}

{\mathbb{E}}(M_{\tau}|\tau_{1}>T){\mathbb{P}}(\tau_{1}>T)\geq 0
because
M_{t}
is non-negative, which gives us

(6.32)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}(M_{\tau}|\tau_{1}\leq T){\mathbb{P}}(\tau_{1}\leq T)\leq\left(\frac{3}{14}\right)^{\lfloor\frac{k}{8}\rfloor-1}

Note that on the event that
\{\tau_{1}\leq T\}
, it holds that
M_{\tau}=M_{\tau_{1}}=\left(\frac{3}{14}\right)^{\lceil\frac{k}{10}\rceil}
, and therefore

(6.33)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}(\tau_{1}\leq T)\leq\left(\frac{3}{14}\right)^{\lfloor\frac{k}{8}\rfloor-1-\lceil\frac{k}{10}\rceil}\leq e^{-0.001k}

for all
T>0
, where the last inequality holds for large enough
k
. In particular, we choose
T=\Omega(e^{k})
to get that with probability at least
1-e^{-0.001k}
,
X_{t}
does not reach
\lceil\frac{k}{10}\rceil
within the first
\Omega(e^{k})
steps, and therefore since
\langle S_{t},\theta\rangle
has an initial value of at least
\frac{k}{8}
, with the same probability it does not decrease to
\frac{k}{10}
within the first
\Omega(e^{k})
iterations.

It remains to consider the case where
\lambda\geq C_{\lambda}\frac{n^{\frac{r-1}{2}}}{k^{\frac{r}{2}-1}}\sqrt{\log n}
, rather than
\lambda=C_{\lambda}\frac{n^{\frac{r-1}{2}}}{k^{\frac{r}{2}-1}}\sqrt{\log n}
. Consider two instances of the problem with the following coupling:

(6.34)

\displaystyle Y_{1}=\frac{\lambda_{1}}{k^{r/2}}\theta^{\otimes r}+W_{1}

and

(6.35)

\displaystyle Y_{2}=\frac{\lambda_{2}}{k^{r/2}}\theta^{\otimes r}+W_{2}

where we couple
W_{1}=W_{2}
with probability 1, so that the only difference is in the signal-to-noise parameters
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}
. Assume that
\lambda_{2}\geq\lambda_{1}=C_{\lambda}\frac{n^{\frac{r-1}{2}}}{k^{\frac{r}{2}-1}}\sqrt{\log n}
, where
C_{\lambda}
is the sufficiently large constant chosen above. Suppose we run Algorithm
12
twice, once with input
(Y_{1},\sigma_{0},M)
and once with
(Y_{2},\sigma_{0},M)
, but condition that the same bit flips are proposed at each iteration. As proven above, the instance with
Y_{1}
outputs
\theta
with probability at least
2/3
. We claim that the same is true for the instance with
Y_{2}
. To see this, first note that by Lemma
6.4
, if the proposed bit flip at a particular iteration is for a coordinate outside the support of
\theta
, then the change in the value of
H_{(r+1)/2,\gamma}
is independent of
\lambda
, so the instances couple perfectly (due to the noise coupling). Secondly, when adding a coordinate in the support of
\theta
, if
H_{(r+1)/2,\gamma}(\sigma+e_{i})-H_{(r+1)/2,\gamma}(\sigma)>0
with
\lambda=\lambda_{1}
, then it trivially remains positive when
\lambda=\lambda_{2}\geq\lambda_{1}
by (
6.5
), so the instance with
\lambda_{2}
adds every coordinate in
\text{supp}(\theta)
that the instance with
\lambda_{1}
adds. Finally, when the proposed bit flip is to remove a coordinate in
\text{supp}(\theta)
, even if the instance with
Y_{1}
removes it, the instance with
Y_{2}
may not if
\lambda_{2}
is large enough. It therefore holds that the iterates of the instance with
Y_{2}
,
S_{t}^{2}
, maintain a higher overlap with
\theta
at every step than the iterates of the instance with
Y_{1}
,
S_{t}^{1}
(i.e.
\langle S_{t}^{2},\theta\rangle\geq\langle S_{t}^{1},\theta\rangle
for each
t
). Therefore,
S_{t}^{2}=\theta
after at most
M
iterations with probability at least
2/3-o(1)
. This completes the proof of Theorem
6.1
.
∎

6.2.
Proofs of Key Lemmas

Here we prove Lemmas
6.2
,
6.3
, and
6.4
.

In order to prove Lemma
6.2
, we will need the following auxiliary technical lemma, whose proof is given in Section
6.3
.

Lemma 6.5
.

Let
m,T\leq n
be integers with
m\geq 2
. Let
{\mathcal{S}}_{m}\subseteq\{\sigma\in\Omega:\|\sigma\|_{0}=m\}
. Let
C_{\mathrm{HW}}
be the universal constant in Lemma
6.7
and let
1\leq j\leq r
. Then, for every
\sigma\in{\mathcal{S}}_{m}
and every
i\in[T]
, there exists a constant
C=C(r)>0
such that for all
a>0
satisfying

(6.36)

\displaystyle\min\left\{\frac{a^{2}}{T},a\right\}\geq C_{\mathrm{HW}}\left(1+\frac{T}{m}\right)\log(n^{d}|{\mathcal{S}}_{m}|),

and any
d\in{\mathbb{N}}
the following statement holds with
probability at least
1-n^{-d}
:

For every
\sigma\in{\mathcal{S}}_{m}
and
q\in[T]
, there are at least
T-q
indices
i\in[T]

satisfying

(6.37)

\left|\langle e_{i}^{\otimes j}\otimes\sigma^{\otimes(r-j)},W\rangle\right|\leq C\sqrt{\frac{m^{(r-j)}(a+T)}{q}}

Proof of Lemma
6.2
.

Throughout this proof, we will use the notation that for all
\sigma\in\{0,1\}^{n},

\ell_{\sigma}=\langle\sigma,\theta\rangle
and
m_{\sigma}=\lVert\sigma\rVert_{0}
. First we prove that (
A
) holds for all
\sigma\in\tilde{\Omega}(C)
satisfying
k-\ell_{\sigma}\leq m_{\sigma}/2
, and then we show that (
A
) holds for all
\sigma\in\tilde{\Omega}(C)
with
k-\ell_{\sigma}>m_{\sigma}/2
.

Finally, we show that (
B
) is satisfied.

We start with proving the first claim.

Case 1: proving that (
A
) holds for
\sigma\in\tilde{\Omega}_{1}(C):=\left\{\sigma\in\tilde{\Omega}(C):k-\ell_{\sigma}\leq m_{\sigma}/2\right\}
.

For any
\sigma\in\tilde{\Omega}_{1}(C)
, let

(6.38)

{\mathcal{S}}_{1}(\sigma)=\left\{\sigma^{\prime}\in\tilde{\Omega}_{1}(C):\ell_{\sigma^{\prime}}=\ell_{\sigma},m_{\sigma^{\prime}}=m_{\sigma}\right\}

Then
|{\mathcal{S}}_{1}(\sigma)|\leq\binom{n}{m_{\sigma}-\ell_{\sigma}}\binom{k}{k-\ell_{\sigma}}\leq n^{m_{\sigma}+k-2\ell_{\sigma}}
. We consider the set of “neighbors” of
\sigma
, i.e.,
\sigma^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}^{n}
with
d_{H}(\sigma,\sigma^{\prime})=1
, that either remove a coordinate outside the support of
\theta
(i.e. transitions from
\sigma
to
\sigma^{\prime}=\sigma-e_{i}
, where
i
satisfies
\sigma_{i}=1
and
\theta_{i}=0
) or which add a coordinate in the support of
\theta
(i.e.
\sigma
to
\sigma^{\prime}=\sigma+e_{i}
, where
\sigma_{i}=0
and
\theta_{i}=1
). The number of possible such transitions is given by
T_{\sigma}=m_{\sigma}+k-2\ell_{\sigma}
. Let
a=16C_{\mathrm{HW}}(m_{\sigma}+k-2\ell_{\sigma})\log n
. Observe that
T_{\sigma}\leq m_{\sigma}
and
m_{\sigma}+k-2\ell_{\sigma}\geq 1
, and thus

(6.39)

\left(1+\frac{T_{\sigma}}{m_{\sigma}}\right)\log(n^{3}|{\mathcal{S}}_{1}(\sigma)|)\leq 4(3+m_{\sigma}+k-2\ell_{\sigma})\log n\leq 16(m_{\sigma}+k-2\ell_{\sigma})\log n

It is clear that
a^{2}/T_{\sigma}\geq a
. Then with
q=T_{\sigma}/3
,
d=3
,
T=T_{\sigma}
, and
m=m_{\sigma}
, the assumptions of Lemma
6.5
are therefore satisfied. Hence, with probability at least
1-n^{-3}
, it holds that for every
\sigma^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{S}}_{1}(\sigma)
and for every
1\leq j\leq r
,, at least two thirds of the
(m_{\sigma}+k-2\ell_{\sigma})
transitions that either remove a coordinate
i\not\in\text{supp}(\theta)
or add a coordinate
i\in\text{supp}(\theta)
satisfy

(6.40)

\displaystyle\left|\langle e_{i}^{\otimes j}\otimes(\sigma^{\prime})^{\otimes r-j},W\rangle\right|

(6.41)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C_{1}\left(\frac{m_{\sigma}^{r-j}(a+T_{\sigma})}{q_{\sigma}}\right)^{1/2}

(6.42)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle C_{1}\left(\frac{m_{\sigma}^{r-j}(16C_{\mathrm{HW}}(m_{\sigma}+k-2\ell_{\sigma})\log n+m_{\sigma}+k-2\ell_{\sigma})}{\frac{1}{3}(m_{\sigma}+k-2\ell_{\sigma})}\right)^{1/2}

(6.43)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle C_{1}\left(48C_{\mathrm{HW}}m_{\sigma}^{r-j}\log n+3m_{\sigma}^{r-j}\right)^{1/2}

(6.44)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C_{2}m_{\sigma}^{(r-j)/2}\sqrt{\log n}

where
C_{1}=C_{1}(r)>0
,
C_{2}=C_{2}(r)>0
are sufficiently large constants depending only on
r
. Thus for a large enough choice of constant
C_{3}(r)>0
, at least two thirds of the transitions satisfy

(6.45)

\displaystyle\left|\sum_{j=1}^{r}\binom{r}{j}(-1)^{j}\langle e_{i}^{\otimes j}\otimes(\sigma^{\prime})^{\otimes r-j},W\rangle\right|\leq C_{3}m_{\sigma}^{(r-1)/2}\sqrt{\log n}

In particular, by a union bound over the
n
possible values of
m_{\sigma}
and the
k
possible values of
\ell_{\sigma}
, it holds with probability at least
1-n^{-1}
that for every
\sigma\in\tilde{\Omega}_{1}(C)
that at least two thirds of the transitions that add a coordinate in
\text{supp}(\theta)
or remove a coordinate outside
\text{supp}(\theta)
satisfy (
6.45
).

Now consider any such transition from
\sigma
to a
\sigma^{\prime}
satisfying (
6.45
). In the case where
\sigma^{\prime}=\sigma-e_{i}
for a coordinate
i\not\in\text{supp}(\theta)
of
\sigma
where
\sigma_{i}=1
, we therefore see by Lemma
6.4
that

(6.46)

\displaystyle H_{(r+1)/2,\gamma}(\sigma-e_{i})-H_{(r+1)/2,\gamma}(\sigma)

(6.47)

\displaystyle=\frac{\lambda\theta_{i}}{k^{r/2}}\sum_{j=1}^{r}\binom{r}{j}(-1)^{j}\langle\sigma,\theta\rangle^{r-j}+\sum_{j=1}^{r}\binom{r}{j}(-1)^{j}\langle e_{i}^{\otimes j}\otimes\sigma^{\otimes r-j},W\rangle

(6.48)

\displaystyle-\gamma\left(\lVert\sigma\rVert_{0}-1\right)^{\frac{r+1}{2}}+\gamma\lVert\sigma\rVert_{0}^{\frac{r+1}{2}}

(6.49)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{r}\binom{r}{j}(-1)^{j}\langle e_{i}^{\otimes j}\otimes\sigma^{\otimes r-j},W\rangle-\gamma\left(\lVert\sigma\rVert_{0}-1\right)^{\frac{r+1}{2}}+\gamma\lVert\sigma\rVert_{0}^{\frac{r+1}{2}}

(6.50)

\displaystyle\geq-C_{3}m_{\sigma}^{(r-1)/2}\sqrt{\log n}-C_{\gamma}\sqrt{\log n}\left(m_{\sigma}-1\right)^{\frac{r+1}{2}}+C_{\gamma}\sqrt{\log n}m_{\sigma}^{\frac{r+1}{2}}

(6.51)

\displaystyle\geq-C_{3}m_{\sigma}^{(r-1)/2}\sqrt{\log n}+C_{\gamma}\sqrt{\log n}\left(\frac{r+1}{2}\right)(m_{\sigma}-1)^{(r-1)/2}

(6.52)

\displaystyle\geq m_{\sigma}^{(r-1)/2}\sqrt{\log n}

for a large enough choice of
C_{\gamma}
, where the next to last inequality holds by the Mean Value Theorem.

Next, consider the case of a transition from
\sigma
to
\sigma^{\prime}
where
\sigma^{\prime}=\sigma+e_{i}
for some coordinate
i\in\text{supp}\theta)
, such that (
6.45
) is satisfied. Then, by Lemma
6.4
,

(6.53)

\displaystyle H_{(r+1)/2,\gamma}(\sigma+e_{i})-H_{(r+1)/2,\gamma}(\sigma)

(6.54)

\displaystyle=\frac{\lambda\theta_{i}}{k^{r/2}}\sum_{j=1}^{r}\binom{r}{j}\langle\sigma,\theta\rangle^{r-j}+\sum_{j=1}^{r}\binom{r}{j}\langle e_{i}^{\otimes j}\otimes\sigma^{\otimes r-j},W\rangle-\gamma\left(\lVert\sigma\rVert_{0}+1\right)^{\frac{r+1}{2}}+\gamma\lVert\sigma\rVert_{0}^{\frac{r+1}{2}}

(6.55)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\lambda}{k^{r/2}}\sum_{j=1}^{r}\binom{r}{j}\langle\sigma,\theta\rangle^{r-j}-C_{3}m_{\sigma}^{(r-1)/2}\sqrt{\log n}-C_{\gamma}\sqrt{\log n}\left(m_{\sigma}+1\right)^{\frac{r+1}{2}}+C_{\gamma}\sqrt{\log n}m_{\sigma}^{\frac{r+1}{2}}

(6.56)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\lambda}{k^{r/2}}r\ell_{\sigma}^{r-1}-C_{3}m_{\sigma}^{(r-1)/2}\sqrt{\log n}-C_{\gamma}\sqrt{\log n}\left(m_{\sigma}+1\right)^{\frac{r+1}{2}}+C_{\gamma}\sqrt{\log n}m_{\sigma}^{\frac{r+1}{2}}

Since
\sigma\in\tilde{\Omega}(C)
, we have
\ell_{\sigma}^{r-1}\geq C^{r-1}\left(\frac{\sqrt{k\log n}}{\lambda}\right)k^{(r-1)/2}m_{\sigma}^{(r-1)/2}
, and therefore this is at least

(6.58)

\displaystyle\geq C^{r-1}r\sqrt{\log n}m_{\sigma}^{(r-1)/2}-C_{3}m_{\sigma}^{(r-1)/2}\sqrt{\log n}-C_{\gamma}\sqrt{\log n}\left(m_{\sigma}+1\right)^{\frac{r+1}{2}}+C_{\gamma}\sqrt{\log n}m_{\sigma}^{\frac{r+1}{2}}

(6.59)

\displaystyle\geq m_{\sigma}^{(r-1)/2}\sqrt{\log n}

for a large enough choice of
C
.
Thus, (
A
) is satisfied for every
\sigma\in\tilde{\Omega}_{1}(C)
with probability at least
1-n^{-1}
.

Case 2: proving that (
A
) holds for
\sigma\in\tilde{\Omega}_{2}(C):=\left\{\sigma\in\tilde{\Omega}(C):k-\ell_{\sigma}>m_{\sigma}/2\right\}
.

Again, for any
\sigma\in\tilde{\Omega}_{2}(C)
, and let

(6.60)

{\mathcal{S}}_{2}(\sigma)=\left\{\sigma^{\prime}\in\tilde{\Omega}_{2}(C):\ell_{\sigma^{\prime}}=\ell_{\sigma},m_{\sigma^{\prime}}=m_{\sigma}\right\}

Then
|{\mathcal{S}}_{2}(\sigma)|\leq\binom{n}{m_{\sigma}}\leq n^{m_{\sigma}}
. Consider the set of “neighbors” of
\sigma
, i.e.,
\sigma^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}^{n}
with
d_{H}(\sigma,\sigma^{\prime})=1
, that add a coordinate in
\text{supp}(\theta)
(i.e.
\sigma
to
\sigma^{\prime}=\sigma+e_{i}
, where
\sigma_{i}=0
and
\theta_{i}=1
). The number of possible such transitions is given by
T_{\sigma}=k-\ell_{\sigma}
. Let
a=5C_{\mathrm{HW}}(k-\ell_{\sigma})\log n
. Observe that

(6.61)

\left(1+\frac{T_{\sigma}}{m_{\sigma}}\right)\log\left(n^{3}|{\mathcal{S}}_{2}(\sigma)|\right)\leq\left(1+\frac{k-\ell_{\sigma}}{m_{\sigma}}\right)(3+m_{\sigma})\log n\leq 5(k-\ell_{\sigma})\log n

Note also that
a^{2}/T_{\sigma}\geq a
. Then with
q=T_{\sigma}/3
,
d=3
,
T=T_{\sigma}
, and
m=m_{\sigma}
, the assumptions of Lemma
6.5
are therefore satisfied. It therefore holds with probability at least
1-n^{-3}
that for every
\sigma^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{S}}_{2}(\sigma)
, and for every
1\leq j\leq r
, at least two thirds of the
k-\ell_{\sigma}
transitions that add a coordinate
i
in
\text{supp}(\theta)
satisfy

(6.62)

\displaystyle\left|\langle e_{i}^{\otimes j}\otimes(\sigma^{\prime})^{\otimes r-j},W\rangle\right|

(6.63)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C_{1}\left(\frac{m_{\sigma}^{r-j}(a+T_{\sigma})}{q_{\sigma}}\right)^{1/2}

(6.64)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle C_{1}\left(\frac{m_{\sigma}^{r-j}(5C_{\mathrm{HW}}(k-\ell_{\sigma})\log n+k-\ell_{\sigma})}{\frac{1}{3}(k-\ell_{\sigma})}\right)^{1/2}

(6.65)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle C_{1}\left(15C_{\mathrm{HW}}m_{\sigma}^{r-j}\log n+3m_{\sigma}^{r-j}\right)^{1/2}

(6.66)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C_{2}m_{\sigma}^{(r-j)/2}\sqrt{\log n}

where again
C_{1}=C_{1}(r)>0
,
C_{2}=C_{2}(r)>0
are sufficiently large constants depending only on
r
. Thus for a large enough choice of constant
C_{3}>0
, at least two thirds of the transitions satisfy

(6.67)

\displaystyle\left|\sum_{j=1}^{r}\binom{r}{j}(-1)^{j}\langle e_{i}^{\otimes j}\otimes(\sigma^{\prime})^{\otimes r-j},W\rangle\right|\leq C_{3}m_{\sigma}^{(r-1)/2}\sqrt{\log n}

By a union bound again, it holds with probability at least
1-n^{-1}
that for every
\sigma\in\tilde{\Omega}_{2}(C)
that at least two thirds of the transitions that add a coordinate in
\text{supp}(\theta)
satisfy (
6.67
). For such a
\sigma
, and such a transition
\sigma^{\prime}=\sigma+e_{i}
, we have by Lemma
6.4
that

(6.68)

\displaystyle H_{(r+1)/2,\gamma}(\sigma+e_{i})-H_{(r+1)/2,\gamma}(\sigma)

(6.69)

\displaystyle=\frac{\lambda\theta_{i}}{k^{r/2}}\sum_{j=1}^{r}\binom{r}{j}\langle\sigma,\theta\rangle^{r-j}+\sum_{j=1}^{r}\binom{r}{j}\langle e_{i}^{\otimes j}\otimes\sigma^{\otimes r-j},W\rangle-\gamma\left(\lVert\sigma\rVert_{0}+1\right)^{\frac{r+1}{2}}+\gamma\lVert\sigma\rVert_{0}^{\frac{r+1}{2}}

(6.70)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\lambda}{k^{r/2}}\sum_{j=1}^{r}\binom{r}{j}\langle\sigma,\theta\rangle^{r-j}-C_{3}m_{\sigma}^{(r-1)/2}\sqrt{\log n}-C_{\gamma}\sqrt{\log n}\left(m_{\sigma}+1\right)^{\frac{r+1}{2}}+C_{\gamma}\sqrt{\log n}m_{\sigma}^{\frac{r+1}{2}}

(6.71)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\lambda}{k^{r/2}}r\ell_{\sigma}^{r-1}-C_{3}m_{\sigma}^{(r-1)/2}\sqrt{\log n}-C_{\gamma}\sqrt{\log n}\left(m_{\sigma}+1\right)^{\frac{r+1}{2}}+C_{\gamma}\sqrt{\log n}m_{\sigma}^{\frac{r+1}{2}}

Since
\sigma\in\tilde{\Omega}(C)
, we have
\ell_{\sigma}^{r-1}\geq C^{r-1}\left(\frac{\sqrt{k\log n}}{\lambda}\right)k^{(r-1)/2}m_{\sigma}^{(r-1)/2}
, and therefore this is at least

(6.73)

\displaystyle\geq C^{r-1}r\sqrt{\log n}m_{\sigma}^{(r-1)/2}-C_{3}m_{\sigma}^{(r-1)/2}\sqrt{\log n}-C_{\gamma}\sqrt{\log n}\left(m_{\sigma}+1\right)^{\frac{r+1}{2}}+C_{\gamma}\sqrt{\log n}m_{\sigma}^{\frac{r+1}{2}}

(6.74)

\displaystyle\geq m_{\sigma}^{(r-1)/2}\sqrt{\log n}

for a large enough choice of
C
, and therefore (
A
) is satisfied with probability at least
1-n^{-1}
.

Case 3: proving (
B
)

Note that by an identical argument as in Case 2 as far as (
6.71
), we have with probability at least
1-n^{-1}
that
for every
\sigma\in\hat{\Omega}(C)
, there exist at least
\lfloor\frac{2}{3}(k-\ell_{\sigma})\rfloor
transitions that add a coordinate
i
in
\text{supp}(\theta)
and that satisfy

(6.75)

\displaystyle H_{(r+1)/2,\gamma}(\sigma+e_{i})-H_{(r+1)/2,\gamma}(\sigma)

(6.76)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\lambda}{k^{r/2}}r\ell_{\sigma}^{r-1}-C_{3}m_{\sigma}^{(r-1)/2}\sqrt{\log n}-C_{\gamma}\sqrt{\log n}\left(m_{\sigma}+1\right)^{\frac{r+1}{2}}+C_{\gamma}\sqrt{\log n}m_{\sigma}^{\frac{r+1}{2}}

By assumption that
\sigma\in\hat{\Omega}(C)
, we have
\ell_{\sigma}\geq\frac{k}{10}
, so this is at least

(6.77)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\lambda}{k^{r/2}}r\left(\frac{k}{10}\right)^{r-1}-C_{3}m_{\sigma}^{(r-1)/2}\sqrt{\log n}-C_{\gamma}\sqrt{\log n}\left(m_{\sigma}+1\right)^{\frac{r+1}{2}}+C_{\gamma}\sqrt{\log n}m_{\sigma}^{\frac{r+1}{2}}

and for large enough
C_{\gamma}
, this is at least

(6.78)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\lambda}{k^{r/2}}r\left(\frac{k}{10}\right)^{r-1}-2rC_{\gamma}\sqrt{\log n}m_{\sigma}^{\frac{r-1}{2}}

by assumption,
m_{\sigma}\leq\frac{3k}{2}
, so we lower bound this by

(6.79)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\lambda}{k^{r/2}}r\left(\frac{k}{10}\right)^{r-1}-2rC_{\gamma}\sqrt{\log n}\left(\frac{3k}{2}\right)^{\frac{r-1}{2}}

(6.80)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{r}{2\cdot 10^{r-1}}\lambda k^{\frac{r}{2}-1}

where the last inequality holds for a large enough choice of
C_{\lambda}
because the first term in
6.79
is dominating. To see this, simply apply the assumption
\lambda\geq C_{\lambda}\frac{n^{\frac{r-1}{2}}}{k^{\frac{r}{2}-1}}\sqrt{\log n}
. Combining this with the result for part (
A
), we see that (
A
) and (
B
) hold simultaneously with probability at least
1-3n^{-1}
, as desired.
∎

We now prove Lemma
6.3
.

Proof of Lemma
6.3
.

Consider

(6.81)

H_{(r+1)/2,\gamma}(\sigma)=\langle\sigma^{\otimes r},Y\rangle-\gamma\lVert\sigma\rVert_{0}^{(r+1)/2}=\frac{\lambda}{k^{r/2}}\langle\sigma,\theta\rangle^{r}+\langle\sigma^{\otimes r},W\rangle-C_{\gamma}\sqrt{\log n}\lVert\sigma\rVert_{0}^{(r+1)/2}.

It is easy to see that since
0\leq\langle\sigma,\theta\rangle\leq k
, it holds that

(6.82)

0\leq\frac{\lambda}{k^{r/2}}\langle\sigma,\theta\rangle^{r}\leq\lambda k^{r/2}

Next,
W
has
n^{r}
entries each distributed as
\mathcal{N}(0,1)
. It therefore holds by Lemmas
9.1
and
9.2
(with
u=\sqrt{\log n}
) that with probability at least
1-n^{-1/2}
,

(6.83)

\max_{\underline{h}\in[n]^{r}}\left|W_{\underline{h}}\right|\leq(\sqrt{2r}+1)\sqrt{\log n}

The sum
\langle\sigma^{\otimes r},W\rangle
has
\lVert\sigma\rVert_{0}^{r}\leq\left(\frac{3}{2}k\right)^{r}
nonzero terms, and so we have

(6.84)

-(\sqrt{2r}+1)\sqrt{\log n}\left(\frac{3}{2}k\right)^{r}\leq\langle\sigma^{\otimes r},W\rangle\leq(\sqrt{2r}+1)\sqrt{\log n}\left(\frac{3}{2}k\right)^{r}

Combining the two bounds, we obtain the desired result.
∎

Proof of Lemma
6.4
.

The proof is identical to the proof of Lemma
4.5
, but applied only to the case of
\sigma\in\{0,1\}^{n}
, and with a different exponent on
\lVert\sigma\rVert_{0}
.
∎

6.3.
Proofs of Auxiliary Lemmas

In order to prove Lemma
6.5
, we will need the following two technical results.

Lemma 6.6
.

Suppose
A\sim{\mathcal{N}}(0,\Sigma_{A})
and
B\sim{\mathcal{N}}(0,\Sigma_{B})
, where
A,B\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
. If
\Sigma_{B}-\Sigma_{A}
is positive semidefinite, then

(6.85)

\lVert A\rVert_{2}^{2}\preceq_{\rm st}\lVert B\rVert_{2}^{2}

Proof of Lemma
6.6
.

Since
A\sim{\mathcal{N}}(0,\Sigma_{A})
, write
A=\Sigma_{A}^{1/2}Z_{A}
, where
Z_{A}\sim{\mathcal{N}}(0,I)
. We therefore see that

(6.86)

\lVert A\rVert_{2}^{2}=(\Sigma_{A}^{1/2}Z_{A})^{\top}(\Sigma_{A}^{1/2}Z_{A})=Z_{A}^{\top}\Sigma_{A}Z_{A}

Next, since
\Sigma_{A}
is symmetric and positive semidefinite, there exists an orthonormal matrix
U_{A}
and a diagonal matrix
\Lambda_{A}=\mathrm{diag}(\lambda_{1}(A),\dots,\lambda_{d}(A))
such that

(6.87)

\Sigma_{A}=U_{A}\Lambda_{A}U_{A}^{\top}

where
(\lambda_{1}(A),\dots,\lambda_{d}(A))
are the (ordered) eigenvalues of
\Sigma_{A}
. Define
\tilde{Z}_{A}=U_{A}^{\top}Z
. Note that since
Z_{A}\sim{\mathcal{N}}(0,I)
and
U_{A}
is orthonormal,
\tilde{Z}_{A}\sim{\mathcal{N}}(0,I)
as well. Thus:

(6.88)

\displaystyle\lVert A\rVert_{2}^{2}=Z_{A}^{\top}\Sigma_{A}Z_{A}

\displaystyle=Z_{A}^{\top}U_{A}\Lambda_{A}U_{A}^{\top}Z_{A}

(6.89)

\displaystyle=\tilde{Z}_{A}^{\top}\Lambda_{A}\tilde{Z}_{A}

(6.90)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\lambda_{i}(A)(\tilde{Z}_{A})_{i}^{2}

Similarly for
B\sim{\mathcal{N}}(0,\Sigma_{B})
, we get

(6.91)

\displaystyle\lVert B\rVert_{2}^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\lambda_{i}(B)(\tilde{Z}_{B})_{i}^{2}

where
(\lambda_{1}(B),\dots,\lambda_{d}(B))
are the eigenvalues of
\Sigma_{B}
and
\tilde{Z}_{B}\sim{\mathcal{N}}(0,I)
. Since
\Sigma_{B}-\Sigma_{A}
is assumed positive semidefinite,
\lambda_{i}(B)\geq\lambda_{i}(A)
for each
i
. Note also that for each
i
,
(\tilde{Z}_{A})_{i}^{2}
and
(\tilde{Z}_{A})_{i}^{2}
both follow a
\chi^{2}_{1}
distribution. It therefore holds that for any
t>0
,

(6.92)

{\mathbb{P}}(\lVert A\rVert_{2}^{2}\geq t)\leq{\mathbb{P}}(\lVert B\rVert_{2}^{2}\geq t)

as desired.
∎

Lemma 6.7

(
[
RV13
]
, Theorem 1.1)
.

Let
Z\sim{\mathcal{N}}(0,I_{n})
be a standard Gaussian random vector. Let
A\in{\mathbb{R}}^{m\times n}

and
X=AZ\in{\mathbb{R}}^{m}
. Let
K=AA^{\top}
be the covariance matrix of
X
. There
exists a universal finite constant
C_{\mathrm{HW}}>1
such that, for any
a>0
, it holds,

(6.93)

{\mathbb{P}}(\|X\|_{2}^{2}\geq{\mathbb{E}}[\|X\|_{2}^{2}]+a)\leq\exp\left(-\frac{1}{C_{\mathrm{HW}}}\min\left(\frac{a^{2}}{\|K\|_{F}^{2}},\frac{a}{\|K\|_{2}^{2}}\right)\right)

We are now ready to prove Lemma
6.5
.

Proof of Lemma
6.5
.

Throughout this proof, we will use the shorthand

(6.94)

Z^{\sigma}_{i}(j)=\langle e_{i}^{\otimes j}\otimes\sigma^{\otimes(r-j)},W\rangle

for any
1\leq j\leq r
, any
\sigma\in{\mathcal{S}}_{m}
and any
i\in[T]
. We will further define

(6.95)

\tilde{Z}^{\sigma}_{i}(j)=\frac{Z^{\sigma}_{i}(j)}{\sqrt{r!r^{2}m^{r-j}}}

and the vector
\tilde{Z}^{\sigma}(j):=(\tilde{Z}_{i}^{\sigma}(j))_{i\in[T]}
. We first bound the second moments of each element of
\tilde{Z}^{\sigma}(j)
:

(6.96)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}[(\tilde{Z}^{\sigma}_{i}(j))^{2}]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{r!r^{2}m^{r-j}}{\mathbb{E}}[(Z^{\sigma}_{i}(j))^{2}]

(6.97)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{r!r^{2}m^{r-j}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}h_{j+1},\dots,h_{r}\in[m]\\
h^{\prime}_{j+1},\dots,h^{\prime}_{r}\in[m]\end{subarray}}\sigma_{h_{j+1}}\cdots\sigma_{h_{r}}\sigma_{h^{\prime}_{j+1}}\cdots\sigma_{h^{\prime}_{r}}{\mathbb{E}}[W_{i,h_{j+1},\dots,h_{r}}W_{i,h^{\prime}_{j+1},\dots,h^{\prime}_{r}}]

(6.98)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{r!r^{2}m^{r-j}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}h_{j+1},\dots,h_{r}\in[m]\\
h^{\prime}_{j+1},\dots,h^{\prime}_{r}\in[m]\end{subarray}}\sigma_{h_{j+1}}\cdots\sigma_{h_{r}}\sigma_{h^{\prime}_{j+1}}\cdots\sigma_{h^{\prime}_{r}}\mathbf{1}\{(i,h_{j+1},\dots,h_{r})=(i,h^{\prime}_{j+1},\dots,h^{\prime}_{r})\}

(6.99)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{r!m^{r-j}}{r!r^{2}m^{r-j}}\leq\frac{1}{4}<1

where the next-to-last inequality holds because
r\geq 2
. Similarly, for each
i,\ell\in[T]
with
i\not=\ell
, we have

(6.100)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}[\tilde{Z}^{\sigma}_{i}(j)\tilde{Z}^{\sigma}_{\ell}(j)]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{r!r^{2}m^{r-j}}{\mathbb{E}}[(Z^{\sigma}_{i}(j))(Z^{\sigma}_{\ell}(j))]

(6.101)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{r!r^{2}m^{r-j}}{\mathbb{E}}[\langle e_{i}^{\otimes j}\otimes\sigma^{\otimes r-j},W\rangle\langle e_{\ell}^{\otimes j}\otimes\sigma^{\otimes r-j},W\rangle]

(6.102)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{r!r^{2}m^{r-j}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}h_{j+1},\dots,h_{r}\in[m]\\
h^{\prime}_{j+1},\dots,h^{\prime}_{r}\in[m]\end{subarray}}\sigma_{h_{j+1}}\cdots\sigma_{h_{r}}\sigma_{h^{\prime}_{j+1}}\cdots\sigma_{h^{\prime}_{r}}{\mathbb{E}}[W_{i,h_{j+1},\dots,h_{r}}W_{\ell,h^{\prime}_{j+1},\dots,h^{\prime}_{r}}]

(6.103)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{r!r^{2}m^{r-j}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}h_{j+1},\dots,h_{r}\in[m]\\
h^{\prime}_{j+1},\dots,h^{\prime}_{r}\in[m]\end{subarray}}\sigma_{h_{j+1}}\cdots\sigma_{h_{r}}\sigma_{h^{\prime}_{j+1}}\cdots\sigma_{h^{\prime}_{r}}\mathbf{1}\{(i,h_{j+1},\dots,h_{r})=(\ell,h^{\prime}_{j+1},\dots,h^{\prime}_{r})\}

(6.104)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{r!(r-j)^{2}m^{r-j-1}}{r!r^{2}m^{r-j}}\leq\frac{1}{m}.

Letting
\Sigma
be the covariance matrix of
\tilde{Z}^{\sigma}(j)
, we therefore see that the diagonal elements of
\Sigma
are in
[0,\frac{1}{4}]
, while the off diagonal elements are in
[0,\frac{1}{m}]
. Next, define the
T\times T
matrix
K=(1-\frac{1}{m}){\rm Id}+\frac{1}{m}\mathbf{1}\mathbf{1}^{\top}
. We claim that
K-\Sigma
is positive semidefinite. To see this, let
x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{T}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}
. Then by the above bounds on
\Sigma
, we have

(6.105)

\displaystyle x^{\top}\Sigma x=\sum_{i=1}^{T}x_{i}(\Sigma x)_{i}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{T}x_{i}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{T}\Sigma_{ij}x_{j}\right)

(6.106)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{T}x_{i}\left(\frac{1}{4}x_{i}+\frac{1}{m}\sum_{j\neq i}^{T}x_{j}\right)

(6.107)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{4}\lVert x\rVert_{2}^{2}+\frac{1}{m}\left(x^{\top}\mathbf{1}\right)^{2}

It is easily checked that
x^{\top}Kx=(1-\frac{1}{m})\lVert x\rVert_{2}^{2}+\frac{1}{m}\left(x^{\top}\mathbf{1}\right)^{2}
, and therefore since
m\geq 2
,

(6.108)

x^{\top}(K-\Sigma)x\geq 0

which proves the claim. Letting
Z\sim{\mathcal{N}}(0,K)
, it thus holds by Lemma
6.6
that

(6.109)

\lVert\tilde{Z}^{\sigma}(j)\rVert_{2}\preceq_{\rm st}\lVert Z\rVert_{2}

Next, note that
\lVert K\rVert_{2}\leq\lVert K\rVert_{\infty}\leq 1+\frac{T}{m}
, so apply Lemma
6.7
to see that for some universal constant
C_{\mathrm{HW}}>1
,

(6.110)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}\left(\lVert\tilde{Z}^{\sigma}(j)\rVert_{2}^{2}\geq{\mathbb{E}}(\lVert Z\rVert_{2}^{2})+a\right)

(6.111)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}\left(\lVert Z\rVert_{2}^{2}\geq{\mathbb{E}}(\lVert Z\rVert_{2}^{2})+a\right)

(6.112)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\exp\left(-\frac{1}{C_{\mathrm{HW}}}\min\left\{\frac{a^{2}}{T+\frac{T^{2}}{m^{2}}},\frac{a}{1+\frac{T}{m}}\right\}\right)

(6.113)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n^{d}|{\mathcal{S}}|}

where the last inequality holds by (
6.36
). Taking a union bound over the elements of
{\mathcal{S}}_{m}
, it therefore holds with probability at least
1-n^{-d}
that for all
\sigma\in{\mathcal{S}}_{m}
,

(6.114)

\displaystyle\lVert Z^{\sigma}(j)\rVert_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq r!r^{2}m^{r-j}\left(a+{\mathbb{E}}(\lVert Z\rVert_{2}^{2})\right)

(6.115)

\displaystyle=r!r^{2}m^{r-j}\left(a+T)\right)

where for the last equality we use
{\mathbb{E}}(\lVert Z\rVert_{2}^{2})=T
. Hence for any
q\in[T],
by Markov’s inequality there at most
q
coordinates of
Z^{\sigma}(j)
with
(Z^{\sigma}_{i}(j))^{2}\geq\frac{1}{q}r!r^{2}m^{r-j}(a+T)
. Choosing
C=C(r)=\sqrt{r!}r
therefore completes the proof of the lemma.
∎

7.
Subset Gaussian Cloning

7.1.
Intuition

Iterative algorithms with input
Y
are hard to analyze because updates reuses the realization
W
, inducing correlations between iterates. Ideally, we hope to ignore these correlations and assume the
t
-th iteration “sees” the random variable
\tilde{Y}_{t}=\frac{\lambda}{k^{r/2}}\theta^{\otimes r}+\tilde{W}_{t}
where
\tilde{W}_{t}
is a Gaussian tensor independent of everything else.

Although this behavior is not true for general iterative algorithms, we design a “corrected” algorithm where this “fresh noise” behavior holds true. This idea underlies the random thresholds in Algorithms
6
and
7

from Section
2
. Indeed, these thresholds “noise” the observation
H_{\frac{r+1}{2},\gamma}(\sigma^{\prime})-H_{\frac{r+1}{2}}(S_{t})
from line 8 of both Algorithms
6
and
7
. Under this noise, each
t
iteration effectively uses the tensor
\tilde{Y}_{t}
mentioned above where
\tilde{W}
has (a bit) inflated variance compared to
W
. We present an abstraction of this technique, deemed
Subset Gaussian Cloning
, which can be applied to general Gaussian additive models.

7.2.
The
{\mathcal{S}}{\mathcal{G}}{\mathcal{C}}
Algorithm

Definition 7.1
.

We consider a Gaussian additive model defined
as follows. Let
N\in{\mathbb{N}}
, “signal”
\mu^{*}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
, “noise”
Z\sim{\mathcal{N}}(0,{\rm Id})
, and a sequence of observations
(Y_{i})_{i\in[N]}=(\mu^{*}_{i}+Z_{i})_{i\in[N]}
.

Now, consider
M\in{\mathbb{N}}
and a (possibly random, independent of
everything else) sequence of sets
(\Delta_{t})=(\Delta_{t})_{t\in{\mathbb{N}}}\subseteq[N]^{\mathbb{N}}
. Then,
Subset Gaussian Cloning
, abbreviated as
