{\mathcal{S}}{\mathcal{G}}{\mathcal{C}}(\mu^{*},(\Delta_{t}),M)
, is defined in Algorithm
13
. It takes an observation
Y\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
, and a sequence of subsets
(\Delta_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{N}}
of
[N]
, and returns for each subset
\Delta_{t}
the noised observation
X_{\Delta_{t}}
, which is simply
Y_{\Delta_{t}}
with an appropriate amount of correlated Gaussian noise added (
Y_{\Delta_{t}}\in\mathbb{R}^{|\Delta_{t}|}
is defined in the obvious way).

Algorithm 13

Subset Gaussian Cloning

0:

Observation
Y\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
, parameter
M\in{\mathbb{N}}
, coordinate subsets
(\Delta_{t})=(\Delta_{t})_{t\in{\mathbb{N}}}\subseteq[N]^{\mathbb{N}}

1:

for

i=1
to
N

do

2:

Sample
G_{i}^{1},\dots,G_{i}^{M}
i.i.d. from
{\mathcal{N}}(0,M)
.

3:

\displaystyle\bar{G}_{i}\leftarrow\frac{1}{M}\sum_{j=1}^{M}G_{i}^{j}

4:

b_{i}^{0}\leftarrow 0

5:

end

for

6:

t\leftarrow 0

7:

T_{M}\leftarrow 0

8:

while

\max_{i\in[N]}\sum_{t^{\prime}\leq t}1\!\!1\{i\in\Delta_{t^{\prime}}\}\leq M
4
4

4

Note that this condition ensures that each coordinate
i\in[N]
is updated at most
M
times.

do

9:

t\leftarrow t+1

10:

T_{M}\leftarrow t

11:

for

i\in[N]

do

12:

if

i\in\Delta_{t}

then

13:

b_{i}^{t}\leftarrow b_{i}^{t-1}+1

14:

else

15:

b_{i}^{t}\leftarrow b_{i}^{t-1}

16:

end

if

17:

end

for

18:

(7.1)

X_{\Delta_{t}}\leftarrow Y_{\Delta_{t}}+\left(G^{b^{t}_{\Delta_{t}}}_{\Delta_{t}}-\bar{G}_{\Delta_{t}}\right)

19:

end

while

20:

return

(X_{\Delta_{t}})_{t\in[T_{M}]}

Notably (as we see in a moment), the algorithm
{\mathcal{S}}{\mathcal{G}}{\mathcal{C}}(\mu^{*},(\Delta_{t}),M)
which has input
Y_{i},i\in[N]
has output

(7.2)

(X_{t})_{t\in[T_{M}]}=\left(\mu^{*}_{\Delta_{t}}+(G^{\prime})_{\Delta_{t}}^{b^{t}_{\Delta_{t}}}\right)_{t\in[T_{M}]},

where each
(G^{\prime})_{i}^{j}\overset{\mathrm{iid}}{\sim}{\mathcal{N}}(0,M)
for
i\in[N]
and

j\in[M].

Observe, the final value of
T_{M}
from Algorithm
13
is equivalent to
the runtime of Algorithm
6
. That is,
T_{M}
at the time
of termination is a random function in the probability space of
(\Delta_{t})_{t\in{\mathbb{N}}}
and can be equivalently defined as

(7.3)

T_{M}((\Delta_{t}))=T_{M}=\inf\left\{t\in{\mathbb{N}}:\max_{i\in[N]}\sum_{t^{\prime}\leq t}1\!\!1\{i\in\Delta_{t}\}=M+1\right\}.

This “noising” technique is an extension of Gaussian cloning (an implication of
the infinite divisibility of Gaussians
[
Sat13
, Chapter 2]
) inspired by the
noise injection methods of
[
ADGM17
, Algorithm 2]
.

7.3.
{\mathcal{S}}{\mathcal{G}}{\mathcal{C}}
Has An Independent Gaussian Law

Theorem 7.2
.

Let
T_{M}
and
(X_{\Delta_{t}})_{t\in[T_{M}]}
be the runtime and output of Algorithm
13

with input
(Y,M,(\Delta_{t}))
where
Y
is from Definition
7.1
for some signal
\mu^{*}
. Then, almost surely over the
random function
T_{M}
,

(X_{\Delta_{t}})_{t\in[T_{M}]}
is a sequence of independent Gaussians with each satisfying

X_{\Delta_{t}}\overset{{\mathcal{L}}}{=}\mu^{*}_{\Delta_{t}}+Z^{\prime}_{\Delta_{t}}
where
Z^{\prime}_{\Delta_{t}}\sim{\mathcal{N}}(0,M\cdot{\rm Id}_{|\Delta_{t}|})
independent of
(\Delta_{t})
.

Proof.

The random variable
T_{M}
has support
{\mathbb{N}}\cap[M,MN]
. Moreover, it is
important to note that random variable
T_{M}
is independent of both the Gaussian
additive model
Y=\mu^{*}+Z
from Definition
7.1
and the sampled
Gaussians
G^{j}_{i}
for each
i\in[N]
and
j\in[M]
as
(\Delta_{t})
is
independent of both random variables. Hence, the following proof is given conditional on a fixed
T_{M}\in({\mathbb{N}}\cap[M,MN])
.

The proof follows from calculating the mean and covariance of

X_{t}=Y_{\Delta_{t}}+(G^{b^{t}_{\Delta_{t}}}_{\Delta_{t}}-\bar{G}_{\Delta_{t}})
and

X_{t^{\prime}}
for two times
t,t^{\prime}\in[T_{M}]
. As
(X_{t})_{t\in[T_{M}]}
is a sum of Gaussian vectors, then
(X_{t})_{t\in[T_{M}]}

is itself a Gaussian vector, concluding the proof. For notational simplicity, we drop the superscript

t
on
b^{t}_{\Delta_{t}}
as it is clear from the context.

First, we check the mean. This follows from the fact that
Y_{\Delta_{t}}=\mu^{*}_{\Delta_{t}}+Z_{\Delta_{t}}
where
Z_{\Delta_{t}}\overset{\mathrm{iid}}{\sim}{\mathcal{N}}(0,{\rm Id}_{|\Delta_{t}|})
and the fact that both
G^{b_{\Delta_{t}}}_{\Delta_{t}}
and
\bar{G}_{\Delta_{t}}
are centered Gaussians. Thus, we have the trivial calculation that

{\mathbb{E}}[X_{\Delta_{t}}]=\mu^{*}_{\Delta_{t}}
—the desired mean.

Second, we check the covariance. We calculate this value on the centered random
variable
\tilde{X}_{t}=Z_{\Delta_{t}}-G^{b_{\Delta_{t}}}_{\Delta_{t}}+\bar{G}_{\Delta_{t}}
as it has the same covariance structure as
X_{t}
. As
\tilde{X}_{t}

is centered, we need just calculate the expected product of each
\tilde{X}_{t,i}
and

\tilde{X}_{t^{\prime},i^{\prime}}
. For concreteness, let
t,t^{\prime}\in[T_{M}]
,
i,i^{\prime}\in\Delta_{t^{\prime}}
. Moreover, we let
j_{t}=b_{\Delta_{t}}
and
j_{t^{\prime}}=b_{\Delta_{t^{\prime}}}
. Calculate,

(7.4)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}[\tilde{X}_{t,i}\tilde{X}_{t^{\prime},i^{\prime}}]

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}[(Z_{i}+G^{j_{t}}_{i}-\bar{G}_{i})(Z_{i^{\prime}}+G^{j_{t^{\prime}}}_{i^{\prime}}-\bar{G}_{i^{\prime}})]

(7.5)

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}[Z_{i}Z_{i^{\prime}}]+{\mathbb{E}}[G^{j_{t}}_{i}G^{j_{t^{\prime}}}_{i^{\prime}}]-{\mathbb{E}}[G^{j_{t}}_{i}\bar{G}_{i^{\prime}}]-{\mathbb{E}}[\bar{G}_{i}G^{j_{t^{\prime}}}_{i^{\prime}}]+{\mathbb{E}}[\bar{G}_{i}\bar{G}_{i^{\prime}}].

We calculate the above summation term by term.

(1)

{\mathbb{E}}[Z_{i}Z_{i^{\prime}}]
: This term is zero if
i\neq i^{\prime}
and one if
i=i^{\prime}
by the assumption on
Z_{i}
in Definition
7.1
.

(2)

{\mathbb{E}}[G^{j_{t}}_{i}G^{j_{t^{\prime}}}_{i^{\prime}}]
: As we assume each
G_{i}^{j}
are
independent Gaussians for each
i\in[N]
and
j\in[M]
.
This term is
M
if
t=t^{\prime}
and
i=i^{\prime}
and zero otherwise.

(3)

{\mathbb{E}}[G^{j_{t}}_{i}\bar{G}_{i^{\prime}}]
: By the definition of
\bar{G}_{i}
from
Definition
7.1
,
{\mathbb{E}}[G^{j_{t}}_{i}\frac{1}{M}\sum_{j=1}^{M}G_{i^{\prime}}^{j}]=\frac{1}{M}{\mathbb{E}}[G^{j_{t}}_{i}G^{j_{t}}_{i^{\prime}}]
,
where the last equality is because
G^{j_{t}}_{i}
and
G^{j_{t^{\prime}}}_{i^{\prime}}

are independent if
j_{t^{\prime}}\neq j_{t}
. It then follows that

{\mathbb{E}}[G^{j_{t}}_{i}\bar{G}_{i^{\prime}}]=1
if and only if
i=i^{\prime}
.

(4)

{\mathbb{E}}[\bar{G}_{i}G^{j_{t^{\prime}}}_{i^{\prime}}]
: By symmetry, this term is also one if
and only if
i=i^{\prime}
.

(5)

{\mathbb{E}}[\bar{G}_{i}\bar{G}_{i^{\prime}}]
: We have that
{\mathbb{E}}[\bar{G}_{i}\bar{G}_{i^{\prime}}]=\frac{1}{M^{2}}\sum_{j=1}^{M}\sum_{j^{\prime}=1}^{M}{\mathbb{E}}[G_{i}^{j}G_{i^{\prime}}^{j^{\prime}}]
. This is equal to

\frac{1}{M^{2}}\sum_{j=1}^{M}M=1
if
i=i^{\prime}
and zero otherwise.

Plugging each term into (
7.5
) gives,

(7.6)

{\mathbb{E}}[\tilde{X}_{t,i}\tilde{X}_{t^{\prime},i^{\prime}}]=1\!\!1\{i=i^{\prime}\}+M\cdot 1\!\!1\{t=t^{\prime},i=i^{\prime}\}-2\cdot 1\!\!1\{i=i^{\prime}\}+1\!\!1\{i=i^{\prime}\}=M\cdot 1\!\!1\{t=t^{\prime},i=i^{\prime}\}.

This is the desired covariance, proving the equivalence in law for the sequence

(X_{\Delta_{t}})_{t\in[T_{M}]}
.

As we have proven this statement for any element in the support of the random
variable
T_{M}
, then the statement holds almost surely over
T_{M}
.
∎

This theorem prescribes that each returned value of
X_{\Delta_{t}}=Y_{\Delta_{t}}+(G^{b_{\Delta_{t}}}_{\Delta_{t}}-\bar{G}_{\Delta_{t}})
is equivalent to viewing

\mu^{*}_{\Delta_{t}}+{\rm noise}
where each “noise” term is an independent
Gaussian sample. The cost of
{\mathcal{S}}{\mathcal{G}}{\mathcal{C}}
’s Gaussian law is from
inflating the variance of the noise by a factor of
M
. To prove the
results from Section
2.3
, we choose
M=\mathrm{po\ell y}(\log(n))
. We will
see in the subsequent sections that by introducing some auxiliary variables, each algorithm defined in Section
2.3
is a
special case of
{\mathcal{S}}{\mathcal{G}}{\mathcal{C}}
with
n
Gaussian observations (See Lemma

8.13
and Lemma
8.14
for details). This will be of significant help in our analysis of the algorithms.

8.
Proof of Theorem
2.7
: The Trajectory of Randomized Greedy with Random Thresholds

In this section we prove Theorem
2.7
.

8.1.
Converting Algorithm

, Algorithm

.

Under these restated algorithms, we provide a more precise version of Theorem

2.7
.

Theorem 8.1

(A stronger statement to Theorem
2.7
)
.

Consider an arbitrary
\theta\in\{-1,0,1\}^{n}
,
\|\theta\|=k
. Assume
k=\Omega(\sqrt{n})
and let
r\geq 3
be any odd tensor power. Let
S_{0}=S_{\rm HOM}
. If
M_{1}=\lceil\log^{4}(n)\rceil
,
M_{2}=\lceil 25\log(3n)\rceil
,
\gamma=2(M_{2}\log(M_{2}n))^{1/2}+1
and
\lambda=\tilde{\Omega}(n^{r/4})
(dependent on
M_{2}
and
\gamma
),
then w.h.p. as
n\overset{}{\rightarrow}+\infty
, if
S_{1}
is the output of Algorithm

15
with input
(Y,S_{0},M_{1})
, then Algorithm
14
with
input
(Y,S_{1},\gamma,M_{2})
outputs
\theta
. Note that both algorithms terminate in
O(n\log^{4}n)

iterations.

We reiterate that Algorithms
14
and
15
differ from Algorithms
7
and
6
only in that they can admit the proposal
q_{t}=(S_{t})_{p_{t}}
where it is irrelevant if such a transition is accepted or not. This change to a “lazy” randomized greedy scheme is made to simplify the analysis. To prove
Theorem
2.7
from Theorem
8.1
, consider the
subset of steps
\{t:q_{t}\neq(S_{t})_{p_{t}}\}
; this set corresponds to the iterative
steps of Algorithms
7
and
6
.
Hence, the obvious coupling which neglects the “lazy” steps where
q_{t}=(S_{t})_{p_{t}}
implies that Algorithms
14
and
15
succeed whenever Algorithms
7
and
6
in at most the same time. Thus Theorem
2.7
holds when Theorem
8.1

holds.

The remainder of this subsection is dedicated to proving Theorem

8.1
, and thus Theorem
2.7
. More precise
conditions on
\lambda
are given in Assumption
8.6
.

8.2.
Useful Definitions And Constants

Recall from Theorem
8.1
we defined
M_{1}=\lceil\log^{4}(n)\rceil
and
M_{2}=\lceil 25\log(3n)\rceil
. Throughout this
section,
S_{t}\in\{-1,0,1\}^{n}
denotes the iterates of either Algorithm

14
or Algorithm
15
, depending on context. Note, for both
Algorithms
14
and
15
, we have introduced random variables

p_{t}
and
q_{t}
(for Algorithm
15
we fix
q_{t}=-(S_{t})_{p_{t}}
) representing the proposal to change coordinate
(S_{t})_{p_{t}}
to

q_{t}
at time
t
. We also defined
T^{*}
as the final time step in Algorithm
15
.

Finally, we define three constants we use repeatedly in what follows. Let,

(8.3)

C_{\rm Noise}:=\left(\sum_{j=1}^{r}\binom{r}{j}^{2}2^{2j}\right)^{1/2}\!\!\!\!\!\!,\qquad C_{\mathrm{Signa\ell}}:=r,\qquad C_{\mathrm{Reg}}:=\frac{r+1}{2}.

Before we prove Theorem
8.1
(And thus Theorem

2.7
), we provide a decomposition of both
D_{t}
and
D^{\prime}_{t}
used
in Algorithm
14
and Algorithm
15
respectively. Through this
decomposition, three terms representing the contribution of the signal, noise,
and regularization are identified.

Definition 8.2
.

Define the following five functions
dependent on either Algorithm
14
or Algorithm
15
’s iterate

S_{t}
and its the assignment of
p_{t}
and
q_{t}
inducing the proposal
S^{\prime}=S_{t}+e_{p_{t}}(q_{t}-(S_{t})_{p_{t}})
(note for Algorithm

15
,
q_{t}=-(S_{t})_{p_{t}}
):

(8.4)

\displaystyle f_{\theta,\lambda}(S_{t},p_{t},q_{t})

\displaystyle=\frac{\lambda}{k^{r/2}}\langle(S^{\prime})^{\otimes r},\,\theta^{\otimes r}\rangle-\langle(S_{t})^{\otimes r},\,\theta^{\otimes r}\rangle,

(8.5)

\displaystyle g_{W}(S_{t},p_{t},q_{t})

\displaystyle=\bigl\langle(S^{\prime})^{\otimes r},\,W\bigr\rangle-\langle(S_{t})^{\otimes r},\,W\rangle,

(8.6)

\displaystyle h_{\gamma}(S_{t},p_{t},q_{t})

\displaystyle=\gamma\left[\Bigl(\bigl\|S_{t}\bigr\|_{0}+\bigl|q_{t}\bigr|-\bigl|(S_{t})_{p_{t}}^{t}\bigr|\Bigr)^{\frac{r+1}{2}}-\,\bigl\|S_{t}\bigr\|_{0}^{\frac{r+1}{2}}\right],

(8.7)

\displaystyle V(S_{t},p_{t},q_{t})

\displaystyle=\|(S^{\prime})^{\otimes r}-(S_{t})^{\otimes r}\|_{F}

(8.8)

\displaystyle K_{p_{t},t_{p_{t}},q_{t}}

\displaystyle=\bigl(G_{p_{t}}^{\,t_{p_{t}}}-\bar{G}_{p_{t}}\bigr)V

Above, the term
K_{p_{t},t_{p_{t}},q_{t}}
uses the Gaussian random variable
G_{i}^{j}
for

i\in[n]
and
j\in[M]
utilized in Algorithms
14
and
15
. The usefulness of the mentioned functions is discussed in the following remark.

Remark 8.3

(Decomposing The Difference of Hamiltonian
H_{\frac{r+1}{2},\gamma}
)
.

Consider the Hamiltonian
H_{\frac{r+1}{2},\gamma}
from
(
1.3
),
S_{t}\in\{-1,0,1\}^{n}
and proposals
p_{t},q_{t}\in[n]\times\{-1,0,1\}
. Denote the corresponding proposed transition by

S^{\prime}=S_{t}+e_{p_{t}}(q_{t}-(S_{t})_{p_{t}})
. Then, we decompose the Hamiltonian difference as
follows:

(8.9)

\displaystyle H_{\frac{r+1}{2},\gamma}(S^{\prime})-H_{\frac{r+1}{2},\gamma}(S_{t})

\displaystyle=\langle S^{\prime\otimes r},Y\rangle-\gamma\|S^{\prime}\|_{0}^{\frac{r+1}{2}}-\langle(S_{t})^{\otimes r},Y\rangle+\gamma\|S_{t}\|_{0}^{\frac{r+1}{2}}

(8.10)

\displaystyle=\langle(S_{t}+e_{p_{t}}(q_{t}-(S_{t})_{p_{t}}))^{\otimes r},Y\rangle-\gamma\|S_{t}+e_{p_{t}}(q_{t}-(S_{t})_{p_{t}})\|_{0}^{\frac{r+1}{2}}

(8.11)

\displaystyle\qquad-\langle(S_{t})^{\otimes r},Y\rangle+\gamma\|S_{t}\|_{0}^{\frac{r+1}{2}}

(8.12)

\displaystyle=\frac{\lambda}{k^{r/2}}\langle(S_{t}+e_{p_{t}}(q_{t}-(S_{t})_{p_{t}}))^{\otimes r},\theta^{\otimes r}\rangle-\langle(S_{t})^{\otimes r},\theta^{\otimes r}\rangle

(8.13)

\displaystyle\qquad+\langle(S_{t}+e_{p_{t}}(q_{t}-(S_{t})_{p_{t}}))^{\otimes r},W\rangle-\langle(S_{t})^{\otimes r},W\rangle

(8.14)

\displaystyle\qquad-\gamma\left((\|S_{t}\|_{0}+(|q_{t}|-|(S_{t})_{p_{t}}^{t}|))^{\frac{r+1}{2}}-\|S_{t}\|_{0}^{\frac{r+1}{2}}\right),

(8.15)

\displaystyle=f_{\theta,\lambda}(S_{t},p_{t},q_{t})+g_{W}(S_{t},p_{t},q_{t})-h_{\gamma}(S_{t},p_{t},q_{t})

Now, given this decomposition, to analyze important quantities of our algorithm such as
D_{t},D^{\prime}_{t}
we can focus on bounding
f_{\theta,\lambda}(S_{t},p_{t},q_{t})
,
g_{W}(S_{t},p_{t},q_{t})
,
and
h_{\gamma}(S_{t},p_{t},q_{t})
. We accomplish this by the auxiliary results, Lemmas

8.18
,
8.19
,
8.20
and

8.21
and collect many useful bounds for these terms
in Corollary
8.11
; we use them extensively.

8.3.
An Initialization-Signal Trade-off For Algorithm
7

On the path to proving Theorem
2.7
, we establish a trade-off
between the quality of an initialization
S_{1}
and the minimal value of

\lambda
for which Algorithm
7
recovers
\theta
. This
trade-off has the following informal description: If

(8.16)

\lambda=\tilde{\Omega}\left(\frac{\sqrt{k}}{\cos(S_{1},\theta)^{r-1}}\right),

then Algorithm
7

“monotonically” converges to
\theta
, in the sense that at every change we strictly decrease the Hamming distance to
\theta
. This result can be (intuitively)
interpreted as a condition for local convexity about the true solution
\theta

for the Hamiltonian
H_{\frac{r+1}{2},\gamma}
for a tuned
\gamma
and

\lambda
large enough. The condition
(
8.16
) is codified by the following Lemma.

Lemma 8.4
.

For any
M\in{\mathbb{N}}
, let
C^{*}_{n}=2M^{1/2}\sqrt{\log(Mn)}
and
\gamma=C^{*}_{n}C_{\rm Noise}/C_{\rm Reg}+1
. For all
t\in[T^{*}_{M}]
, where

(8.17)

T^{*}_{M}=\min\left\{t\in{\mathbb{N}}:\max_{i\in[n]}\sum_{t^{\prime}\leq t}1\!\!1\{p_{t^{\prime}}=i\}=M+1\right\}.

Then, the following holds with probability
1-o(1)
:

If
S_{1}\in\{-1,0,1\}^{n}
,
\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
, satisfy
\langle S_{1},\theta\rangle\geq 2
and
5
5

5

As any vector
v\in\{-1,0,1\}^{n}
has
\|v\|_{2}^{2}=\|v\|_{0}
.

(8.18)

\left(\frac{\langle S_{1},\theta\rangle-2}{\|\theta\|_{2}(\|S_{1}\|_{2}+1)}\right)^{r-1}=\left(\frac{\langle S_{1},\theta\rangle-2}{k^{1/2}(\|S_{1}\|_{0}+1)^{1/2}}\right)^{r-1}\geq\frac{2\gamma}{C_{\rm Signa\ell}}\frac{\sqrt{k}}{\lambda},

then, for each
t\in[T_{M}^{*}]
,

(1)

When
p_{t},q_{t}
are such that
\theta_{p_{t}}=(S_{t})_{p_{t}}
and

q_{t}\neq\theta_{p_{t}}
, then
D_{t}<0
, leading to rejection.

(2)

When
p_{t},q_{t}
are such that
\theta_{p_{t}}\neq(S_{t})_{p_{t}}
and

q_{t}=\theta_{p_{t}}
, then
D_{t}>0
, leading to acceptance.

The proof of Lemma
8.4
is given in Section
8.5
.

8.4.
Proving Theorem
8.1
/
2.7

Now, we turn to the proof of Theorem
8.1
. Assume that
k\geq C_{k}\sqrt{n}
for some positive constant
C_{k}
. Before continuing, we make a quantitative assumption on
\lambda
to
make the following analysis more explicit.

Remark 8.5
.

For convenience, parameterize
\lambda=\tau\sqrt{M_{1}n^{r/4}}
, recalling that
M_{1}=\lceil\log^{4}(n)\rceil
from Theorem

8.1
;
\tau
may scale with
n
. As
M_{1}=\mathrm{po\ell y}(\log(n))
, it
suffices to prove the statement in Theorem
8.1
for all

\tau
larger than a
\mathrm{po\ell y}(\log(n))
factor, as then
\tau\sqrt{M_{1}}

remains
\mathrm{po\ell y}(\log(n))
.

Assumption 8.6
.

Assume the following on
\tau
, recalling

M_{1}=\lceil\log^{4}(n)\rceil
and
M_{2}=\lceil 25\log(3n)\rceil
:

(Here
C^{*}_{n}=2(M_{2})^{1/2}\sqrt{\log(M_{2}n)}
and
\gamma=(C^{*}_{n}C_{\rm Noise})/C_{\rm Reg}+1
from
Lemma
8.4
)

For a sufficiently large constant
C_{\tau}>0
(dependent on
r
and the constant
C_{k}>0
that comes from the inequality
k\geq C_{k}\sqrt{n}
),

(a)

\tau\geq C_{\tau}\gamma/\sqrt{M_{1}}
,

(b)

\tau\geq C_{\tau}C_{\rm Noise}\sqrt{\log(M_{1})}/C_{\rm Signa\ell}

(c)

\tau\geq C_{\tau}
.

Note that all terms in the right hand sides of the above inequalities are of
\mathrm{po\ell y}(\log(n))
order.

We see momentarily that we often need to consider
V(S_{t},p_{t},q_{t})
, given in
Definition
8.2
, when
\|S_{t}\|_{0}=n
and
\|S_{t}-2(S_{t})_{p_{t}}e_{p_{t}}\|_{0}=n
. When this is the case, by using
\|v\|_{2}^{2}=\|v\|_{0}
for vectors in
\{-1,1\}^{n}
, we use the shorthand

(8.19)

\displaystyle V_{n}

\displaystyle:=V(S_{t},p_{t},q_{t})=(\|(S_{t}-2e_{p_{t}})^{\otimes r}-(S_{t})^{\otimes r}\|_{F}^{2})^{1/2}

(8.20)

\displaystyle=(\|S_{t}-2(S_{t})_{p_{t}}e_{p_{t}}\|^{2r}_{2}-2(\langle S_{t}-2(S_{t})_{p_{t}}e_{p_{t}},S_{t}\rangle)^{r}+\|S_{t}\|_{2}^{2r})^{1/2}

(8.21)

\displaystyle=(2n^{r}-2(\langle S_{t},S_{t}\rangle-2(S_{t})_{p_{t}}\langle e_{p_{t}},S_{t}\rangle)^{r})^{1/2}

(8.22)

\displaystyle=(2(n^{r}-(n-2)^{r}))^{1/2}.

Now, Theorem
8.1
(and thus, Theorem
2.7
) follows
from the following key lemmas. Their proofs are given in Section

8.5
. Notably, Lemma
8.7
is the only one that requires
r
to be odd.

Lemma 8.7

(“Homotopy initialization achieves sufficient overlap with
\theta
”)
.

Let
\lambda=\tau\sqrt{M_{1}}n^{r/4}
,
where
\tau
satisfies Assumption
8.6
and recall
S_{\rm HOM}
from
Definition
2.6
. If
r
is odd, then with probability
1-o(1)
,

(8.23)

\langle S_{\rm HOM},\theta\rangle\geq n^{1/4}k^{1/2}/5\qquad\text{and}\qquad\|S_{\rm HOM}\|_{0}=n.

Lemma 8.8

(“Algorithm
15
boosts small overlap to large overlap”)
.

Let
\lambda=\tau\sqrt{M_{1}}n^{r/4}
,
where
\tau
satisfies Assumption
8.6
. Assume that
S_{1}\in\{-1,1\}^{n}
has
\langle S_{1},\theta\rangle\geq n^{1/4}k^{1/2}/5
.

Then,
with probability
1-o(1)
, there exists a time step
t^{*}\leq\lfloor T^{*}/2\rfloor
in Algorithm
15
(with input
(Y,S_{1},\gamma,M_{2})
) such that
\langle S_{t^{*}},\theta\rangle\geq 2
and satisfies

(8.23)

\frac{2C_{\rm Signa\ell}\frac{\lambda}{k^{r/2}}}{V_{n}\sqrt{M_{1}}}(\langle S_{t^{*}},\theta\rangle-2)^{r-1}\geq 2\sqrt{\log(M_{1}n)}.\hypertarget{eq:threshold}{}

Lemma 8.9

(“Algorithm
15
boosts large overlap to sign recovery”)
.

Let
\lambda=\tau\sqrt{M_{1}}n^{r/4}
,
where
\tau
satisfies Assumption
8.6
.

Then, with probability
1-o(1)
,
if there exists a time step
t_{0}\leq\lfloor T^{*}/2\rfloor
in Algorithm

15
(with input
(Y,S_{1},\gamma,M_{2})
) such that
\langle S_{t_{0}},\theta\rangle\geq 2
and
satisfies (for

\varepsilon>0
sufficiently small),

(8.25)

\frac{2C_{\rm Signa\ell}\frac{\lambda}{k^{r/2}}}{V_{n}\sqrt{M_{1}}}(\langle S_{t_{0}},\theta\rangle-2)^{r-1}\geq(2-\varepsilon)\sqrt{\log(M_{1}n)},

we have that
Algorithm
15
outputs
S\in\{-1,1\}^{n}
with
\langle S,\theta\rangle=k
, i.e., for all
i\in[n]
if
\theta_{i}\neq 0,
it holds
S_{i}=\theta_{i}
.

Lemma 8.10

(“Algorithm
14
boosts sign recovery to full recovery” )
.

Let
\lambda=\tau\sqrt{M_{1}}n^{r/4}
and where
\tau
satisfies Assumption
8.6
.

Then, with
probability
1-o(1)
, Algorithm
14
with input
(Y,S_{1},\gamma,M_{2})
(with
\gamma
from Assumption
8.6
) outputs
\theta
whenever
\langle S_{1},\theta\rangle=k
,

\|S_{1}\|_{0}=n
.

Proof of Theorem
8.1
(and thus Theorem

2.7
).

Consider the following events for
r
odd:

(a)

\langle S_{\rm HOM},\theta\rangle\geq n^{1/4}k^{1/2}/5
with
\|S_{\rm HOM}\|_{0}=n
.

(b)

Algorithm
15
with input
(Y,S_{1},M_{1})
contains an iterate
S_{t^{*}}
for
t^{*}\leq\lfloor T^{*}/2\rfloor
such that,

(8.26)

\frac{2C_{\rm Signa\ell}\frac{\lambda}{k^{r/2}}}{V_{n}\sqrt{M_{1}}}(\langle S_{t^{*}},\theta\rangle-2)^{r-1}\geq 2\sqrt{\log(M_{1}n)}

and
\langle S_{t^{*}},\theta\rangle\geq 2
when
\langle S_{1},\theta\rangle\geq n^{1/4}k^{1/2}/5
with
\|S_{1}\|_{0}=n
.

(c)

If Algorithm
15
contains an iterate
S_{t_{0}}
with
\frac{2C_{\rm Signa\ell}\frac{\lambda}{k^{r/2}}}{V_{n}\sqrt{M_{1}}}(\langle S_{t_{0}},\theta\rangle-2)^{r-1}\geq(2-\varepsilon)\sqrt{\log(M_{1}n)}
and
\langle S_{t_{0}},\theta\rangle\geq 2
where
t_{0}\leq\lfloor T^{*}/2\rfloor

then Algorithm
15
outputs
S\in\{-1,1\}^{n}
where
\langle S,\theta\rangle=k
and
\|S\|_{0}=n
.

(d)

Algorithm
14
with input
(Y,S,\gamma,M_{2})
, where

\gamma
is from the statement of Lemma
8.10
and
S
is from
(c), outputs
\theta
.

The definition of Algorithm
15
(specifically how the only
proposed transitions in Algorithm
15
maintain
\|S_{t}\|_{0}=\|S_{t-1}\|_{0}
), Lemmas
8.7
,
8.8
,

8.9
and Lemma
8.10

imply that the events (a), (b), (c), and (d) hold jointly with
probability
1-o(1)
under Assumption
8.6
for
\lambda=\tau\sqrt{M_{1}}n^{r/4}
.
Therefore, Theorem
8.1
holds by the following argument:

By (a), we have that
\langle S_{\rm HOM},\theta\rangle\geq n^{1/4}k^{1/2}/5
. Thus, (b) implies that Algorithm
15
with
input
(Y,S_{\rm HOM},M_{1})
contains an iterate
S_{t^{*}}
for
t^{*}\leq\lfloor T^{*}/2\rfloor
such that

(8.27)

\frac{2C_{\rm Signa\ell}\frac{\lambda}{k^{r/2}}}{V_{n}\sqrt{M_{1}}}(\langle S_{t^{*}},\theta\rangle-2)^{r-1}\geq 2\sqrt{\log(M_{1}n)}.

The iterate
S_{t^{*}}
is then boosted by Algorithm
15
to output
iterate
S
with
\langle S,\theta\rangle=k
and
\|S\|_{0}=n
by
(c). Then, (d) finally implies Theorem
8.1
holds as (d)
implies Algorithm
14
with input
(Y,S,\gamma,M_{2})
outputs

\theta
. The statement on the run-time follows as both Algorithm
14
and
15
have, at most,
\Theta(n\log^{4}(n))
proposals.
∎

8.5.
Proofs of Key Lemmas

In this section we prove Lemmas
8.4
,
8.7
,
8.8
,
8.9
and
8.10
.

As previously mentioned, a majority of the proofs to key results require bounds
on the terms
f_{\theta,\lambda}(S_{t},p_{t},q_{t})
,
g_{W}(S_{t},p_{t},q_{t})
,
h_{\gamma}(S_{t},p_{t},q_{t})
. The unfortunately very technical corollary we provide below, which
is implied by the Lemmas
8.18
,
8.19
,

8.20
,
8.21
stated in Section

8.6
, is used of great help and used extensively in this
section. Its proof is deferred to Section
8.6
.

Corollary 8.11
.

If
S^{\prime}=S_{t}+e_{p_{t}}(q_{t}-(S_{t})_{p_{t}})
is the proposed transition, the following statements all hold:

(a)

Recall
g_{W}(S_{t},p_{t},q_{t})
from Definition
8.2
, let

Z
be a standard Gaussian, we have that
g_{W}(S_{t},p_{t},q_{t})\overset{{\mathcal{L}}}{=}V(S_{t},p_{t},q_{t})Z
.

(b)

Recall
V(S_{t},p_{t},q_{t})
from Definition
8.2
, we
have the upper bound:

(8.28)

V(S_{t},p_{t},q_{t})\leq C_{\rm Noise}|q_{t}-(S_{t})_{p_{t}}|\;\|S_{t}\|_{0}^{\frac{r-1}{2}}.

(c)

Let
K_{n}\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
such that
{\mathbb{P}}(|Z|\leq K_{n})=1-o(1)
for a standard Gaussian
Z
, then with probability
1-o(1)
:

(8.29)

\hskip-30.0pt-K_{n}\cdot C_{\rm Noise}|q_{t}-(S_{t})_{p_{t}}|\;\|S_{t}\|_{0}^{\frac{r-1}{2}}\leq g_{W}(S_{t},p_{t},q_{t})\leq K_{n}\cdot C_{\rm Noise}|q_{t}-(S_{t})_{p_{t}}|\;\|S_{t}\|_{0}^{\frac{r-1}{2}}.

(d)

Recall
f_{\theta,\lambda}(S_{t},p_{t},q_{t})
from Definition
8.2
. Assume
that

(8.30)

\min(\langle S_{t},\theta\rangle,\langle S^{\prime},\theta\rangle)\geq 0.

If
(q_{t}-e_{p_{t}})\theta_{p_{t}}\geq 0
, then

(8.31)

C_{\rm Signa\ell}\frac{\lambda}{k^{r/2}}\min(\langle S^{\prime},\theta\rangle,\langle S_{t},\theta\rangle)^{r-1}(q_{t}-(S_{t})_{p_{t}})\theta_{p_{t}}\leq f_{\theta,\lambda}(S_{t},p_{t},q_{t}),

(8.32)

\text{and,\;}f_{\theta,\lambda}(S_{t},p_{t},q_{t})\leq C_{\rm Signa\ell}\frac{\lambda}{k^{r/2}}\max(\langle S^{\prime},\theta\rangle,\langle S_{t},\theta\rangle)(q_{t}-(S_{t})_{p_{t}})^{r-1}\theta_{p_{t}}.

Moreover, if
(q_{t}-e_{p_{t}})\theta_{p_{t}}\leq 0
, then,

(8.33)

\hskip-40.0ptC_{\rm Signa\ell}\frac{\lambda}{k^{r/2}}\max(\langle S^{\prime},\theta\rangle,\langle S_{t},\theta\rangle)^{r-1}(q_{t}-(S_{t})_{p_{t}})\theta_{p_{t}}\leq f_{\theta,\lambda}(S_{t},p_{t},q_{t}),

(8.34)

\text{and,\;}f_{\theta,\lambda}(S_{t},p_{t},q_{t})\leq C_{\rm Signa\ell}\frac{\lambda}{k^{r/2}}\min(\langle S^{\prime},\theta\rangle,\langle S_{t},\theta\rangle)^{r-1}(q_{t}-(S_{t})_{p_{t}})\theta_{p_{t}}.

(e)

Recall
h_{\gamma}(S_{t},p_{t},q_{t})
from Definition
8.2
, we have the following upper and lower bounds:

(8.35)

C_{\rm Reg}\gamma\min(\|S_{t}\|_{0},\|S_{t}\|_{0}+(|q_{t}|-|(S_{t})_{p_{t}}|))^{\frac{r+1}{2}}(|q_{t}|-|(S_{t})_{p_{t}}|)\leq h_{\gamma}(S_{t},p_{t},q_{t}),

(8.36)

\text{and,\;}h_{\gamma}(S_{t},p_{t},q_{t})\leq C_{\rm Reg}\gamma\max(\|S_{t}\|_{0},\|S_{t}\|_{0}+(|q_{t}|-|(S_{t})_{p_{t}}|))^{\frac{r+1}{2}}(|q_{t}|-|(S_{t})_{p_{t}}|).

We also provide a more specific bounds on
V_{n}
used in Lemma
8.9
and Lemma
8.8

Corollary 8.12
.

We have the following upper bounds,

(8.37)

V_{n}=\|(S_{t}-2(S_{t})_{p_{t}}^{t}e_{p_{t}})^{\otimes r}-(S_{t})^{\otimes r}\|_{F}\leq C_{\rm Noise}\|S_{t}\|_{0}^{\frac{r-1}{2}}=C_{\rm Noise}n^{\frac{r-1}{2}},

Proof.

The penultimate inequality is due to (
8.28
) in Corollary
8.11
and the final inequality uses
\|S_{t}\|_{0}=n
.
∎

8.5.1.
Proof of Lemma
8.4

To prove Lemma

8.4
, we make use of the following lemma, whose proof is deferred to Section
8.6
.

Lemma 8.13
.

Let
M\in{\mathbb{N}}
be arbitrary,
(p_{t})_{t\in{\mathbb{N}}}
,
(q_{t})_{t\in{\mathbb{N}}}
be a sequence of independent uniform draws from

[n]
,
\{-1,0,1\}
, respectively. Define the random function,

(8.38)

T^{*}_{M}((p_{t})_{t\in{\mathbb{N}}})=T^{*}_{M}=\inf\left\{t\in{\mathbb{N}}:\max_{i\in[n]}\sum_{t^{\prime}\leq t}1\!\!1\{p_{t^{\prime}}=i\}=M+1\right\}.

Almost surely over the value of
T^{*}_{M}
, the
sequence of observations
(D_{t})_{t\in[T^{*}_{M}]}
from
(
8.1
), has the following equivalence in law,

(8.39)

\hskip-30.0pt(D_{t})_{t\in[T^{*}_{M}]}\overset{{\mathcal{L}}}{=}\bigg(f_{\theta,\lambda}(S_{t},p_{t},q_{t})+h_{\gamma}(S_{t},p_{t},q_{t})+V(S_{t},p_{t},q_{t})M^{1/2}Z_{t}\bigg)_{t\in[T^{*}_{M}]},

where each
Z_{t}\overset{\mathrm{iid}}{\sim}{\mathcal{N}}(0,1)
is independent of

(p_{t})_{t\in{\mathbb{N}}}
and thus
T^{*}_{M}
.

We are now ready to prove Lemma
8.4
.

Proof of Lemma
8.4
.

For each
t\in[T^{*}_{M}]
, let
Z_{t}\overset{\mathrm{iid}}{\sim}{\mathcal{N}}(0,1)
. Then by Lemma

8.13
, almost surely with respect to
T^{*}_{M}
, it holds that

(8.40)

\hskip-30.0pt(D_{t})_{t\in[T^{*}_{M}]}\overset{{\mathcal{L}}}{=}\bigg(f_{\theta,\lambda}(S_{t},p_{t},q_{t})+h_{\gamma}(S_{t},p_{t},q_{t})+V(S_{t},p_{t},q_{t})M^{1/2}Z_{t}\bigg)_{t\in[T^{*}_{M}]},

where

(D_{t})_{t\in[T^{*}_{M}]}
are the observations from
(
8.1
) and each
Z_{t}
is independent of
(p_{t})_{t\in[T^{*}_{M}]}
. By Lemma
9.10
and a union bound, we have

(8.41)

{\mathbb{P}}\left(\max_{t\in[T^{*}_{M}]}|Z_{t}|>2\sqrt{\log(Mn)}\right)\leq{\mathbb{P}}\left(\max_{t\in[nM]}|Z_{t}|>2\sqrt{\log(Mn)}\right)\leq Mn{\mathbb{P}}(|Z_{1}|>2\sqrt{\log(Mn)})=o(1),

and thus,
\max_{t\in[T_{M}^{*}]}M^{1/2}Z_{t}\leq 2M^{1/2}\sqrt{\log(Mn)}=C^{*}_{n}

with probability
1-o(1)
.
For the remainder of the proof of Lemma
8.4
, we will use the shorthand

S^{\prime}=S_{t}+e_{p_{t}}(q_{t}-(S_{t})_{p_{t}})
and condition on the above bound for
\max_{t\in[Mn]}|Z_{t}|
—an event with probability
1-o(1)
. For simplicity, we often abbreviate
\max_{t\in[Mn]}|Z_{t}|
by
\max|Z_{t}|
, leaving maximum over the set of
t\in[nM]
implicit.

We prove the following by induction on
t
: For each
t\in\{0,1,\dots,T^{*}_{M}\}
and for any transition that is accepted conditional on
\max|Z_{t}|\leq C_{n}^{*}
, we have that the statements (1) and (2) in Lemma
8.4
hold at time
t
and

(8.42)

\langle S_{t+1},\theta\rangle\geq 2

and

(8.43)

\left(\frac{\langle S_{t+1},\theta\rangle-2}{k^{1/2}(\|S_{t}\|_{0}+1)^{1/2}}\right)^{r-1}\geq\left(\frac{\langle S_{1},\theta\rangle-2}{k^{1/2}(\|S_{1}\|_{0}+1)^{1/2}}\right)^{r-1}\geq\frac{2\gamma}{C_{\rm Signa\ell}}\frac{\sqrt{k}}{\lambda},

(assuming, for notational simplicity, both the statements (1) and (2) in Lemma
8.4
at time
t=0
and (
8.42
), (
8.43
) at time
t=T^{*}_{M}+1
are voidly true).

For the base case
t=0
(
8.42
), (
8.43
) hold trivially by
assumption on
\langle S_{1},\theta\rangle
and the voidness of the the statements (1) and (2) in Lemma
8.4
at time
t=0
. Now, assume the inductive hypothesis holds at time
t
. We also repeat here for reference statements (1) and (2) from Lemma
8.4
for convenience.

(1)

When
p_{t},q_{t}
are such that
\theta_{p_{t}}=(S_{t})_{p_{t}}
and

q_{t}\neq\theta_{p_{t}}
, then
D_{t}<0
, leading to rejection.

(2)

When
p_{t},q_{t}
are such that
\theta_{p_{t}}\neq(S_{t})_{p_{t}}
and

q_{t}=\theta_{p_{t}}
, then
D_{t}>0
, leading to acceptance.

We now prove the inductive claim by showing that statements (1) and (2) hold for time
t
and the inequalities (
8.42
) and (
8.43
) hold for time
t+1
. We do this by a case-by-case analysis of the behavior of the algorithm under each proposal. Notice that some possible transitions do not belong in the set of transitions considered in statements (1) or (2). To address all possible transitions we divide our analysis in the following four steps.

•

Step 1.
Prove the inductive statement for the set of transitions that aren’t considered in the statements (1) and (2) in Lemma
8.4
and leave both the dot product of
S_{t}
with
\theta
and the support size of
S_{t}
unchanged. In that case the inductive claim boils down to proving (
8.42
) and (
8.43
).

•

Step 2.
Classify which proposals are going to be accepted or not for all the proposals considered in Table
1
, i.e. all proposals that are specified in statements (1) and (2).

•

Step 3.
Classify which proposals are going to be accepted or not for all the proposals considered in Table
2
, i.e., not specified in Steps 1 and 2.

•

Step 4.
Combine our findings from Steps 3,4 to prove the inductive step.

To further assist the reader, in the Tables
1
and
2
we added an extra column, using “A” for the cases we will prove
D_{t}>0
(i.e., acceptance) with high probability and
for transitions with a “R” we will prove
D_{t}<0
(i.e., rejection) with high probability (now, without the conditioned event on
\max|Z_{t}|
).

Step 1.

There are two sets of proposals we will consider here.
If a proposal has
(S_{t})_{p_{t}}=q_{t}
, then the
proposal
S^{\prime}
has
\langle S^{\prime},\theta\rangle=\langle S_{t},\theta\rangle
and
\|S^{\prime}\|_{0}=\|S_{t}\|_{0}
, for which the inductive claim of inequality (
8.43
) holds trivially for
t+1
whether the algorithm accepts the proposal or not. Moreover, the absence of change in
\langle S^{\prime},\theta\rangle
and
\|S^{\prime}\|_{0}
when

(\theta_{p_{t}},(S_{t})_{p_{t}},q_{t})=(0,1,-1)
or
(0,-1,1)
, means these two proposals also satisfy the inductive claim of inequality (
8.43
) trivially for
t+1
regardless of the proposals acceptance or rejection.
Note, neither of these transitions satisfy the statements of (1) or (2) from Lemma
8.4
so these transitions can be safely ignored with respect to statements (1) and (2) in the inductive claim.

\theta_{p_{t}}=-1

\theta_{p_{t}}=0

which satisfy conditions (1) or (2) from Lemma

.
The first two columns give a possible transition with respect to the value of
\theta_{p_{t}}
. The third columns details where we prove that a given proposal is accepted or rejected. The fourth column, A/R (short for accept or reject) designates whether we will prove that the proposal is going to be accepted or not with high probability.

Step 2.

In this step, we analyze the different proposal split into a series of cases. All cases (in both Steps 2,3) are hanled in the same fashion. First we invoke Lemma
8.13
according to which we know the following equality in law,

(8.44)

D_{t}\overset{{\mathcal{L}}}{=}f_{\theta,\lambda}(S_{t},p_{t},q_{t})+h_{\gamma}(S_{t},p_{t},q_{t})+V(S_{t},p_{t},q_{t})M^{1/2}Z_{t}.

We then plug
in the given choices of
\theta_{p_{t}},(S_{t})_{p_{t}},q_{t}
of each case, and we employ the
bounds from Corollary
8.11
(alongside our conditional bound on
the
\max|Z_{t}|
) to control the difference
D_{t}
and derive the desired acceptance/rejection guarantees. On top of that, we use that via the
inductive hypothesis,
\langle S_{t},\theta\rangle\geq 2
,
and therefore any transition
S^{\prime}
will have
\langle S^{\prime},\theta\rangle\geq 0
as no proposal from the algorithm can decrease
\langle S_{t},\theta\rangle
by more than two. For this reason, we will also use
Corollary
8.11
for time
t
to bound
f_{\theta,\lambda}(S_{t},p_{t},q_{t})
.

(a)

First, we consider transitions which satisfy condition (1) in the statement of Lemma
8.4
. For all we prove the algorithm is going to reject them with high probability.

We consider three sub-cases (i), (ii) and (iii):

(i)

If
(\theta_{p_{t}},(S_{t})_{p_{t}},q_{t})=(0,0,1)
or
(0,0,-1)
, then

(8.45)

(q_{t}-(S_{t})_{p_{t}})\theta_{p_{t}}=0,\quad\langle S_{t},\theta\rangle=\langle S^{\prime},\theta\rangle,\quad|q_{t}|-|(S_{t})_{p_{t}}|=1,\quad|q_{t}-(S_{t})_{p_{t}}|=1.

Therefore, using
(
8.34
), (
8.35
), and (
8.29
),

(8.46)

\displaystyle f_{\theta,\lambda}(S_{t},p_{t},q_{t})

\displaystyle\leq C_{\rm Signa\ell}\frac{\lambda}{k^{r/2}}(\langle S_{t},\theta\rangle-2)^{r-1}(q_{t}-(S_{t})_{p_{t}})\theta_{p_{t}}=0,

(8.47)

\displaystyle h_{\gamma}(S_{t},p_{t},q_{t})

\displaystyle\geq C_{\rm Reg}\gamma\|S_{t}\|_{0}^{(r-1)/2},

(8.48)

\displaystyle\text{and, }\quad g_{W}(S_{t},p_{t},q_{t})

\displaystyle\leq C^{*}_{n}C_{\rm Noise}\|S_{t}\|_{0}^{(r-1)/2}.

Thus,
D_{t}
is bounded above by,

(8.49)

D_{t}\leq C^{*}_{n}C_{\rm Noise}\|S_{t}\|_{0}^{(r-1)/2}-C_{\rm Reg}\gamma\|S_{t}\|_{0}^{(r-1)/2}<0,\text{ { as } }\gamma>C^{*}_{n}C_{\rm Noise}/C_{\rm Reg}.

(ii)

If
(\theta_{p_{t}},(S_{t})_{p_{t}},q_{t})=(-1,-1,1)
or

(1,1,-1)
, then

(8.50)

(q_{t}-(S_{t})_{p_{t}})\theta_{p_{t}}=-2,\quad\langle S_{t},\theta\rangle-2=\langle S^{\prime},\theta\rangle,\quad|q_{t}|-|(S_{t})_{p_{t}}|=0,\quad|q_{t}-(S_{t})_{p_{t}}|=2.

Therefore, using (
8.34
) and
(
8.29
),

(8.51)

\displaystyle f_{\theta,\lambda}(S_{t},p_{t},q_{t})

\displaystyle\leq-2C_{\rm Signa\ell}\frac{\lambda}{k^{r/2}}(\langle S_{t},\theta\rangle-2)^{r-1}

(8.52)

\displaystyle\text{and, }\quad g_{W}(S_{t},p_{t},q_{t})

\displaystyle\leq 2C^{*}_{n}C_{\rm Noise}\|S_{t}\|_{0}^{(r-1)/2}.

Thus,
D_{t}
is bounded above by,

\begin{split}D_{t}&\leq 2\left(-C_{\rm Signa\ell}\frac{\lambda}{k^{r/2}}(\langle S_{t},\theta\rangle-2)^{r-1}+C^{*}_{n}C_{\rm Noise}\|S_{t}\|_{0}^{(r-1)/2}\right)<0,\\
&\text{{ as } }\frac{\lambda}{k^{r/2}}\left(\langle S_{t},\theta\rangle-2\right)^{r-1}>C^{*}_{n}C_{\rm Noise}\|S_{t}\|_{0}^{(r-1)/2}/C_{\rm Signa\ell}.\end{split}

(iii)

If

(\theta_{p_{t}},(S_{t})_{p_{t}},q_{t})=(1,1,0)
or
(-1,-1,0)
,
then,

(8.53)

(q_{t}-(S_{t})_{p_{t}})\theta_{p_{t}}=-1,\qquad\langle S_{t},\theta\rangle-1=\langle S^{\prime},\theta\rangle,\qquad|q_{t}|-|(S_{t})_{p_{t}}|=1,\qquad|q_{t}-(S_{t})_{p_{t}}|=1.

Therefore, using
(
8.34
), (
8.36
), and (
8.29
),

(8.54)

\displaystyle f_{\theta,\lambda}(S_{t},p_{t},q_{t})

\displaystyle\leq-C_{\rm Signa\ell}\frac{\lambda}{k^{r/2}}(\langle S_{t},\theta\rangle-2)^{r-1},

(8.55)

\displaystyle h_{\gamma}(S_{t},p_{t},q_{t})

\displaystyle\leq C_{\rm Reg}\gamma(\|S_{t}\|_{0}+1)^{(r-1)/2},

(8.56)

\displaystyle\text{and, }\quad g_{W}(S_{t},p_{t},q_{t})

\displaystyle\leq C^{*}_{n}C_{\rm Noise}\|S_{t}\|_{0}^{(r-1)/2}.

Thus,
D_{t}
is bounded above by,

\begin{split}D_{t}&\leq-C_{\rm Signa\ell}\frac{\lambda}{k^{r/2}}(\langle S_{t},\theta\rangle-1)^{r-1}+C^{*}_{n}C_{\rm Noise}\|S_{t}\|_{0}^{(r-1)/2}+\gamma C_{\rm Reg}(\|S_{t}\|_{0}+1)^{(r-1)/2}<0,\\
&\text{ { as } }\frac{\lambda}{k^{r/2}}(\langle S_{t},\theta\rangle-1)^{r-1}>(C^{*}_{n}C_{\rm Noise}\|S_{t}\|_{0}^{(r-1)/2}+C_{\rm Reg}\gamma(\|S_{t}\|_{0}+1)^{(r-1)/2})/C_{\rm Signa\ell}.\end{split}

(b)

Second, we consider transitions which satisfy condition (2) in the statement of
Lemma
8.4
. For all we prove the algorithm is going to accept them with high probability.

Again, we consider three sub-cases (i), (ii) and (iii):

(i)

If
(\theta_{p_{t}},(S_{t})_{p_{t}},q_{t})=(0,1,0)
or
(0,-1,0)
, then

(8.57)

\hskip-42.67912pt(q_{t}-(S_{t})_{p_{t}})\theta_{p_{t}}=0,\qquad\langle S^{\prime},\theta\rangle=\langle S_{t},\theta\rangle,\qquad|q_{t}|-|(S_{t})_{p_{t}}|=-1,\qquad|q_{t}-(S_{t})_{p_{t}}|=1.

Therefore, using (
8.31
),
(
8.36
), and (
8.29
),

(8.58)

\displaystyle f_{\theta,\lambda}(S_{t},p_{t},q_{t})

\displaystyle\geq C_{\rm Signa\ell}\frac{\lambda}{k^{r/2}}(\langle S_{t},\theta\rangle)^{r-1}(q_{t}-(S_{t})_{p_{t}})\theta_{p_{t}}=0,

(8.59)

\displaystyle h_{\gamma}(S_{t},p_{t},q_{t})

\displaystyle\leq-C_{\rm Reg}\gamma\|S_{t}\|_{0}^{(r-1)/2},

(8.60)

\displaystyle\text{and, }\quad g_{W}(S_{t},p_{t},q_{t})

\displaystyle\geq-C^{*}_{n}C_{\rm Noise}\|S_{t}\|_{0}^{(r-1)/2}.

Thus,
D_{t}
is bounded below by,

(8.61)

D_{t}\geq-C^{*}_{n}C_{\rm Noise}\|S_{t}\|_{0}^{(r-1)/2}+C_{\rm Reg}\gamma\|S_{t}\|_{0}^{(r-1)/2}>0,\text{ { as } }\gamma>C^{*}_{n}C_{\rm Noise}/C_{\rm Reg}.

(ii)

If
(\theta_{p_{t}},(S_{t})_{p_{t}},q_{t})=(-1,1,-1)
or

(1,-1,1)
, we have that

(8.62)

(q_{t}-(S_{t})_{p_{t}})\theta_{p_{t}}=2,\quad\langle S_{t},\theta\rangle+2=\langle S^{\prime},\theta\rangle,\quad|q_{t}|-|(S_{t})_{p_{t}}|=0,\quad|q_{t}-(S_{t})_{p_{t}}|=2.

Therefore, using
(
8.31
) and (
8.29
),

(8.63)

\displaystyle f_{\theta,\lambda}(S_{t},p_{t},q_{t})

\displaystyle\geq 2C_{\rm Signa\ell}\frac{\lambda}{k^{r/2}}(\langle S_{t},\theta\rangle-2)^{r-1},

(8.64)

\displaystyle\text{and, }\quad g_{W}(S_{t},p_{t},q_{t})

\displaystyle\geq-2C^{*}_{n}C_{\rm Noise}\|S_{t}\|_{0}^{(r-1)/2}.

Thus,
D_{t}
is bounded below by,

(8.65)

\displaystyle D_{t}

\displaystyle\geq 2\left(\frac{\lambda}{k^{r/2}}(\langle S_{t},\theta\rangle-2)^{r-1}-C^{*}_{n}C_{\rm Noise}\|S_{t}\|_{0}^{(r-1)/2}\right)>0,

(8.66)

\displaystyle\text{ { as }
}\frac{\lambda}{k^{r/2}}(\langle S_{t},\theta\rangle-2)^{r-1}>C^{*}_{n}C_{\rm Noise}\|S_{t}\|_{0}^{(r-1)/2}/C_{\rm Signa\ell}.

(iii)

If
(\theta_{p_{t}},(S_{t})_{p_{t}},q_{t})=(1,0,1)
or

(-1,0,-1)
we have that

(8.67)

(q_{t}-(S_{t})_{p_{t}})\theta_{p_{t}}=1,\quad\langle S_{t},\theta\rangle+1=\langle S^{\prime},\theta\rangle,\quad|q_{t}|-|(S_{t})_{p_{t}}|=1,\quad|q_{t}-(S_{t})_{p_{t}}|=1.

Therefore, using
(
8.31
), (
8.36
), and (
8.29
),

(8.68)

\displaystyle f_{\theta,\lambda}(S_{t},p_{t},q_{t})

\displaystyle\geq C_{\rm Signa\ell}\frac{\lambda}{k^{r/2}}\langle S_{t},\theta\rangle^{r-1},

(8.69)

\displaystyle g_{W}(S_{t},p_{t},q_{t})

\displaystyle\geq-C^{*}_{n}C_{\rm Noise}\|S_{t}\|_{0}^{(r-1)/2},

(8.70)

\displaystyle\text{and, }\quad h_{\gamma}(S_{t},p_{t},q_{t})

\displaystyle\leq-C_{\rm Reg}(\gamma\|S_{t}\|_{0}+1)^{(r-1)/2}.

Thus,
D_{t}
is bounded below by,

(8.71)

\displaystyle D_{t}

\displaystyle\geq C_{\rm Aigna\ell}\frac{\lambda}{k^{r/2}}\langle S_{t},\theta\rangle^{r-1}-C^{*}_{n}C_{\rm Noise}\|S_{t}\|_{0}^{(r-1)/2}+C_{\rm Reg}\gamma(\|S_{t}\|_{0}+1)^{(r-1)/2}>0,

(8.72)

\displaystyle\text{ { as }
}\frac{\lambda}{k^{r/2}}\langle S_{t},\theta\rangle^{r-1}>(C^{*}_{n}C_{\rm Noise}\|S_{t}\|_{0}^{(r-1)/2}+C_{\rm Reg}\gamma(\|S_{t}\|_{0}+1)^{(r-1)/2})/C_{\rm Signa\ell}.

Under the conditioned event
\max_{t\in[nM]}|Z_{t}|\leq C^{*}_{n}
, statement (1) from Lemma
8.4
(at time
t
) involves proposals where
\theta_{p_{t}}=(S_{t})_{p_{t}}
and
q_{t}\neq(S_{t})_{p_{t}}
. This is the exact set of transitions analyzed in case (a) where we proved any proposal in this set will have
D_{t}<0
. Moreover, statement (2) involves proposals where
\theta_{p_{t}}\neq(S_{t})_{p_{t}}
and
q_{t}=\theta_{p_{t}}
. Again, this is the exact set of transition analyzed in case (b) where we proved any proposal in this set will have
D_{t}>0
. As the conditioned event has probability
1-o(1)
uniformly over
t\in[T^{*}_{M}]
, we can remove the conditioning at time
t
to prove statements (1) and (2) hold with high probability uniformly over
t\in[T^{*}_{M}]\subseteq[nM]
. This partially confirms the inductive claim. It remains to show that
\langle S_{t+1},\theta\rangle\geq 2
and the inequality (
8.43
) for
t+1
for the case (b) that leads to acceptance. We shortly complete this step below, with a unified argument across steps, after we prove which cases are accepted or rejected in Step 3 as well.

Step 3.

Although we have proven the inductive claim on statements (1) and (2) at time
t
in Lemma
8.4
with respect to cases (a) and (b), there remains four possible transitions left to analyze to complete the inductive claim for all possible transitions. Each are given in Table
2
.

\theta_{p_{t}}=-1

\theta_{p_{t}}=1

(S_{t})_{p_{t}}

q_{t}

Case

Table 2.
Transitions where
\langle S^{\prime},\theta\rangle\neq\langle S_{t},\theta\rangle
or
\|S^{\prime}\|_{0}\neq\|S_{t}\|_{0}
but have not been analyzed by cases (a) or (b). These transitions are required to finish proving the inductive claim of the inductive argument, but are not directly relevant to the conclusion of Lemma
8.4
. The purpose of each column is identical to Table
1
.

(c)

If
(\theta_{p_{t}},(S_{t})_{p_{t}},q_{t})=(-1,0,1)
or
(1,0,-1)
, then

(8.73)

(q_{t}-(S_{t})_{p_{t}})\theta_{p_{t}}=-1,\quad\langle S_{t},\theta\rangle-2=\langle S^{\prime},\theta\rangle,\quad|q_{t}|-|(S_{t})_{p_{t}}|=1,\quad|q_{t}-(S_{t})_{p_{t}}|=1.

Therefore, using (
8.34
), (
8.29
) (
8.35
),

(8.74)

\displaystyle f_{\theta,\lambda}(S_{t},p_{t},q_{t})

\displaystyle\leq-C_{\rm Signa\ell}\frac{\lambda}{k^{r/2}}\left(\langle S_{t},\theta\rangle-2\right)^{r-1},

(8.75)

\displaystyle g_{W}(S_{t},p_{t},q_{t})

\displaystyle\leq C^{*}_{n}C_{\rm Noise}\|S_{t}\|_{0}^{(r-1)/2},

(8.76)

\displaystyle\text{and, }\quad h_{\gamma}(S_{t},p_{t},q_{t})

\displaystyle\leq-C_{\rm Reg}\gamma\|S_{t}\|_{0}^{(r-1)/2}.

Thus,
D_{t}
is bounded above by,

(8.77)

\displaystyle D_{t}

\displaystyle\leq-C_{\rm Signa\ell}\frac{\lambda}{k^{r/2}}\left(\langle S_{t},\theta\rangle-2\right)^{r-1}+C^{*}_{n}C_{\rm Noise}\|S_{t}\|_{0}^{(r-1)/2}-C_{\rm Reg}\gamma\|S_{t}\|_{0}^{(r-1)/2}<0,

(8.78)

\displaystyle\text{ { as } }\gamma>C^{*}_{n}C_{\rm Noise}/C_{\rm Reg}.

(d)

If
(\theta_{p_{t}},(S_{t})_{p_{t}},q_{t})=(1,-1,0)
or
(-1,1,0)
, then

(8.79)

(q_{t}-(S_{t})_{p_{t}})\theta_{p_{t}}=1,\quad\hskip-2.84544pt\langle S_{t},\theta\rangle+2=\langle S^{\prime},\theta\rangle,\quad|q_{t}|-|(S_{t})_{p_{t}}|=-1,\quad|q_{t}-(S_{t})_{p_{t}}|=1.

Therefore, using (
8.34
), (
8.29
) (
8.35
),

(8.80)

\displaystyle f_{\theta,\lambda}(S_{t},p_{t},q_{t})

\displaystyle\geq C_{\rm Signa\ell}\frac{\lambda}{k^{r/2}}\left(\langle S_{t},\theta\rangle+2\right)^{r-1},

(8.81)

\displaystyle g_{W}(S_{t},p_{t},q_{t})

\displaystyle\geq-C^{*}_{n}C_{\rm Noise}\|S_{t}\|_{0}^{(r-1)/2},

(8.82)

\displaystyle\text{and, }\quad h_{\gamma}(S_{t},p_{t},q_{t})

\displaystyle\geq C_{\rm Reg}\gamma\|S_{t}\|_{0}^{(r-1)/2}.

Thus,
D_{t}
is bounded above by,

(8.83)

\displaystyle D_{t}

\displaystyle\geq C_{\rm Signa\ell}\frac{\lambda}{k^{r/2}}\left(\langle S_{t},\theta\rangle+2\right)^{r-1}-C^{*}_{n}C_{\rm Noise}\|S_{t}\|_{0}^{(r-1)/2}+C_{\rm Reg}\gamma\|S_{t}\|_{0}^{(r-1)/2}>0

(8.84)

\displaystyle\text{ { as }
}C_{\rm Signa\ell}\frac{\lambda}{k^{r/2}}\left(\langle S_{t},\theta\rangle+2\right)^{r-1}+C_{\rm Reg}\gamma(\|S_{t}\|_{0})^{(r-1)/2}>C^{*}_{n}C_{\rm Noise}\|S_{t}\|_{0}^{(r-1)/2}.

This completes the analysis of all possible transitions.

Step 4
. We now prove the inductive statement, that is, we have shown that statements (1) and (2) hold for time
t
and the inequalities (
8.42
) and (
8.43
) hold for time
t+1
. Notice first that for the second part of the induction in Step 2 we proved in (a) that for any
p_{t},q_{t}
such that
\theta_{p_{t}}=(S_{t})_{p_{t}}
and

q_{t}\neq\theta_{p_{t}}
,
D_{t}<0
, leading to rejection.
Additionally, in (b) we proved that for and
p_{t},q_{t}
such that
\theta_{p_{t}}\neq(S_{t})_{p_{t}}
and

q_{t}=\theta_{p_{t}}
,
D_{t}>0
, leading to acceptance. Combining these two we complete the proof of the inductive claim for the statements in (1) and (2). We now can complete the induction by proving that inequalities (
8.42
) and (
8.43
) holds for
t+1
by considering the set of transitions that are accepted (i.e., the cases (b) and
(d)) and proving they lead to an
S_{t+1}
that satisfies (
8.42
) and (
8.43
).

First, it always holds
\langle S_{t+1},\theta\rangle\geq\langle S_{t},\theta\rangle\geq 2
as in both (b), (d) cases it holds
\langle S_{t+1},\theta\rangle\geq\langle S_{t},\theta\rangle,
.

In terms of (
8.43
), the cases (b)(i), (b)(ii) and (d) this is trivial since

(8.85)

\left(\frac{\langle S_{t},\theta\rangle-1}{k^{1/2}(\|S_{t}\|_{0}+2)^{1/2}}\right)^{r-1}

is an increasing function of
\langle S_{t},\theta\rangle
and a decreasing function of
\|S_{t}\|_{0}
.
For case (b)(iii) where
S^{\prime}=S_{t+1}
and it holds
\langle S^{\prime},\theta\rangle=\langle S_{t},\theta\rangle+1
and
\|S^{\prime}\|_{0}=\|S_{t}\|_{0}+1
, a sufficient condition to prove the inductive claim for (
8.43
) is,

(8.86)

\displaystyle\left(\frac{\langle S_{t},\theta\rangle-1}{k^{1/2}(\|S_{t}\|_{0}+2)^{1/2}}\right)^{r-1}

\displaystyle\geq\left(\frac{\langle S_{t},\theta\rangle-2}{k^{1/2}(\|S_{t}\|_{0}+1)^{1/2}}\right)^{r-1}.

Or, equivalently,

(8.87)

\displaystyle\frac{\langle S_{t},\theta\rangle-1}{(\|S_{t}\|_{0}+2)^{1/2}}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\langle S_{t},\theta\rangle-2}{(\|S_{t}\|_{0}+1)^{1/2}},

which we can rewrite as,

(8.88)

\displaystyle 1+\frac{1}{\langle S_{t},\theta\rangle-1}

\displaystyle\geq\left(1+\frac{1}{\|S_{t}\|_{0}}\right)^{1/2}.

And thus, a sufficient condition for the above displayed inequality to hold is,

(8.89)

\|S_{t}\|_{0}\geq\langle S_{t},\theta\rangle-1

which is trivial.

Thus, the inductive argument is complete and the statement of the lemma holds for all
t\in[T^{*}_{M}]
.
∎

8.5.2.
Proof of Lemma
8.7

Proof of Lemma
8.7
.

Recall from Definition

2.6
, when
r
is odd, the homotopy initialization
S_{\rm HOM}
is
defined as

(8.90)

(S_{\rm HOM})_{i}=2\cdot 1\!\!1\left\{\sum_{j_{1},\dots,j_{(r-1)/2}=1}^{n}Y_{i,j_{1},j_{1},\dots,j_{(r-1)/2},j_{(r-1)/2}}\geq 0\right\}-1.

Obviously, we see that
(S_{\rm HOM})_{i}\in\{-1,1\}
and therefore

\|S_{\rm HOM}\|_{0}=n
. Using the definition of
Y
from (
1.1
),
we have for each
i\in[n]
that

(8.91)

\sum_{j_{1},\dots,j_{(r-1)/2}=1}^{n}Y_{i,j_{1},j_{1},\dots,j_{(r-1)/2},j_{(r-1)/2}}\overset{{\mathcal{L}}}{=}\theta_{i}\frac{\lambda}{k^{r/2}}k^{(r-1)/2}+n^{(r-1)/4}Z,

where
Z\sim{\mathcal{N}}(0,1)
. Thus, as the elements of
Y
are independent, we
have that
(S_{\rm HOM})_{i}=\theta_{i}
, for
i\in[n]
where
\theta_{i}\neq 0
, with probability

(8.92)

p:={\mathbb{P}}\left(\frac{\lambda}{\sqrt{k}n^{(r-1)/4}}+Z>0\right).

Moreover, by this independence, we write

(8.93)

\langle S_{\rm HOM},\theta\rangle\overset{{\mathcal{L}}}{=}\sum_{i\in[k]}R_{i},

where
each
R_{i}
is independent and has the law,

(8.94)

R_{i}\sim\begin{cases}1&\qquad\text{with probability }p\\
-1&\qquad\text{with probability }1-p\end{cases}.

Clearly this summation is
stochastically increasing as
p
increases (and thus as
\lambda
increases).
Thus, we
stochastically lower bound
\langle S_{\rm HOM},\theta\rangle
by choosing
considering a smaller
\mathring{\lambda}=n^{r/4}
where
\lambda\geq\mathring{\lambda}
by Assumption
8.6
for large enough
C_{\tau}
.

Substituting in
\mathring{\lambda}
, we have
\mathring{p}\leq p
where

(8.95)

\mathring{p}={\mathbb{P}}\left(\frac{\mathring{\lambda}}{\sqrt{k}n^{(r-1)/4}}+Z>0\right)={\mathbb{P}}\left(\frac{n^{1/4}}{k^{1/2}}+Z>0\right)\geq\frac{1}{2}+\frac{3}{10}\frac{n^{1/4}}{k^{1/2}}.

The final inequality above is due to
\Phi(x)
being concave on
x\in[0,+\infty)
,
therefore for any
x\leq C
, we have that
\Phi(x)\geq 1/2+(\Phi(C)-1/2)x/C
,
choosing
C=1
and recognizing that
\Phi(1)\geq 8/10
gives the inequality.

Therefore, each
R_{i}
is stochastically
lower bounded by a random variable
R^{\prime}_{i}
where we have replaced
p
with
1/2+\frac{3}{10}\frac{n^{1/4}}{\sqrt{k}}
in the definition of
R_{i}
. The
summation
\sum_{i=1}^{k}R^{\prime}_{i}
has expectation

\frac{3}{5}\sqrt{k}n^{1/4}
(as there are
k
terms in the
sum). Then, using that each
R_{i}\in[-1,1]
are independent, Hoeffding’s
inequality then gives that,

(8.96)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k}R^{\prime}_{i}\leq\sqrt{k}n^{1/4}/5\right)

\displaystyle={\mathbb{P}}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k}R^{\prime}_{i}-\frac{3}{5}\frac{\lambda\sqrt{k}}{n^{(r-1)/4}}\leq-\frac{2}{5}\sqrt{k}n^{1/4}\right)

(8.97)

\displaystyle\leq e^{-c\left(\frac{2}{5}\sqrt{k}n^{1/4}\right)^{2}/k}

for some
c>0
. The above is
1-o(1)
for large enough
n
. Using
the previously justified
\sum_{i=1}^{k}R_{i}\preceq_{\rm st}\sum_{i=1}^{k}R_{i}\overset{{\mathcal{L}}}{=}\langle S_{\rm HOM},\theta\rangle
, then the conclusion of the statement follows.
∎

8.5.3.
Proofs of Lemma
8.8
and Lemma

8.9

In order to prove Lemma
8.8
and Lemma
8.9
, we
required the following auxiliary lemma, whose proof is deferred to Section

9.2
.

Lemma 8.14
.

Recall
M_{1}=\lceil\log^{4}(n)\rceil
, sequence of proposals
(p_{t})_{t\in[nM_{1}]}
, and random runtime

T^{*}((p_{t})_{t\in[nM_{1}]})=T^{*}
from Algorithm

15
.

Then, almost surely over
T^{*}
, with
D^{\prime}_{t}
from (
8.2
), it holds that

(8.98)

(D^{\prime}_{t})_{t\in[T^{*}]}\overset{{\mathcal{L}}}{=}\left(\frac{f_{\theta,\lambda}(S_{t},p_{t},-(S_{t}){p_{t}})}{V_{n}\sqrt{M_{1}}}+Z_{t}\right)_{t\in[T^{*}]}

where each
Z_{t}
is an independent standard normal random
variables independent of
(p_{t})_{t\in T^{*}}
and thus
T^{*}
. As
a consequence, the event that
\{D^{\prime}_{t}>0\}
for
t\in[T^{*}]
is
equivalent to the event that

(8.99)

\left\{\frac{f_{\theta,\lambda}(S_{t},p_{t},-(S_{t})_{p_{t}})}{V_{n}\sqrt{M_{1}}}+Z_{t}>0\right\}.

A proof sketch for Lemmas
8.8
and
8.9
Due to the technical complexity of our proofs to Lemmas
8.8
and Lemma
8.9
, we provide a high level sketch of the arguments for the reader’s convenience.

(1)

Calculating the probabilities of each one-step change of the “overlap”
\langle S_{t},\theta\rangle
. Recalling
D^{\prime}_{t}
is the difference of the Hamiltonian plus injected noise in Algorithm
15
, we use Lemma
8.14
to find a convenient equivalence in law for the event
D^{\prime}_{t}>0
with respect to the random variables
(p_{t})_{t\in[T^{*}]}
and auxiliary Gaussian random variables
(Z_{t})_{t\in[T^{*}]}
.
This permits an exact calculation of the probability that
\langle S_{t},\theta\rangle
will either increase or decrease by two for each time step
t
.

(2)

Modeling the overlap as a stochastic process
A_{t}
.
Due to the exact form of these probabilities, we can model the dynamics of
\langle S_{t},\theta\rangle
in Algorithm
15
by a stochastic process
A_{t}
. The process has position-dependent transition probabilities (i.e., dependent on the current value of
\langle S_{t},\theta\rangle
) and hence requires a less standard analysis. Interestingly, we prove that it’s evolution undergoes two phases: an oscillatory phase until it reaches a specific level of overlap, and a second phase happening above this critical level where it switches to a monotonic evolution.

(3)

Deriving the total runtime to reach a high enough
\langle S_{t},\theta\rangle
.
Due to the above inhomogenity of
\langle S_{t},\theta\rangle
(which does not allow us to invoke standard random walk results), we calculate the needed runtime for the stochastic process to either double or half the
\langle S_{t},\theta\rangle
for each region up to the threshold where
\langle S_{t},\theta\rangle
satisfies the inequality (
8.4
), a bound dependent on the maximal value of any
|Z_{t}|
. This is the “oscillatory” phase. For each region, the stochastic process behaves similarly to a biased random walk (where the bias changes per region), which allows us to use standard results on each region.

(4)

Confirming
\langle S_{t},\theta\rangle
is non-decreasing above a specific threshold (
8.4
).
Once
\langle S_{t},\theta\rangle
passes a specific threshold, the dynamics switch to a deterministic increase. To prove this, we utilize techniques similar to proving Lemma
8.4
to show that beyond that threshold, with high probability over the sequence
(Z_{t})_{t\in[T^{*}]}
, we always reject transitions that decreases the overlap and always accept transitions that increase the overlap. Note (4) is only used in the proof of Lemma
8.8
.

Proof of Lemma
8.8
.

We translate the statement to be proved into a statement about an time dependent
stochastic process on the integers which is later stochastically lower bounded
by a family of random walks.

Step 1. Calculating the probability of changes for
\langle S_{t},\theta\rangle
.

Let
s=\langle S_{t},\theta\rangle
; note, this overlap may either
increase/decrease by two or remain the same. We then calculate the probability
that
\langle S_{t+1},\theta\rangle=s+2
or
s-2
. For example,
s
will
increase by two if, given the sampled
p_{t}
, we have
(S_{t})_{p_{t}}\neq\theta_{p_{t}},\theta_{p_{t}}\neq 0
, fixed
q_{t}=-(S_{t})_{p_{t}}
and
D^{\prime}_{t}>0
(meaning we accept the transition).
The
probability of sampling such a
p_{t}
given
s
is calculated as
\left(\frac{k}{2n}-\frac{s}{2n}\right)
. Moreover, using Lemma
8.14

and recalling
V_{n}
from Corollary
8.12
, we have almost surely over

T^{*}
, that
\{D^{\prime}_{t}>0\}
is equivalent to the event that

(8.100)

\left\{\frac{f_{\theta,\lambda}(S_{t},p_{t},-(S_{t})_{p_{t}})}{V_{n}\sqrt{M_{1}}}+Z_{t}>0\right\},

where each
Z_{t}
is an independent
standard normal random variable independent of both
(p_{t})_{t\in[nM_{1}]}
and
T^{*}
. Such an event has probability

\Phi([f_{\theta,\lambda}(S_{t},p_{t},-(S_{t})_{p_{t}})/(V_{n}\sqrt{M_{1}})]s^{r-1})
where
\Phi
is the Gaussian CDF. As the sample

p_{t}
is independent of
Z_{t}
, the product of these two events gives that

\langle S_{t+1},\theta\rangle=s+2
has probability

(8.101)

\left(\frac{k}{2n}-\frac{s}{2n}\right)\Phi\left(\frac{f_{\theta,\lambda}(S_{t},p_{t},-(S_{t})_{p_{t}})}{V_{n}\sqrt{M_{1}}}\right),

for any

S_{t}
where,
\langle S_{t},\theta\rangle=s
and
p_{t}
such that

\theta_{p_{t}}\neq 0
,
(S_{t})_{p_{t}}\neq\theta_{p_{t}}
and fixed
q_{t}=-(S_{t})_{p_{t}}
. Using

(8.102)

f_{\theta,\lambda}(S_{t},p_{t},-(S_{t})_{p_{t}})=\frac{\lambda}{k^{r/2}}\left(\langle(S_{t}-2(S_{t})_{p_{t}}e_{p_{t}})^{\otimes r},\,\theta^{\otimes r}\rangle-\langle(S_{t})^{\otimes r},\,\theta^{\otimes r}\rangle\right),

we can decompose this value further with

(8.103)

\displaystyle\langle(S_{t}-2(S_{t})_{p_{t}}e_{p_{t}})^{\otimes r},\,\theta^{\otimes r}\rangle-\langle(S_{t})^{\otimes r},\,\theta^{\otimes r}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle S_{t}-2(S_{t})_{p_{t}}e_{p_{t}},\theta\rangle^{r}-\langle S_{t},\theta\rangle^{r}

(8.104)

\displaystyle=\Bigl(\langle S_{t},\,\theta\rangle\;-\;2\,(S_{t})_{p_{t}}\,\langle e_{p_{t}},\,\theta\rangle\Bigr)^{r}\;-\;\langle S_{t},\,\theta\rangle^{r}

(8.105)

\displaystyle=\Bigl(\langle S_{t},\,\theta\rangle-2\,(S_{t})_{p_{t}}\,\theta_{p_{t}}\Bigr)^{r}-\langle S_{t},\,\theta\rangle^{r}

(8.106)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{r}\binom{r}{k}\,\langle S_{t},\,\theta\rangle^{\,r-k}\,\bigl(-2\,(S_{t})_{p_{t}}\,\theta_{p_{t}}\bigr)^{k}.

Under our previously mentioned constraints, we then conclude that
\langle S_{t},\theta\rangle=s+2
has probability

(8.107)

\left(\frac{k}{2n}-\frac{s}{2n}\right)\Phi\left(\frac{\tilde{f}_{\theta,\lambda}(s,-1)}{V_{n}\sqrt{M_{1}}}\right),

where

\tilde{f}_{\theta,\lambda}(s,s^{\prime}):=\frac{\lambda}{k^{r/2}}\sum_{k=1}^{r}\binom{r}{k}\,s^{\,r-k}\,(-2s^{\prime})^{k}
.
Symmetry implies that

\langle S_{t+1},\theta\rangle=s-2
has probability

(8.108)

\left(\frac{k}{2n}+\frac{s}{2n}\right)\Phi\left(\frac{\tilde{f}_{\theta,\lambda}(s,1)}{V_{n}\sqrt{M_{1}}}\right).

Note, we still have the following bound, due to Corollary
8.11
(d), when
s\geq 2
,

(8.109)

-2C_{\rm Signa\ell}\frac{\lambda}{k^{r/2}}s^{r-1}\leq\tilde{f}_{\theta,\lambda}(s,1)\leq-2C_{\rm Signa\ell}\frac{\lambda}{k^{r/2}}(s-2)^{r-1},

and,

(8.110)

2C_{\rm Signa\ell}\frac{\lambda}{k^{r/2}}s^{r-1}\leq\tilde{f}_{\theta,\lambda}(s,-1)\leq 2C_{\rm Signa\ell}\frac{\lambda}{k^{r/2}}(s+2)^{r-1}.

Step 2. Modeling the overlap as a stochastic process
A_{t}
.

Further, Lemma
8.14
and the above two probabilities gives that

\langle S_{t},\theta\rangle
(where
(S_{t})t
are the iterates of Algorithm
15
) is equivalent in law
to the following stochastic process
A_{t}
almost surely over
T^{*}
:

Definition 8.15

(Stochastic Process
A_{t}
tracking the dynamics of
\langle S_{t},\theta\rangle
in Algorithm
15
)
.

Let
A_{1}=\langle S_{1},\theta\rangle
, then for
t\geq 1
, we randomly
select one of the following actions:

(1)

With probability
\frac{k}{2n}+\frac{A_{t}}{2n}
we do the following:
With probability
\Phi\left(\frac{\tilde{f}_{\theta,\lambda}(A_{t},1)}{V_{n}\sqrt{M_{1}}}\right)
we set
A_{t+1}=A_{t}-2
, otherwise set
A_{t+1}=A_{t}
.

(2)

With probability
\frac{k}{2n}-\frac{A_{t}}{2n}
we do the following:
With probability
\Phi\left(\frac{\tilde{f}_{\theta,\lambda}(A_{t},-1)}{V_{n}\sqrt{M_{1}}}\right)
we set
A_{t+1}=A_{t}+2
, otherwise set
A_{t+1}=A_{t}
.

(3)

With the remaining probability, set
A_{t+1}=A_{t}
.

As each transition in the above process can change
A_{t}
to either
A_{t}-2
,
A_{t}+2
or remain the same, therefore special care is taken with respect to the parity
of
A_{1}
. Indeed, the parity of the initialization dictates the
support of
A_{t}
. In what follows, denote
\wp=\wp(x)
, for
x\in{\mathbb{N}}
, as the function,

(8.111)

\wp(x)=\begin{cases}1&x\text{ is odd}\\
0&x\text{ is even}\end{cases}.

If no argument is given, we assume

\wp=\wp(A_{1})
.

Recall the statement of Lemma
8.8
, henceforth denoted as event

{\mathcal{B}}
,

“If
S_{1}
has
\langle S_{1},\theta\rangle\geq n^{1/4}k^{1/2}/5
,
there exists time step
t^{*}\leq\lfloor T^{*}/2\rfloor
in Algorithm
15
(with input
(Y,S_{1},\gamma,M_{2})
) such that

(8.112)

\frac{2C_{\rm Signa\ell}\frac{\lambda}{k^{r/2}}}{V_{n}\sqrt{M_{2}}}(\langle S_{t^{*}},\theta\rangle-2)^{r-1}\geq 2\sqrt{\log(M_{2}n)}\;\text{''}

Let
s^{*}=s^{*}(n,k,\lambda)
be the minimal positive value of
\langle S_{t},\theta\rangle
for which

(8.113)

\frac{2C_{\rm Signa\ell}\frac{\lambda}{k^{r/2}}}{V_{n}\sqrt{M_{1}}}(\langle S_{t},\theta\rangle-2)^{r-1}\geq 2\sqrt{\log(M_{1}n)}

holds
and
\wp(s^{*})=\wp
. Now, define
T=\inf\{t\in{\mathbb{N}}:A_{t}\in\{s^{*},\wp\}\}
and the event
{\mathcal{A}}
where
T\leq\lfloor T^{*}/2\rfloor
and
A_{T}=s^{*}
. If
{\mathcal{A}}
holds, then so does
{\mathcal{B}}
; taking
probabilities,
{\mathbb{P}}({\mathcal{A}})\leq{\mathbb{P}}({\mathcal{B}})
.
Further, the event
{\mathcal{A}}
is of equal probability if the states
\wp\in\{0,1\}
and
s^{*}
are absorbing as event
{\mathcal{A}}
is only with respect to the stochastic process
A_{t}
up to the time
T
where
A_{T}
is in one of these absorbing states. Hence, we restrict the support of the stochastic process
A_{t}
to the set
[\wp,s^{*}]\cap{\mathbb{Z}}
. This allows us to neglect the
natural support of the process
A_{t}
in
\pm[k]\cup 0
and instead
consider the support to be this non-negative set. Thus, the proof of Lemma

8.8
is reduced to proving
{\mathbb{P}}({\mathcal{A}})=1-o(1)
.

Next, cover the set
\{i\in[s^{*}]:i\geq 2\lceil\langle S_{1},\theta\rangle/4\rceil+\wp\}
by
m
sets
6
6

6

We see momentarily that
m=O(\log(n))

{\mathcal{I}}_{1},\dots,{\mathcal{I}}_{m}
defined by

(8.114)

a_{i}=2\lceil 3a_{i-1}/4\rceil\text{ for }(i-1)\in[m-1],a_{1}=\langle S_{1},\theta\rangle

where

(8.115)

{\mathcal{I}}_{j}=\{i\in[s^{*}]:2\lceil a_{j}/4\rceil+\wp\leq i\leq 2\lceil 3a_{j}/4\rceil+\wp\}.

Note, for every element
s\in{\mathcal{I}}_{j}
which is
non-empty, we have that
s\geq n^{1/4}k^{1/2}/10\geq 2
for sufficiently large

n
(this is relevant at the end of the proof were we show that
\langle S_{t^{*}},\theta\rangle\geq 2
). Therefore, we are free to invoke the bounds from (
8.109
) and
(
8.110
). Moreover, every endpoint
x\in\{\partial{\mathcal{I}}_{1},\dots,\partial{\mathcal{I}}_{m}\}
has
\wp(x)=\wp
; and thus,
A_{t}
can equal
x
with
non-zero probability. Additionally, define a sequence of deterministic times
\{T_{1},\dots,T_{m}\}
, where each
T_{i}=10n\log^{2}(n)
(as we will see later in the
proof,
T_{i}
will serve as an upper bound on the time for process
A_{t}
to transition from

\partial{\mathcal{I}}_{i}
to
\partial{\mathcal{I}}_{i+1}
). As
s^{*}\leq n
(trivially),
m

can be bounded above than the solution to
\left(3/2\right)^{m}a_{1}=n
, and as
a_{1}\geq 2
for large
n
, then
m=O(\log(n))
. Notably,
{\mathcal{I}}_{i}
for larger
i

may be the empty set; for such
i
we set
T_{i}=0
.

For each
i\in[m]
, define the event
{\mathcal{E}}_{i}
, in which stochastic process

A_{t}
, initialized at
A_{1}=a_{i}
from (
8.114
), has
A_{T^{\prime}_{i}}=2\lceil 3a_{i}/4\rceil+\wp
and
T^{\prime}_{i}\leq T_{i}
, where we define
T^{\prime}_{i}=\inf\{t\in{\mathbb{N}}:A_{t}\in\partial{\mathcal{I}}_{i}\}
. If
{\mathcal{I}}_{i}=\varnothing
, then vacuously set

{\mathbb{P}}({\mathcal{E}}_{i})=1
. Further, let
{\mathcal{E}}=\bigcap_{i=1}^{m}{\mathcal{E}}_{i}
. Notice that under
{\mathcal{E}}(\omega)
,
the following holds. By
{\mathcal{E}}_{1}
we have that
A_{t}
will hit
2\lceil 3a_{1}/4\rceil+\wp
before
2\lceil a_{1}/4\rceil+\wp
within time
T_{1}
. Moreover,
event
{\mathcal{E}}_{2}
implies that once
A_{t}
hits
2\lceil 3a_{1}/4\rceil+\wp=a_{2}
then the process
A_{t}
will then hit
2\lceil 3a_{2}/4\rceil+\wp

(notice this term has
a_{2}
instead of
a_{1}
) before
2\lceil a_{2}/4\rceil+\wp
within time
T_{2}
. Therefore, recalling
m=O(\log(n))
, the event
{\mathcal{E}}(\omega)\cap\{10n\log^{3.5}(n)\leq\lceil T^{*}/2\rceil\}
(as
\sum_{i=1}^{m}T^{\prime}_{i}\leq 10n\log^{2}(n)m\leq 10n\log^{3.5}(n))
for large
n
) implies the event
{\mathcal{A}}(\omega)
holds. Hence, taking probabilities

(8.116)

{\mathbb{P}}({\mathcal{E}}\cap\{10n\log^{3.5}(n)\leq\lceil T^{*}/2\rceil\})\leq{\mathbb{P}}({\mathcal{A}})\leq{\mathbb{P}}({\mathcal{B}}).

Therefore, if we prove
{\mathbb{P}}({\mathcal{E}}\cap\{10n\log^{3.5}(n)\})\geq 1-o(1)
, we complete the proof. Moreover, by a union bound,

(8.117)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}({\mathcal{E}}\cap\{10n\log^{3.5}(n)\leq\lceil T^{*}/2\rceil\})

\displaystyle\geq 1-{\mathbb{P}}({\mathcal{E}}^{c})-{\mathbb{P}}(\{10n\log^{3.5}(n)\leq\lceil T^{*}/2\rceil\}^{c})

(8.118)

\displaystyle={\mathbb{P}}({\mathcal{E}})-{\mathbb{P}}(\{10n\log^{3.5}(n)>\lceil T^{*}/2\rceil\}).

Now, we prove that
{\mathbb{P}}({\mathcal{E}})=1-o(1)
. We then prove that
{\mathbb{P}}(\{10n\log^{3.5}(n)>\lceil T^{*}/2\rceil\})=o(1)
, completing the proof.

The choice of covering set
{\mathcal{I}}_{1},\dots,{\mathcal{I}}_{m}
and the fact that
{\mathcal{E}}
implies

{\mathcal{A}}
is visualized in Figure
6
.

Notice, the statement of
{\mathbb{P}}({\mathcal{E}})\geq 1-o(1)
follows from proving
{\mathbb{P}}({\mathcal{E}}^{c})=o(1)
. Conveniently, it suffices to prove for all
i=1,\ldots,m
that
{\mathbb{P}}({\mathcal{E}}_{i}^{c})=O(\log^{-2}(n))
. Indeed, as
m\leq C\log(n)
for some constant
C>0
. We
could then conclude,

(8.119)

{\mathbb{P}}({\mathcal{E}}^{c})={\mathbb{P}}\left(\bigcup_{i=1}^{m}{\mathcal{E}}_{i}^{c}\right)\leq\sum_{i=1}^{m}{\mathbb{P}}({\mathcal{E}}_{i}^{c})\leq C\log(n)\max_{i\in[m]}{\mathbb{P}}({\mathcal{E}}_{i}^{c})=O(\log^{-1}(n)),

as
desired.

Figure 7.
A cartoon visualization of the covering sets
\{{\mathcal{I}}_{1},\dots,{\mathcal{I}}_{m}\}
which divide the stochastic process into easier to handle “chunks”. We
also see how event
{\mathcal{A}}
, represented abstractly by the red arrow, is
implied by the sequence of events
{\mathcal{E}}=\cup_{i}{\mathcal{E}}_{i}
, each with a blue

will eventually output the absorbing state

with
