steps.
Note for visual simplicity we assume that

\wp=0
.

Fix an
i\in[m]
where
{\mathcal{I}}_{i}\neq\varnothing
for the rest of the proof.
The
probability of event
{\mathcal{E}}_{i}
can be analyzed using the stochastic process
A^{i}_{t}

initialized with
A^{i}_{1}=a_{i}
from (
8.114
), with two absorbing states at

2\lceil a_{i}/4\rceil+\wp
and
2\lceil 3a_{i}/4\rceil+\wp
(i.e. the
elements of
\partial{\mathcal{I}}_{i}
) outputting
A^{i}_{T^{\prime}_{i}}=2\lceil 3a_{i}/4\rceil+\wp
for
T^{\prime}_{i}\leq T_{i}
. The stochastic process
A_{t}^{i}
is formally defined by the
following transition probabilities for
A_{t}^{i}\in{\mathcal{I}}_{i}\setminus\partial{\mathcal{I}}_{i}
:

Definition 8.16

(Stochastic process
A^{i}_{t}
used to calculate
{\mathbb{P}}({\mathcal{E}}_{i})
)
.

Let
A^{i}_{1}=a_{i}
with absorbing barriers at
2\lceil a_{i}/4\rceil+\wp

and
\min(2\lceil 3a_{i}/4\rceil+\wp,s^{*})
. Then, for
t\geq 1
, we
randomly select one of the following actions:

(1)

With probability
\frac{k}{2n}+\frac{A^{i}_{t}}{2n}
, we do the following: With
probability
\Phi\left(\frac{\tilde{f}_{\theta,\lambda}(A^{i}_{t},1)}{V_{n}\sqrt{M_{1}}}\right)
, we set
A^{i}_{t+1}=A^{i}_{t}-2
, otherwise set
A^{i}_{t+1}=A^{i}_{t}
.

(2)

With probability
\frac{k}{2n}-\frac{A^{i}_{t}}{2n}
we do the following: With
probability
\Phi\left(\frac{\tilde{f}_{\theta,\lambda}(A^{i}_{t},-1)}{V_{n}\sqrt{M_{1}}}\right)
we set
A^{i}_{t+1}=A^{i}_{t}+2
, otherwise set

A^{i}_{t+1}=A^{i}_{t}
.

(3)

With the remaining probability, set
A^{i}_{t+1}=A^{i}_{t}
.

Recall,
A_{t}
represents the dynamics of
\langle S^{t},\theta\rangle
and has no absorbing states. We have introduced
A_{t}^{i}
to keep track of the dynamics of
A_{t}
within a given interval
{\mathcal{I}}_{i}
with added absorbing states at
\partial{\mathcal{I}}_{i}
. It is immediate that
A^{i}_{t}
is equivalent in law to
A_{t}
if we initialize
A_{1}=a_{i}
so long as
A_{t}\in{\mathcal{I}}_{i}
.

Step 3(a). Deriving the total runtime to reach a high enough
\langle S_{t},\theta\rangle
.

Observe that if

A^{i}_{t}=s
, the right and left step transition probabilities
p(s)=\left(\frac{k}{2n}-\frac{s}{2n}\right)\Phi\left(\frac{\tilde{f}_{\theta,\lambda}(s,-1)}{V_{n}\sqrt{M_{1}}}\right)
and

q(s)=\left(\frac{k}{2n}+\frac{s}{2n}\right)\Phi\left(\frac{\tilde{f}_{\theta,\lambda}(s,1)}{V_{n}\sqrt{M_{1}}}\right)

both change with the value of
s
. To deal with this “position-dependent” transition probabilities, we construct a
random walk
B^{i}_{t}
, with non-varying transition probabilities, that
stochastically lower bounds the original random walk
A^{i}_{t}
.

Let
B^{i}_{t}
be a random walk where
B^{i}_{1}=A^{i}_{1}
and
B^{i}_{t}
has the
same absorbing states as
A^{i}_{t}
. Moreover, set
{\mathbb{P}}(B^{i}_{t+1}=B^{i}_{t}+2)=\tilde{p}
,
{\mathbb{P}}(B^{i}_{t+1}=B^{i}_{t}-2)=\tilde{q}
, and

{\mathbb{P}}(B^{i}_{t+1}=B^{i}_{t})=1-\tilde{p}-\tilde{q}
for
\tilde{p}
and

\tilde{q}
defined momentarily. Notice that for all
s\in{\mathcal{I}}_{i}\setminus\partial{\mathcal{I}}_{i}
, using (
8.110
) (as we previously showed that
s=\omega(1)
for any
s\in{\mathcal{I}}_{i}
),

(8.120)

\displaystyle p(s)

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{k}{2n}-\frac{s}{2n}\right)\Phi\left(\frac{\tilde{f}_{\theta,\lambda}(s,-1)}{V_{n}\sqrt{M_{1}}}\right)

(8.121)

\displaystyle\geq\left(\frac{k}{2n}-\frac{s}{2n}\right)\Phi\left(2C_{\rm Signa\ell}\frac{\lambda}{k^{r/2}V_{n}\sqrt{M_{1}}}s^{r-1}\right),

(8.122)

\displaystyle\geq\left(\frac{k}{2n}-\frac{s}{2n}\right)\Phi\left(2C_{\rm Signa\ell}\frac{\lambda}{k^{r/2}V_{n}\sqrt{M_{1}}}(s-2)^{r-1}\right)

(as
s\geq 2
for large
n
), and, using
(
8.34
),

(8.123)

\displaystyle q(s)

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{k}{2n}+\frac{s}{2n}\right)\Phi\left(\frac{\tilde{f}_{\theta,\lambda}(s,1)}{V_{n}\sqrt{M_{1}}}\right)

(8.124)

\displaystyle\leq\left(\frac{k}{2n}+\frac{s}{2n}\right)\Phi\left(-2C_{\rm Signa\ell}\frac{\lambda}{k^{r/2}V_{n}\sqrt{M_{1}}}(s-2)^{r-1}\right),

(as this is the acceptance probability for a transition which decreases the overlap). We immediately see that
\frac{k}{2n}{-}\frac{s}{2n}

\left(\text{resp. }\frac{k}{2n}+\frac{s}{2n}\right)
is decreasing (resp. increasing) in
s
, and

\Phi\left(2C_{\rm Signa\ell}\frac{\lambda}{k^{r/2}V_{n}\sqrt{M_{1}}}(s-2)^{r-1}\right)

\left(\text{resp. }\Phi\left(-2C_{\rm Signa\ell}\frac{\lambda}{k^{r/2}V_{n}\sqrt{M_{1}}}(s-2)^{r-1}\right)\right)
is decreasing (resp. increasing) in

s
when
s\geq 2
. Therefore, denoting the minimal and maximal
elements of
{\mathcal{I}}_{i}
as
s_{1}=s_{1}(i)
and
s_{2}=s_{2}(i)
(recalling that the smallest
possible value of
s_{1}
is
2\lceil a_{1}/4\rceil=\omega(1)
which is greater than two for large
n
), we can choose

(8.125)

\displaystyle\tilde{p}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{k}{2n}-\frac{s_{2}}{2n}\right)\Phi\left(2C_{\rm Signa\ell}\frac{\lambda}{k^{r/2}V_{n}\sqrt{M_{1}}}(s_{1}-2)^{r-1}\right)

and

(8.126)

\displaystyle\tilde{q}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{k}{2n}+\frac{s_{2}}{2n}\right)\Phi\left(-2C_{\rm Signa\ell}\frac{\lambda}{k^{r/2}V_{n}\sqrt{M_{1}}}(s_{1}-2)^{r-1}\right).

Further, notice this choice of
\tilde{p}
and
\tilde{q}
has

\tilde{p}+\tilde{q}\leq 1
as
s_{1}\in[k]
, seen by calculating,

(8.127)

\displaystyle\tilde{p}+\tilde{q}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\frac{k}{2n}-\frac{s_{1}}{2n}\right)\Phi\left(2C_{\rm Signa\ell}\frac{\lambda}{k^{r/2}V_{n}\sqrt{M_{1}}}(s_{2}-2)^{r-1}\right)

(8.128)

\displaystyle\qquad+\left(\frac{k}{2n}+\frac{s_{1}}{2n}\right)\Phi\left(-2C_{\rm Signa\ell}\frac{\lambda}{k^{r/2}V_{n}\sqrt{M_{1}}}(s_{2}-2)^{r-1}\right)

(8.129)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{k}{2n}-\frac{s_{1}}{2n}+\frac{k}{2n}+\frac{s_{1}}{2n}=\frac{k}{n}\leq 1.

Two observations become apparent: (1)
\min_{s\in{\mathcal{I}}_{i}\setminus\partial{\mathcal{I}}_{i}}p(s)-\max_{s\in{\mathcal{I}}_{i}\setminus\partial{\mathcal{I}}_{i}}q(s)\geq\tilde{p}-\tilde{q}
. (2) As
\tilde{p}
is uniformly smaller than
p(s)
for all

s\in{\mathcal{I}}_{i}\setminus\partial{\mathcal{I}}_{i}
,
\tilde{q}
is uniformly larger than

q(s)
for all
s\in{\mathcal{I}}_{i}\setminus\partial{\mathcal{I}}_{i}
and
\tilde{p}+\tilde{q}\leq 1
, we have that each step of
B^{i}_{t}(s)
stochastically lower bound the
steps of
A^{i}_{t}(s)
for all
s\in{\mathcal{I}}_{i}\setminus\partial{\mathcal{I}}_{i}
by Lemma

9.5
; further—using Lemma
9.6
—we have that
B^{i}_{t}\preceq_{\rm st}A^{i}_{t}
for all
t\geq 1
.

Recalling the stopping time
T^{\prime}_{i}
, we can equivalently write

(8.130)

T^{\prime}_{i}=\inf\{t\in{\mathbb{N}}:A^{i}_{t}\in\{2\lceil a_{i}/4\rceil+\wp,2\lceil 3a_{i}/4\rceil+\wp\}\}.

Thus, observation (1) combined with Theorem
9.3
and

s_{2}-s_{1}\leq 2k
(as both
k\geq s_{2}\geq s_{1}\geq 0
) gives that

(8.131)

{\mathbb{E}}[T^{\prime}_{i}]\leq\frac{2k}{\tilde{p}-\tilde{q}}.

We then calculate,

(8.132)

\displaystyle\tilde{p}-\tilde{q}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{k}{2n}-\frac{s_{2}}{2n}\right)\Phi\left(2C_{\rm Signa\ell}\frac{\lambda}{k^{r/2}V_{n}\sqrt{M_{1}}}(s_{1}-2)^{r-1}\right)

(8.133)

\displaystyle\qquad-\left(\frac{k}{2n}+\frac{s_{2}}{2n}\right)\Phi\left(-2C_{\rm Signa\ell}\frac{\lambda}{k^{r/2}V_{n}\sqrt{M_{1}}}(s_{1}-2)^{r-1}\right)

(8.134)

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{k}{2n}-\frac{s_{2}}{2n}\right)\Phi\left(2C_{\rm Signa\ell}\frac{\lambda}{k^{r/2}V_{n}\sqrt{M_{1}}}(s_{1}-2)^{r-1}\right)

(8.135)

\displaystyle\qquad+\left(\frac{k}{2n}+\frac{s_{2}}{2n}\right)\left(1-\Phi\left(2C_{\rm Signa\ell}\frac{\lambda}{k^{r/2}V_{n}\sqrt{M_{1}}}(s_{1}-2)^{r-1}\right)\right)

(8.136)

\displaystyle=\frac{k}{2n}+\frac{s_{2}}{2n}\left(1-2\Phi\left(2C_{\rm Signa\ell}\frac{\lambda}{k^{r/2}V_{n}\sqrt{M_{1}}}(s_{1}-2)^{r-1}\right)\right)\geq\frac{1}{2n}(k-s_{2})\geq\frac{k}{5n}.

The penultimate inequality is due to
\Phi(x)\leq 1
for any
x\in{\mathbb{R}}
, and
the last inequality holds for large enough
n
when
s_{2}\leq k/2+2
, proven
by the following argument: Recall that
s_{2}\leq s^{*}
where

(8.137)

2C_{\rm Signa\ell}\frac{\lambda}{k^{r/2}V_{n}\sqrt{M_{1}}}(s^{*}-2)^{r-1}\leq 2\sqrt{\log(M_{1}n)}.

Rearranging this inequality then gives,

(8.138)

(s^{*}-2)^{r-1}\leq\frac{V_{n}\sqrt{M_{1}}}{C_{\rm Signa\ell}}\frac{k^{r/2}}{\lambda}\sqrt{\log(M_{1}n)}.

Now, using
the form of
\lambda=\tau\sqrt{M_{1}}n^{r/4}
, and the bound

V_{n}\leq C_{\rm Noise}n^{(r-1)/2}
from Corollary
8.12
,

(8.139)

(s^{*}-2)^{r-1}\leq\frac{C_{\rm Noise}n^{(r-1)/2}}{C_{\rm Signa\ell}}\frac{k^{r/2}}{\tau n^{r/4}}\sqrt{\log(M_{1}n)}\leq\frac{C_{\rm Noise}}{C_{\rm Signa\ell}}\frac{k^{r/2}n^{(r-2)/4}}{\tau}\sqrt{\log(M_{1}n)}.

Then, as
k=\Omega(\sqrt{n})
this implies that there exists
C_{k}>0
such that
k\geq C_{k}\sqrt{n}
. Substituting this in we get the inequality,

(8.140)

(s^{*}-2)^{r-1}\leq\frac{1}{\tau}\frac{C_{\rm Noise}}{C_{\rm Signa\ell}}C_{k}^{(r-2)/4}k^{r-1}\sqrt{\log(M_{1}n)}

By Assumption

8.6
(b), we have that
\tau\geq C_{\tau}C_{\rm Noise}\sqrt{\log(M_{1}n)}/C_{\rm Signa\ell}
, and taking the
r-1
root and
choosing
C_{\tau}
sufficiently large gives
s_{2}-2\leq s^{*}-2\leq k/13\leq k/2
; rearranging proves the desired inequality for
sufficiently large
n
(as
k=\omega(1)
).

Using (
8.136
) in (
8.131
), we then have that
{\mathbb{E}}[T^{\prime}_{i}]\leq 10n
with
T^{\prime}_{i}
from (
8.130
);
Markov’s inequality then gives that
{\mathbb{P}}(T^{\prime}_{i}>10n\log^{2}(n))\leq\frac{1}{\log(n)^{2}}
.
We can see by a union bound that
{\mathbb{P}}({\mathcal{E}}_{i}^{c})\leq{\mathbb{P}}(T^{\prime}_{i}>T_{i})+{\mathbb{P}}(A^{i}_{T_{i}^{\prime}}=s_{1})
. Thus, we are done after showing that
{\mathbb{P}}(A^{i}_{T_{i}^{\prime}}=s_{2})=1-O(\log^{-2}(n))
as we have just shown that
{\mathbb{P}}(T^{\prime}_{i}>T_{i})=O(\log^{-2}(n))
.

Step 3(b). Stochastically lower bounding
A_{t}
over various regions by fixed probability random walks.

Observation (2) gave that
B^{i}_{t}\preceq_{\rm st}A^{i}_{t}
for all
t\geq 1
, this means
that

(8.141)

{\mathbb{P}}(B^{i}_{t}=s_{2})={\mathbb{P}}(B^{i}_{t}\geq s_{2})\leq{\mathbb{P}}(A^{i}_{t}\geq s_{2})={\mathbb{P}}(A^{i}_{t}=s_{2})

as both
B^{i}_{t}
and
A^{i}_{t}
have absorbing boundaries at the
points
s_{1}
and
s_{2}
. Indeed, the above displayed equation must hold as
B_{t}^{i}
cannot be larger than
s_{2}
and hence
\{B_{t}^{i}=s_{2}\}
holds if and only if the event
\{B_{t}^{i}\geq s_{2}\}
holds; a similar argument holds for
A_{t}^{i}
. Meaning, with
\tilde{T}_{i}=\inf\{t:B^{i}_{t}\in\{s_{1},s_{2}\}\}
, if we prove that
{\mathbb{P}}(B^{i}_{\tilde{T}_{i}}=s_{2})=1-O(\log^{-2}(n))

then
{\mathbb{P}}(A^{i}_{\tilde{T}_{i}}=s_{2})=1-O(\log^{-2}(n))
, further implying that

{\mathbb{P}}(A^{i}_{T_{i}^{\prime}}=s_{2})=1-O(\log^{-2}(n))
. Next, we utilize Lemma

9.7
to analyze
B^{i}_{\tilde{T}_{i}}
. As such, we must certify
each condition of this lemma where (in the language of Lemma

9.7
)
a=s_{1}
,
b=s_{2}
and
\beta=2C_{\rm Signa\ell}\frac{\lambda}{k^{r/2}V_{n}\sqrt{M_{1}}}
.

As previously demonstrated,
s_{2}\leq s^{*}\leq\frac{k}{13}+2
, then it is immediate that
0\leq s_{2}\leq k/12
for sufficiently large
n
(recalling that
k=\omega(1)
).
Recall that
s_{1}=2\lceil a_{i}/4\rceil+\wp
,
s_{2}=2\lceil 3a_{i}/4\rceil+\wp
and
B_{1}^{i}
is initialized
with
B_{1}^{i}=a_{i}
from (
8.114
). Moreover, we see in Lemma

9.7
the initialization of our walk satisfies the following
(again, in what follows,
a=s_{1}
,
b=s_{2}
and
\beta=2C_{\rm Signa\ell}\frac{\lambda}{k^{r/2}V_{n}\sqrt{M_{1}}}
describing the random walk
X_{t}
): there should exist
a,b\in{\mathbb{N}}
with
a,b=\omega(1)
of the same parity and
X_{1}\in{\mathbb{N}}
fixed with value
X_{1}=a+C(b-a)
for
some
C\in[1/4,1)
with identical parity as
a
and
b
.

Indeed, in our case
a_{i}=\omega(1)
. Thus, for a
sufficiently large
n
and fixed
\varepsilon>0
, there exist a constant
C\geq 0.5-\varepsilon
, such that
B_{1}^{i}=a_{i}=2\lceil a_{i}/4\rceil+\wp+C(2\lceil 3a_{i}/4\rceil-2\lceil a_{i}/4\rceil)
; choosing
\varepsilon=1/4

gives that
C\geq 1/4
.

Moreover, using the set values of
s_{1}
and
s_{2}
, we must certify the following
inequalities to apply Lemma
9.7
for
a=s_{1}
,
b=s_{2}
and

\beta=2C_{\rm Signa\ell}\frac{\lambda}{k^{r/2}V_{n}\sqrt{M_{1}}}
. First, inequality

(8.142)

2C_{\rm Signa\ell}\frac{\lambda}{k^{r/2}n^{(r-1)/2}\sqrt{M_{1}}}(s_{1}-2)^{r-1}\geq 5\frac{s_{2}}{2k}

is equivalent
to the inequality

(8.143)

\lambda\geq 5k^{r/2-1}n^{(r-1)/2}\frac{2\lceil 3a_{i}/4\rceil+\wp}{4C_{\rm Signa\ell}(2\lceil a_{i}/4\rceil+\wp-2)^{r-1}}.

Next, recalling
a_{i}=\omega(1)
for all
i\in[m]
we know that
\lceil ca_{i}\rceil=(1+o(1))ca_{i}
for any
c\in{\mathbb{R}}
. Therefore, it suffices to prove,
for
n
sufficiently large, that
\lambda\geq C\cdot k^{r/2-1}n^{(r-1)/2}/a_{i}^{r-2}
(As
C_{\rm Signa\ell}=r\geq 1
) where
C>0
is a
large constant dependent on the above displayed inequality. Additionally, as
each
a_{i}\geq n^{1/4}k^{1/2}/5
by assumption, we then have

(8.144)

\frac{k^{r/2-1}n^{(r-1)/2}}{a_{i}^{r-2}}\leq 5^{r-2}\frac{k^{r/2-1}n^{(r-1)/2}}{n^{(r-2)/4}k^{(r-2)/2}}=5^{r-2}n^{r/4}.

Meaning that we only require
\lambda\geq C5^{r-2}\cdot n^{r/4}
. This holds by Assumption
8.6
(c) as
M_{1}\geq 1
for large
n
and
C_{\tau}
is considered a sufficiently large constant
dependent on
r
.

Second, inequality

(8.145)

2C_{\rm Signa\ell}\frac{\lambda}{k^{r/2}n^{(r-1)/2}\sqrt{M_{1}}}(s_{1}-2)^{r-1}(s_{2}-s_{1})/16\geq\log(\log^{2}(n)),

can be equivalently written as

(8.146)

2C_{\rm Signa\ell}\frac{\lambda}{k^{r/2}n^{(r-1)/2}\sqrt{M_{1}}}\left(2\lceil a_{i}/4\rceil+\wp-2\right)^{r-1}(2\lceil 3a_{i}/4\rceil-2\lceil a_{i}/4\rceil)/16\geq\log(\log^{2}(n)).

Again, using that
a_{i}=\omega(1)
for
all
i\in[m]
, a sufficient condition
for inequality (
8.146
) is, when
n
is sufficiently large,

(8.147)

\lambda\geq C\frac{\log(\log^{2}(n))k^{r/2}n^{(r-1)/2}\sqrt{M_{1}}}{C_{\rm Signa\ell}a_{i}^{r}},

where, again,
C>0
is a large
constant dependent on (
8.146
). Recalling that
a_{i}\geq n^{1/4}k^{1/2}/5
and
\lambda=\tau\sqrt{M_{1}}n^{r/4}
, the above inequality
holds when,

(8.148)

\tau n^{r/4}\geq C\cdot 5^{r-1}n^{(r-1)/2-r/4}\log(\log^{2}(n))=C5^{r-1}n^{r/4-1/2}\log(\log^{2}(n)),

which follows from
\tau\geq 1
for large enough
n
—assumed true by
Assumption
8.6
(c).

Now that each condition for Lemma
9.7
is certified for walk

B^{i}_{t}
. Invoking this Lemma gives that
{\mathbb{P}}(B^{i}_{\tilde{T}_{i}}=s_{2})=1-O(\log^{-2}(n))
. By our reduction of Lemma
8.8
to proving that

{\mathbb{P}}({\mathcal{E}}_{i}^{c})=O(\log^{-2}(n))
and using
{\mathbb{P}}({\mathcal{E}}_{i}^{c})\leq{\mathbb{P}}(T^{\prime}_{i}>T_{i})+{\mathbb{P}}(A^{i}_{T_{i}^{\prime}}=s_{1})={\mathbb{P}}(T_{i}^{\prime}>T_{i})+(1-{\mathbb{P}}(A^{i}_{T_{i}^{\prime}}=s_{2}))
, we are
done once we prove that
\sum_{i=1}^{m}T_{i}\leq\lfloor T^{*}/2\rfloor
.

Now, recalling (
8.118
), it remains to prove that
\lfloor T^{*}/2\rfloor<10n\log^{3.5}(n)
with probability
o(1)
. A sufficient condition for this to hold is when

(8.149)

T^{*}((p_{t})_{t\in[nM_{1}]})=\inf\left\{t:\max_{i\in[n]}\sum_{t^{\prime}\leq t}1\!\!1\{p_{t}=i\}=M_{1}+1\right\}=\omega(n\log^{3}(n))

with probability
1-o(1)
.

This follows as
{\mathbb{P}}(T^{*}\leq Cn\log^{4}(n))\leq n{\mathbb{P}}(X_{1}>M_{1})
where
X_{i}
is a
{\rm Binomia\ell}(Cn\log^{4}(n),1/n)
random
variable. Thus, as
{\mathbb{E}}[X_{1}]=C\log^{4}(n)
and
M_{1}=\lceil\log^{4}(n)\rceil
, we have, for any
\epsilon>0
, that

(8.150)

{\mathbb{P}}(T^{*}\leq C\log^{4}(n))\leq n{\mathbb{P}}(X_{1}>\frac{(1-\epsilon)}{C}{\mathbb{E}}[X_{1}]),

for sufficiently large
n
. Therefore, setting
C=2/(3(1-\epsilon))
, by a
Chernoff bound (see
[
MR95
, Section 4.1]
),

(8.151)

{\mathbb{P}}(T^{*}\leq Cn\log^{4}(n))\leq n\exp\left(-(0.5)^{2}\left(\frac{2}{3(1-\epsilon)}\log^{4}(n)\right)/6\right)=o(1).

By the preceding arguments and the definition
\partial{\mathcal{I}}_{1}
and event
{\mathcal{E}}_{1}
, with probability
1-o(1)
,
\langle S_{t},\theta\rangle
must be larger than
a_{1}/2=\Omega(k^{1/2}n^{1/4})=\omega(1)
for all time
t>0
until the inequality (
8.4
) holds. Therefore, this immediately implies that
\langle S_{t^{*}},\theta\rangle\geq 2
with probability
1-o(1)
as well.

∎

Proof of Lemma
8.9
.

Using Lemma
8.14
, we have almost surely over the value of
T^{*}
,
that the sign of the sign of
D^{\prime}_{t_{0}}
is equal in law to the sign of

(8.152)

\frac{f_{\theta,\lambda}(S_{t},p_{t},-(S_{t})_{p_{t}})}{V_{n}\sqrt{M_{1}}}+Z_{t_{0}},

where
Z_{t_{0}}\sim{\mathcal{N}}(0,1)
independently of the sequence
(p_{t})_{t\in[nM_{1}]}
and
T^{*}
. Then, using Lemma

9.10
applied to
|Z_{t}|
, for each
t\in[nM_{1}]
, and a union
bound, we have that
\max_{t\in[T^{*}]}|Z_{t}|\leq\max_{t\in[nM_{1}]}|Z_{t}|\leq 2(1-\varepsilon)\sqrt{\log(M_{1}n)}
with probability

1-o(1)
, for sufficiently small
\varepsilon>0
. We condition on this bound. Notice, this does not change random variable
(p_{t})_{t\in[T^{*}]}
as it is independent on
Z_{t}
.

Additionally, by the assumption
\langle S_{t_{0}},\theta\rangle\geq 2
, we can invoke the bounds in Corollary
8.11
(d).

Step 4. Confirming
\langle S_{t},\theta\rangle
is non-decreasing above threshold (
8.4
).

Notice, the overlap does not
change when the algorithm flips a sign of
(S_{t})_{p_{t}}
if
\theta_{p_{t}}=0
.
Therefore, we only consider
\theta_{p_{t}}\neq 0
. We then have two cases:

(a)

If
(S_{t_{0}})_{P_{t_{0}}}\neq\theta_{P_{t_{0}}}
, then
(Q_{t_{0}}-(S_{t_{0}})_{P_{t_{0}}})\theta_{P_{t_{0}}}=-2(S_{t_{0}})_{P_{t_{0}}}\theta_{P_{t_{0}}}=2\theta_{P_{t_{0}}}^{2}=2
. Thus, using (
8.31
) from Corollary
8.11
,

(8.153)

D^{\prime}_{t_{0}}\geq\frac{2C_{\rm Signa\ell}\frac{\lambda}{k^{r/2}}}{V_{n}\sqrt{M_{1}}}\langle S_{t_{0}},\theta\rangle^{r-1}-2(1-\varepsilon)\sqrt{\log(M_{1}n)},

(note, we can
invoke this inequality since we assumed that
\langle S_{t_{0}},\theta\rangle-2\geq 0
, and thus, any transition
S_{t_{0}}+e_{p_{t}}(q_{t}-(S_{t_{0}})_{P_{t_{0}}})
also has
\langle S_{t_{0}}+e_{p_{t}}(q_{t}-(S_{t_{0}})_{P_{t_{0}}}),\theta\rangle\geq 0
). And therefore, recalling the statement of the lemma assumed that
\frac{2C_{\rm Signa\ell}\frac{\lambda}{k^{r/2}}}{V_{n}\sqrt{M_{1}}}\langle S_{t_{0}},\theta\rangle^{r-1}\geq(2-\epsilon)\sqrt{\log(M_{1}n)}
and as
(2-\varepsilon)>2(1-\varepsilon)
for sufficiently small
\varepsilon>0
, we have that
D_{t_{0}}\geq 0
and the algorithm accepts the transition.

(b)

If
(S_{t_{0}})_{P_{t_{0}}}=\theta_{P_{t_{0}}}
, then
(Q_{t_{0}}-(S_{t_{0}})_{P_{t_{0}}})\theta_{P_{t_{0}}}=-2(S_{t_{0}})_{P_{t_{0}}}\theta_{P_{t_{0}}}=-2\theta_{P_{t_{0}}}^{2}=-2
. Thus,
using (
8.34
) from Corollary
8.11
,

(8.154)

D^{\prime}_{t_{0}}\leq-\frac{2C_{\rm Signa\ell}\frac{\lambda}{k^{r/2}}}{V_{n}\sqrt{M_{1}}}(\langle S_{t_{0}},\theta\rangle-2)^{r-1}+2(1-\varepsilon)\sqrt{\log(M_{1}n)}.

And therefore,
D_{t}<0
and the algorithm rejects this transition by identical reasoning to (a).

Thus, dependent on our conditioned bound over each
|Z_{t}|
, we have that

(1)

D_{t_{0}}>0
(and thus we accept the transition) when

(S_{t_{0}})_{p_{t}}\neq\theta_{p_{t}}
and
\theta_{p_{t}}\neq 0
;

(2)

D_{t_{0}}<0
(and thus we reject the transition) when

(S_{t_{0}})_{p_{t}}=\theta_{p_{t}}
and
\theta_{p_{t}}\neq 0
.

As such, the algorithm always accepts a transition which increases
\langle S_{t_{0}},\theta\rangle
after flipping the sign of
(S_{t_{0}})_{p_{t}}
and never accepts
a transition where
\langle S_{t_{0}},\theta\rangle
decreases after flipping
the sign of
(S_{t_{0}})_{p_{t}}
. Thus,
\langle S_{t_{0}+1},\theta\rangle\geq\langle S_{t_{0}},\theta\rangle
with probability
1-o(1)
. Recursively applying
this argument to
D^{\prime}_{t}
from (
8.2
) over all times
t\geq t_{0}
gives
that each transition has

(8.155)

\frac{2C_{\rm Signa\ell}\frac{\lambda}{k^{r/2}}}{V_{n}\sqrt{M_{1}}}(\langle S_{t},\theta\rangle-2)^{r-1}\geq\frac{2C_{\rm Signa\ell}\frac{\lambda}{k^{r/2}}}{V_{n}\sqrt{M_{1}}}(\langle S_{t_{0}},\theta\rangle-2)^{r-1}>(2-\varepsilon)\sqrt{\log(M_{1}n)}.

Hence, the overlap

\langle S_{t},\theta\rangle
is non-decreasing dependent on our bound on each
|Z_{t}|
for
every
t\geq t_{0}
and is always bounded below by two.

Step 6. Checking probabilistic conditions on
(p_{t})_{t\in[T^{*}]}
.

Therefore, a sufficient
condition for Algorithm
15
to return
S\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
with
\langle S,\theta\rangle=k
is for
{\mathbb{P}}({\mathcal{A}})=1-o(1)
, where
{\mathcal{A}}=\{\{T^{*}\geq\frac{3}{4}nM_{1}\}\cap\{\text{each }i\in[n]\text{ is in }(p_{t})_{\frac{1}{2}nM_{1}\leq t\leq\frac{3}{4}nM_{1}}\}\}
. Indeed, this follows from the assumption that
t\leq\lfloor T^{*}/2\rfloor\leq nM_{1}/2
. Notice that

(8.156)

{\mathbb{P}}({\mathcal{A}}^{c})\leq{\mathbb{P}}\left(\left\{T^{*}<\frac{3}{4}nM_{1}\right\}\right)+{\mathbb{P}}(\{\text{each }i\in[n]\text{ is in }(p_{t})_{\frac{1}{2}nM_{1}\leq t\leq\frac{3}{4}nM_{1}}\}^{c}),

and thus, define the event
{\mathcal{B}}_{i}=\{i\not\in(p_{t})_{\frac{1}{2}nM_{1}\leq t\leq\frac{3}{4}nM_{1}}\}
. The statement follows if

{\mathbb{P}}(\{T^{*}<\frac{3}{4}nM_{1}\})
and
{\mathbb{P}}({\mathcal{B}}_{i})=o(1/n)
by a union bound.
Then, with
X_{i}\sim\mathrm{Binomia\ell}(\frac{3}{4}nM_{1},\frac{1}{n})
independent for
i\in[n]
, we have that

(8.157)

{\mathbb{P}}\left(\left\{T^{*}<\frac{3}{4}nM_{1}\right\}\right)={\mathbb{P}}\left(\max_{i\in{n}}X_{i}\geq M_{1}\right)\leq n{\mathbb{P}}(X_{1}\geq M_{1})\leq n{\mathbb{P}}\left(X_{1}\geq\frac{4}{3}{\mathbb{E}}[X_{1}]\right).

Therefore, using a Chernoff bound
[
MR95
, Section 4.1]
gives that,

(8.158)

{\mathbb{P}}\left(\left\{T^{*}<\frac{3}{4}nM_{1}\right\}\right)\leq ne^{-(0.5)^{2}M_{1}/6}=o(1/n).

The final equality comes from
M_{1}=\lceil\log^{4}(n)\rceil
.
Moreover, we have that

(8.159)

{\mathbb{P}}({\mathcal{B}}_{i})=\left(1-\frac{1}{n}\right)^{nM_{1}/8}\leq e^{-M_{1}/8}\leq n^{-\log(n)}=o(1/n),

for sufficiently large
n
as
M_{1}=\lceil\log^{4}(n)\rceil
.

Thus, recalling the above is done under a conditioned event on
(Z_{t})_{t\in[T^{*}]}
which holds with probability
1-o(1)
, independent of

(p_{t})_{t\in[nM_{1}]}
, we complete the proof.

∎

8.5.4.
Proof of Lemma
8.10

Lemma 8.17
.

Consider Algorithm
14
and set
T=\lceil M_{2}n/2\rceil
. Recall
(p_{t},q_{t})_{t\in[T]}
is the sequence
of proposals used in Algorithm
14
. Then the following holds with
probability
1-o(1)
:

For the set of proposals
(p_{t},q_{t})_{t\in[T]}
, each possible
(i,j)\in[n]\times\{-1,0,1\}
is proposed at least once and no single
i\in[n]
is
proposed by
(p_{t})_{t\in[T]}
strictly more than
M_{2}
times.

Proof of Lemma
8.17
.

Consider an arbitrary
M\in{\mathbb{N}}
, we first
derive probabilistic bounds for the statements of the lemma, denoting
T=\lceil Mn/2\rceil
(slightly abusing notation). Afterwords, we use
the definition of
M_{2}
to conclude the proof.

Let
{\mathcal{A}}_{i}
be the event that
i
is proposed by
(p_{t})_{t\in[T]}
more
than
M
times and
{\mathcal{B}}_{i,j}
be the event that
(i,j)
is not proposed
by
(p_{t},q_{t})_{t\in[T]}
, then the statement follows by a union bound if

{\mathbb{P}}(\cup_{i\in[n]}{\mathcal{A}}_{i})=o(1)
and
{\mathbb{P}}(\cup_{(i,j)\in[n]\times\{-1,0,1\}}{\mathcal{B}}_{i,j})=o(1)
.

Define the random variable
X_{i}=\sum_{t=1}^{T}1\!\!1\{p_{t}=i\}
. Marginally,

X_{i}\sim{\rm Binomia\ell}(T,\frac{1}{n})
. As such, by a Chernoff bound (see
Section 4.1 of
[
MR95
]
), we have for sufficiently large

n
,

(8.160)

{\mathbb{P}}(\cup_{i\in[n]}{\mathcal{A}}_{i})={\mathbb{P}}(\cup_{i\in[n]}\{X_{i}\geq M\})\leq n{\mathbb{P}}\left(X_{i}\geq 1.5\frac{T}{n}\right)\leq ne^{-(0.5)^{2}M/6}\leq ne^{-M/24}.

The penultimate inequality is due to
T=\lceil Mn/2\rceil\geq Mn/2
.

Further, by the existence of a bijection between
[n]\times\{-1,0,1\}
and

[3n]
, the event

\cup_{(i,j)\in[n]\times\{-1,0,1\}}{\mathcal{B}}_{i,j}

is equivalent to it requiring more than
T
uniform draws to collect all
3n

out of
3n
coupons. Then, Lemma
9.11
implies that the time
T^{\prime}
to
collect all
3n
coupons is bounded by

(8.161)

{\mathbb{P}}(T^{\prime}>\beta(3n)\log(3n))\leq(3n)^{-\beta+1}

where
\beta=T/(3n\log(3n))\geq M/(6\log(3n))
. As we have
assumed that
M=M_{2}=\lceil 25\log(3n)\rceil
, then
both
ne^{-M_{2}/24}=o(1)
and
(3n)^{-\beta+1}\leq(3n)^{-25/6+1}=o(1)
as
n\to\infty
, giving the proof.
∎

Proof of Lemma
8.10
.

By Assumption
8.6
(a), recalling we can choose
C_{\tau}\geq 4C_{k}^{(r-2)/4}
, where
k\geq C_{k}\sqrt{n}
for some constant
C_{k}>0
, we have that

(8.162)

\lambda\geq 4C_{k}^{(r-2)/4}\gamma n^{r/4}=4C_{k}^{(r-2)/4}\gamma\frac{n^{(r-1)/2}}{n^{r/4-1/2}}\geq 4\gamma\frac{n^{(r-1)/2}}{k^{(r-2)/2}}.

Thus, for any
vector
S_{1}\in\{-1,1\}^{n}
satisfying the statement of the lemma,
for large
n
, we have

(8.163)

\displaystyle\left(\frac{\langle S_{1},\theta\rangle-2}{k^{1/2}(\|S_{1}\|_{0}+1)^{1/2}}\right)^{r-1}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{k-2}{k^{1/2}(n+1)^{1/2}}}^{r-1}\geq\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{k/n}^{r-1}

(8.164)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{k}\frac{k^{(r-2)/2}}{n^{(r-1)/2}}\geq 2\gamma\frac{\sqrt{k}}{\lambda}\geq\frac{2\gamma}{C_{\rm Signa\ell}}\frac{\sqrt{k}}{\lambda},

and therefore, the condition
\left(\frac{\langle S_{1},\theta\rangle-2}{k^{1/2}(\|S_{1}\|_{0}+1)^{1/2}}\right)^{r-1}\geq\frac{2\gamma}{C_{\mathrm{Signa\ell}}}\frac{\sqrt{k}}{\lambda}
holds for
large enough
n
. Moreover, since
\langle S_{1},\theta\rangle=k
, then for sufficiently large
n
we have that
\langle S_{t},\theta\rangle\geq 2
. And therefore, we satisfy both conditions of
Lemma
8.4
. Combining this result with Lemma

8.17
, ensuring that we propose each possible transition once and we can inject noise for the duration of the runtime of Algorithm
14
, proves the statement as both lemmas hold with
probability
1-o(1)
.
∎

8.6.
Proofs of Secondary Lemmas

In this section we prove Corollary
8.11
and Lemma
8.13
, that were used in the previous section to prove the Key Lemmas. The proof of Lemma
8.14
is deferred to Section
9.2
.

In order to prove Corollary
8.11
, we introduce Lemmas
8.18
,
8.19
,

8.20
and
8.21
. Their proofs are given in Section
9.3
.

Lemma 8.18
.

Let
\sigma\in\{-1,0,1\}
,
a\in{\mathbb{R}}
,
i\in[n]
, and
r\in{\mathbb{N}}
. Consider the function

(8.165)

g(\sigma,a,i)=\langle(\sigma+ae_{i})^{\otimes r},W\rangle-\langle\sigma^{\otimes r},W\rangle,

where
W\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n^{\otimes r}}
has i.i.d. standard Gaussian elements. Then, the following equivalence in law holds:

(8.166)

g(\sigma,i,a)\overset{{\mathcal{L}}}{=}\|(\sigma+ae_{i})^{\otimes r}-\sigma^{\otimes r}\|_{F}Z,

where
Z
is a standard
Gaussian random variable.

In order to make this equality in law more practical to apply, we
provide a non-asymptotic bound on the pre-factor
V(S_{t},p_{t},q_{t})=\|(S_{t}+e_{p_{t}}(q_{t}-(S_{t})_{p_{t}}))^{\otimes r}-(S_{t})^{\otimes r}\|_{F}
in front of
Z
.

Lemma 8.19
.

Let
\sigma\in\{-1,0,1\}^{n}
and
a\in[-2,2]
,
i\in[n]
, and
r\in{\mathbb{N}}
. We have the following bound on

\|(\sigma+ae_{i})^{\otimes r}-\sigma^{\otimes r}\|_{F}
:

(8.167)

\|(\sigma+ae_{i})^{\otimes r}-\sigma^{\otimes r}\|_{F}\leq C_{\rm Noise}|a|\;\|\sigma\|_{0}^{\frac{r-1}{2}},

where
C_{\rm Noise}=\left(\sum_{j=1}^{r}\binom{r}{j}^{2}2^{2j}\right)^{1/2}
.

It remains to bound both
f_{\theta,\lambda}(S_{t},p_{t},q_{t})
and
h_{\gamma}(S_{t},p_{t},q_{t})
; this is demonstrated by the
next two lemmas.

Lemma 8.20
.

Let
a\in{\mathbb{R}}
,
i\in[n]
,
\sigma\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
,
\theta\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
,
a\in{\mathbb{R}}
, and
r\in{\mathbb{N}}
where

\min(\langle\sigma+ae_{i},\theta\rangle,\langle\sigma,\theta\rangle)\geq 0
. Consider the function

(8.168)

f(\sigma,\theta,a,i)=\langle(\sigma+ae_{i})^{\otimes r},\theta^{\otimes r}\rangle-\langle\sigma^{\otimes r},\theta^{\otimes r}\rangle.

We then have the following
upper and lower bounds: When
a\theta_{i}\geq 0
,

(8.169)

C_{\rm Signa\ell}\min(\langle\sigma+ae_{i},\theta\rangle,\langle\sigma,\theta\rangle)^{r-1}a\theta_{i}\leq f(\sigma,\theta,a,i)\leq C_{\rm Signa\ell}\max(\langle\sigma+ae_{i},\theta\rangle,\langle\sigma,\theta\rangle)^{r-1}a\theta_{i},

where
C_{\rm Signa\ell}=r
. Moreover, when

a\theta_{i}\leq 0
,

(8.170)

C_{\rm Signa\ell}\max(\langle\sigma+ae_{i},\theta\rangle,\langle\sigma,\theta\rangle)^{r-1}a\theta_{i}\leq f(\sigma,\theta,a,i)\leq C_{\rm Signa\ell}\min(\langle\sigma+ae_{i},\theta\rangle,\langle\sigma,\theta\rangle)^{r-1}a\theta_{i}.

Lemma 8.21
.

Let
s,a,\gamma\in{\mathbb{R}}
where
\min(s,s+a,\gamma)\geq 0
. Consider the function

(8.171)

h(s,a)=\gamma((s+a)^{\frac{r+1}{2}}-s^{\frac{r+1}{2}}),

we then have the
following upper and lower bounds:

(8.172)

C_{\rm Reg}\gamma\min(s,s+a)^{\frac{r+1}{2}}a\leq h(s,a)\leq C_{\rm Reg}\gamma\max(s,s+a)^{\frac{r+1}{2}}a,

where
C_{\rm Reg}=\frac{r+1}{2}
.

Proof of Corollary
8.11
.

(a): Apply Lemma
8.18
with the choice of
\sigma=S_{t}
,
i=p_{t}
and
a=q_{t}-(S_{t})_{p_{t}}

(b): Apply Lemma
8.19
with
\sigma=S_{t}
,
i=p_{t}
and
a=q_{t}-(S_{t})_{p_{t}}\in[-2,2]

(c): Combine statements (a) and (b)

(d): Apply Lemma
8.20
with
\sigma=S_{t}
,
i=p_{t}
, and
a=q_{t}-(S_{t})_{p_{t}}
. The factor

\frac{\lambda}{k^{r/2}}
is due to the pre-factor on
\theta^{\otimes r}
—as
seen in Definition (
1.1
).

(e): Apply Lemma
8.21
with
s=\|S_{t}\|_{0}
and
a=|q_{t}|-|(S_{t})_{p_{t}}|
. Clearly
s
and
s+a
must be larger than zero as no proposal can have zero-norm less than zero.

∎

Proof of Lemma
8.13
.

For notational convenience, for this proof only, we abbreviate
V(S_{t},p_{t},q_{t})

as
V
.

Recall (
8.1
), defining
D_{t}
as

(8.173)

D_{t}=f_{\theta,\lambda}(S_{t},p_{t},q_{t})+g_{W}(S_{t},p_{t},q_{t})-h_{\gamma}(S_{t},p_{t},q_{t})+K_{p_{t},t_{p_{t}},q_{t}}

for a given time
t
.
Moreover, using Corollary
8.11
(a) and the decomposition of
D_{t}
from Remark
8.3
, we have that

(8.174)

D_{t}\overset{{\mathcal{L}}}{=}f_{\theta,\lambda}(S_{t},p_{t},q_{t})+VZ-h_{\gamma}(S_{t},p_{t},q_{t})+K_{p_{t},t_{p_{t}},q_{t}},

where each
Z\overset{\mathrm{iid}}{\sim}{\mathcal{N}}(0,{\rm Id}_{n})
. Consider the term
D_{t}/V=f_{\theta,\lambda}(S_{t},p_{t},q_{t})/V+Z-h_{\gamma}(S_{t},p_{t},q_{t})/V+K_{p_{t},t_{p_{t}},q_{t}}/V
. Then (as a slight abuse of notation), define a vector
v=\mu+Z
where
\mu_{p_{t}}=f_{\theta,\lambda}(S_{t},p_{t},q_{t})/V-h_{\gamma}(S_{t},p_{t},q_{t})/V
for
p_{t}\in[n]
and
Z\overset{\mathrm{iid}}{\sim}{\mathcal{N}}(0,{\rm Id}_{n})
. Thus,

(8.175)

(D_{t}/V)_{t\in[T^{*}_{M}]}=(v_{p_{t}}+K_{p_{t},t_{p_{t}},q_{t}}/V)_{t\in[T^{*}_{M}]},

and expanding the definition of
K_{p_{t},t_{p_{t}},q_{t}}
, we
equivalently have

(8.176)

(D_{t}/V)_{t\in[T^{*}_{M}]}=(v_{p_{t}}+G_{P_{t}}^{t_{p_{t}}}-\bar{G}_{p_{t}})_{t\in[T^{*}_{M}]}.

Recall how Algorithm
14
generated
each element
G_{i}^{j}
for
i\in[n]
and
j\in[M]
. This generation leads
to the right-hand side above to correspond to the output of a
{\mathcal{S}}{\mathcal{G}}{\mathcal{C}}(\mu,(\Delta_{t}),M)
algorithm where
T_{M}=T^{*}_{M}
. And therefore, by Theorem

7.2
,

(8.177)

(D_{t}/V)_{t\in T^{*}_{M}}\overset{{\mathcal{L}}}{=}(f_{\theta,\lambda}(S_{t},p_{t},q_{t})-h_{\gamma}(S_{t},p_{t},q_{t}))/V+Z^{\prime}_{t},

where, almost
surely over
T^{*}_{M}
, the random variable
Z^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{T^{*}_{M}}\sim{\mathcal{N}}(0,M{\rm Id}_{T^{*}_{M}})
, additionally
Z^{\prime}
is independent
of
(p_{t})_{t\in{\mathbb{N}}}
and thus
T^{*}_{M}((p_{t})_{t\in{\mathbb{N}}})=T^{*}_{M}
.
Multiplying the above by
V
then gives that

(8.178)

(D_{t})_{t\in t^{*}_{M}}\overset{{\mathcal{L}}}{=}(f_{\theta,\lambda}(S_{t},p_{t},q_{t})-h_{\gamma}(S_{t},p_{t},q_{t})+VZ^{\prime}_{t})_{t\in[T^{*}_{M}]}.

Pulling out a
M^{1/2}
factor from
Z^{\prime}_{t}

concludes the proof.
∎

9.
Deferred Material

9.1.
Auxiliary Lemmas

Lemma 9.1

(
[
BLM13
]
, Theorem 2.5)
.

Let
(Z_{i})_{i=1}^{N}
be a collection of mean zero, possibly dependent, Gaussian random variables, each with variance at most
\sigma^{2}
. Then

(9.1)

\mathbb{E}\left(\max_{i}Z_{i}\right)\leq\sqrt{2\sigma^{2}\log N}

Lemma 9.2

(
[
BLM13
]
, Theorem 5.8)
.

Let
(Z_{i})_{i=1}^{N}
be a collection of mean zero, possibly dependent, Gaussian random variables, each with variance at most
\sigma^{2}
. Then
\mathrm{Var}(\max_{i}Z_{i})\leq\sigma^{2}
, and for all
u>0
,

(9.2)

\mathbb{P}\left(\left|\max_{i}Z_{i}-\mathbb{E}\left(\max_{i}Z_{i}\right)\right|\geq u\right)\leq\exp\left(-\frac{u^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}\right)

Theorem 9.3
.

Let
(X_{t})_{t>0}
be a sequence of random
variables supported on the finite interval
{\mathcal{I}}=[a,b]\cap{\mathbb{Z}}
, where

a,b\in{\mathbb{Z}}
with
a\leq b-1
. Let
T=\inf\{t\geq 0:X_{t}=b\text{ or
}X_{t}=a\}
, if
\delta_{t}(s)={\mathbb{E}}[X_{t+1}-X_{t}|X_{t}=s]\geq\delta

for all
s\in{\mathcal{I}}\setminus\{a,b\}
and
X_{t+1}=X_{t}
almost surely when

X_{t}\in\{a,b\}
, then
{\mathbb{E}}[T]\leq(b-a)/\min(\delta,1)
.

Proof.

Let
\tilde{X}_{t+1}
be a random variable with
{\mathbb{P}}(\tilde{X}_{t+1}=a+1|\tilde{X}_{t}=a)=1
and all other probabilities are
equivalent to
X_{t}
. Then, setting
\tilde{X}_{1}=X_{1}
, we construct a coupling
where we generate
X_{t}
given
X_{t-1}
and then set
\tilde{X}_{t}=X_{t}
until

X_{t}=a
, after which draw
\tilde{X}_{t+1}
and the subsequent
iterates of
(\tilde{X}_{t})_{t^{\prime}\geq t+1}
independently of
(X_{t})_{t^{\prime}\geq t+1}
. Under this coupling we have that

\tilde{X}_{t}=X_{t}
almost surely until
X_{t}=a
.

If
X_{t}
hits
b
before
a
then
\tilde{X}_{t}=X_{t}=b
. Otherwise, if
X_{t}

hits
a
before
b
then
\tilde{X}_{t}=X_{t}=a
, and it requires at least one
more step for
\tilde{X}
to reach
b
. Therefore, with
\tilde{T}=\inf\{t\geq 0:\tilde{X}_{t}=b\}
, we have that
T\leq\tilde{T}
almost surely on the coupling’s probability
space; meaning
{\mathbb{E}}[T]\leq{\mathbb{E}}[\tilde{T}]
.

Let
Y_{t}=-\tilde{X}_{t}+b
, where
Y_{t}
is supported on the set
{\mathcal{I}}^{\prime}=[0,b-a]\cap{\mathbb{Z}}
. It is trivial that
{\mathbb{E}}[Y_{t}-Y_{t+1}|Y_{t-1}=s]={\mathbb{E}}[\tilde{X}_{t+1}-\tilde{X}_{t}|\tilde{X}_{t-1}=b-s]\geq\min(\delta,1)
for all
s\in{\mathcal{I}}^{\prime}\setminus\{0\}
as this inequality holds for
\tilde{X}_{t+1}
when
\tilde{X}_{t}\not\in\{a,b\}
, and we constructed
\tilde{X}_{t+1}
for this to hold
when
\tilde{X}_{t}=a
. Now letting
T^{\prime}
be the hitting time for
Y_{t}
to reach

0
, by the additive drift theorem
[
Len20
, Theorem 1]
,
we have that
{\mathbb{E}}[T^{\prime}]\leq{\mathbb{E}}[Y_{0}]/\min(\delta,1)\leq(b-a)/\min(\delta,1)
. As
Y_{t}=0

is equivalent to
X_{t}=b
by construction, we in turn have that
{\mathbb{E}}[T]\leq{\mathbb{E}}[\tilde{T}]={\mathbb{E}}[T^{\prime}]\leq(b-a)/\min(\delta,1)
.

∎

Remark 9.4
.

Lemma
9.6
and Lemma
9.5
rely on two equivalent
definitions of stochastic domination, denoted between two scalar random
variables
X
,
Y
by
X\preceq_{\rm st}Y
.

Let
X\sim\mu
and
Y\sim\nu
, for two probability measures
\mu
and

\nu
. Then,
X\preceq_{\rm st}Y
if either condition below holds:

(1)

For all
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
, we have that
{\mathbb{P}}(X\geq t)\leq{\mathbb{P}}(Y\geq t)
.

(2)

There exists a coupling
\gamma
of
(X,Y)
such that
X=Y+\delta
for some random variable
\delta
which is non-positive almost
surely. Equivalently, also could have
Y=X+\delta
for some random
variable
\delta
which is non-negative almost surely.

Notably, this definition of stochastic domination differs slightly from the
traditional definition as
X\preceq_{\rm st}Y
even when
X\overset{{\mathcal{L}}}{=}Y
.

Lemma 9.5
.

Let
X
be a random variable with law

(9.3)

{\mathbb{P}}(X=-2)=q,{\mathbb{P}}(X=2)=p,{\mathbb{P}}(X=0)=1-q-p.

If
Y
is
supported on the set
\{-2,0,2\}
and
{\mathbb{P}}(Y=-2)\geq q
and
{\mathbb{P}}(Y=2)\leq p
then
Y\preceq_{\rm st}X
.

Proof.

The proof follows if we can write that
{\mathbb{P}}(Y\geq t)\leq{\mathbb{P}}(X\geq t)

for
t\in\{-2,0,2\}
as this is the support of both
Y
and
X
. This
follows immediately as
{\mathbb{P}}(Y\geq-2)=1={\mathbb{P}}(X\geq-2)
,
{\mathbb{P}}(Y\geq 0)=1-{\mathbb{P}}(Y=-2)\leq 1-q={\mathbb{P}}(X\geq 0)
, and
{\mathbb{P}}(Y\geq 2)=p\leq{\mathbb{P}}(X\geq 2)
.
∎

Lemma 9.6
.

Let
S_{t}=\sum_{i=1}^{t}X_{i}
be a random walk on
the set
{\mathcal{I}}=[a,b]\cap{\mathbb{Z}}
where
a,b\in{\mathbb{Z}}
with each
X_{i}=X_{i}(s)

dependent on
S_{i-1}=s
. Moreover, if
S_{t}\in\{a,b\}
then
X_{t+1}=0
almost surely. In addition, let
S^{\prime}_{t}=\sum_{i=1}^{t}Y_{i}
be a random
walk on the set
{\mathcal{I}}
, we similarly assume that
S^{\prime}_{t}\in\{a,b\}
implies
that
Y_{t+1}=0
almost surely. If
Y_{i}\preceq_{\rm st}X_{i}(s)
almost surely for
all
s\in{\mathcal{I}}\setminus\{a,b\}
then
S^{\prime}_{t}\preceq_{\rm st}S_{t}
for all
t\geq 1
.

Proof.

We prove the statement
S^{\prime}_{t}\preceq_{\rm st}S_{t}
by induction on
t
, as
S^{\prime}_{1}=Y_{1}\preceq_{\rm st}X_{1}=S_{1}
, the base case holds.

Thus, assuming that
S^{\prime}_{t}\preceq_{\rm st}S_{t}
, there exists a coupling of
(S^{\prime}_{t},S_{t})
such that
S_{t}=S^{\prime}_{t}+\delta
where
\delta\geq 0
almost surely.
Therefore, under this coupling, if
S_{t}=b
then
S^{\prime}_{t}\leq b
almost
surely, wherein
S^{\prime}_{t+1}\leq S_{t+1}=b
is trivial. If
S^{\prime}_{t}=a
then

S_{t}\geq a
by definition and, again,
a=S^{\prime}_{t+1}\leq S_{t+1}
is
trivial. Finally, consider
S^{\prime}_{t}>a
and
S_{t}<b
, by the inductive
hypothesis we must also have
S_{t}>a
and
S^{\prime}_{t}<b
almost surely.
Therefore,
Y_{t+1}\preceq_{\rm st}X_{t+1}
for all possible values of
S_{t}
in this
case. We can then calculate, under the coupling implied by
S^{\prime}_{t}\preceq_{\rm st}S_{t}

and
Y_{t+1}\preceq_{\rm st}X_{t+1}
, that

(9.4)

\displaystyle S_{t+1}

\displaystyle=S_{t}+X_{t+1}

(9.5)

\displaystyle=S_{t}+\delta_{1}+Y_{t+1}+\delta_{2}

(9.6)

\displaystyle=S^{\prime}_{t+1}+(\delta_{1}+\delta_{2}),

where both
\delta_{1}\geq 0
and
\delta_{2}\geq 0
almost surely; the proof
follows from Remark
9.4
.
∎

Lemma 9.7
.

Let
\beta\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
. Consider a random
walk
S_{t}=\sum_{i=1}^{t}X_{i}
on the set
[a,b]\cap{\mathbb{Z}}
where
a,b\in{\mathbb{N}}
have
a,b=\omega(1)
of the same parity. Moreover, let
X_{1}\in{\mathbb{N}}
be fixed with value
X_{1}=a+C(b-a)
for some
C\in(0,1)
and,
in addition,
X_{1}
must have same parity as
a
and
b
. We define each

X_{i}
, for
i>1
, as an independent random variable with
law,

(9.7)

X_{i}\sim\begin{cases}2&\qquad\text{with probability }p=\left(\frac{k}{2n}-\frac{b}{2n}\right)\Phi(\beta(a-2)^{r-1})\\
-2&\qquad\text{with probability }q=\left(\frac{k}{2n}+\frac{b}{2n}\right)\Phi(-\beta(a-2)^{r-1})\\
0&\qquad\text{with probability }1-p-q\end{cases}.

If
C\geq 1/4
,
0\leq b\leq k/12
,
\beta(a-2)^{r-1}\geq\frac{5b}{2k}
, and
\beta(a-2)^{r-1}(b-a)/16\geq\log(\log^{2}(n))
then with
T=\inf\{t\geq 0:S_{t}=b\text{ or }S_{t}=a\}
we have
{\mathbb{P}}(S_{T}\geq b)\geq 1-\log^{-2}(n)
for sufficiently large
n
.

Proof.

First we will get a useful bounds on the ratio of
q/p
,

(9.8)

\displaystyle\frac{q}{p}

\displaystyle=\frac{(\frac{k}{2n}+\frac{b}{2n})\Phi(-\beta(a-2)^{r-1})}{(\frac{k}{2n}-\frac{b}{2n})\Phi(\beta(a-2)^{r-1})}

(9.9)

\displaystyle=\frac{\frac{k}{2n}+\frac{b}{2n}}{\frac{k}{2n}-\frac{b}{2n}}\frac{\Phi(-\beta(a-2)^{r-1})}{\Phi(\beta(a-2)^{r-1})}.

As
\beta(a-2)^{r-1}\geq 0
, Lemma
9.8
gives that
\Phi(-\beta(a-2)^{r-1})/\Phi(\beta(a-2)^{r-1})\leq e^{-\beta a^{r-1}}
. Additionally, as
b\leq k/12
, Lemma
9.9
gives that
\frac{k/(2n)+b/(2n)}{k/(2n)-b/(2n)}\leq 1+5b/(2k)
. Thus,

(9.10)

q/p\leq\left(1+5\frac{b}{2k}\right)\cdot e^{-\beta(a-2)^{r-1}},

for

n
sufficiently large. Taking the logarithm of both sides gives

(9.11)

\log(q/p)\leq\log\left(1+5\frac{b}{2k}\right)-\beta(a-2)^{r-1},

and
using that
\log(1+x)\leq x
and
\frac{1}{2}\beta(a-2)^{r-1}\geq\frac{5b}{2k}
(by assumption) results in

(9.12)

\log(q/p)\leq-\frac{1}{2}\beta(a-2)^{r-1}.

Thus,
q/p\leq 1
. We can now
calculate the probability that
S_{T}\geq b
using that
C\geq 1/4
and the
formula
[
GS01
, Section 3.9]
,

(9.13)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}(S_{T}=b)=\frac{1-(q/p)^{\frac{1}{2}(S_{1}-a)}}{1-(q/p)^{\frac{1}{2}(b-a)}}\geq\frac{1-(q/p)^{\frac{C}{2}(b-a)}}{1-(q/p)^{\frac{1}{2}(b-a)}}\geq 1-(q/p)^{\frac{C}{2}(b-a)}\geq 1-(q/p)^{\frac{1}{8}(b-a)}.

The

\frac{1}{2}
factor in the exponent of
(q/p)
is due to the walk
S_{t}

taking steps of size two instead of size one as in

[
GS01
]
, note the formula still holds as we assumed
that the parity of
a
and
b
is equal to the parity of
X_{1}
, accounting
for steps of size two. Therefore, using (
9.12
), our assumed
lower bound on
\beta(b-a)(a-2)^{r-1}
and
n
sufficiently large, we have

(9.14)

(q/p)^{\frac{1}{8}(b-a)}=e^{\frac{1}{8}(b-a)\log(q/p)}\leq e^{-\frac{1}{16}(b-a)\beta(a-2)^{r-1}}\leq e^{-\log(\log^{2}(n))}=\log^{-2}(n).

Which in turn implies that
{\mathbb{P}}(S_{T}=b)\geq 1-\log^{-2}(n)
for sufficiently large
n
.
∎

Lemma 9.8
.

Let
\Phi
be the Gaussian CDF. For each
x\geq 0
, we have that

\frac{\Phi(-x)}{\Phi(x)}\leq e^{-x}
.

Proof.

Let
\phi(x)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-x^{2}/2}
. Define
f(x)=\log\left(\frac{\Phi(-x)}{\Phi(x)}\right)+x
, clearly
f(x)\leq 0
for a
fixed
x
implies that
\frac{\Phi(-x)}{\Phi(x)}\leq e^{-x}
by rearranging
and taking the exponential. Since
\Phi(0)=1/2
, we have that
f(0)=0

and thus, if
f^{\prime}(x)\leq 0
for all
x\geq 0
, then
f
is non-increasing,
meaning
f(x)\leq 0
for all
x\geq 0
. Simple calculus then gives that

(9.15)

f^{\prime}(x)=1-\phi(x)\left(\frac{1}{\Phi(x)}-\frac{1}{1-\Phi(x)}\right).

Therefore, the condition
f^{\prime}(x)\leq 0
holds if

(9.16)

\phi(x)\left(\frac{1}{\Phi(x)}-\frac{1}{1-\Phi(x)}\right)\geq 1,

or
equivalently,
\phi(x)\geq\Phi(x)(1-\Phi(x))
.

We further define
h(x)=\phi(x)-\Phi(x)(1-\Phi(x))
. We see that
h(0)\geq 0
and, moreover, we calculate
h^{\prime}(x)=-x\phi(x)+\phi(x)(1-\Phi(x))-\Phi(x)(-\phi(x))=-\phi(x)(x+1-2\Phi(x))
. Thus, using that
\Phi(x)\leq\frac{1}{2}(1+x)
for
x\geq 0
, we have that
h^{\prime}(x)\leq 0
for
x\geq 0
. Thus,
h
is non-decreasing for
x\geq 0
and hence
h(x)\geq 0

for each
x\geq 0
. This implies that
\phi(x)\geq\Phi(x)(1-\Phi(x))
for
all
x\geq 0
, which in turn gives the stated lemma.
∎

Lemma 9.9
.

Let
x\in[0,k/(12n)]
, then
\frac{k/(2n)+x}{k/(2n)-x}\leq 1+5\frac{n}{k}x
.

Proof.

Calculate the second derivative of
x\mapsto\frac{k/(2n)+x}{k/(2n)-x}
as

-\frac{2k}{n(2x-\frac{k}{2n})^{3}}
. This function is positive for any
0\leq x<k/(2n)
,
therefore
x\mapsto\frac{k/(2n)+x}{k/(2n)-x}
is convex on the domain
\{0\leq x\leq 1/12\}
. Thus, setting
f(x)=\frac{k/(2n)+x}{k/(2n)-x}
, we have
that
f(x)
is bounded above by the line connecting the points
(0,1)
and

(k/(12n),7/5)
when
0\leq x\leq k/(12n)
by convexity. Basic arithmetic then
gives the bound
f(x)\leq 1+\frac{12n}{k}(7/5-1)x\leq 1+5\frac{n}{k}x
when
0\leq x\leq 1/12
, completing the proof.
∎

Lemma 9.10
.

Let
Z
be a standard Gaussian
random variable, then for all
t\geq 0
,
{\mathbb{P}}(Z>t)\leq e^{-t^{2}/2}
.

Proof.

If
t\geq\sqrt{2\pi}^{-1}
, by the Mills ratio
[
Abr74
, Formula 7.1.13]
, we have the bound of
{\mathbb{P}}(Z>t)\leq\frac{1}{t}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-t^{2}/2}\leq e^{-t^{2}/2}
. As

\sqrt{2\pi}^{-1}\leq 1
the proof follows by observing that
{\mathbb{P}}(Z>t)\leq 1/2
and
e^{-1^{2}/2}
(i.e. our desired upper bound at
t=1
) being
greater than
1/2
.
∎

Lemma 9.11
.

Consider the classical coupon collector problem with
n
coupons, let
T
be
the time required to collect all
n
coupons, then

(9.17)

{\mathbb{P}}(T>\beta n\log n)\leq n^{-\beta+1}.

Proof.

Let

Z_{i}^{r}
be the event that the
i
-th coupon was not picked in the first
r

draws. Then
{\mathbb{P}}(Z_{i}^{r})=\left(1-\frac{1}{n}\right)^{r}
and thus, for
r=\beta n\log n
, we have that
{\mathbb{P}}(Z_{i}^{r})=n^{-\beta}
and therefore, a
union bound implies that

(9.18)

{\mathbb{P}}(T>\beta n\log n)\leq{\mathbb{P}}(\cup_{i\in[n]}Z_{i}^{r})\leq n{\mathbb{P}}(Z_{1}^{r})\leq n^{-\beta+1}.

∎

9.2.
Proof of Lemma
8.14

Proof of Lemma
8.14
.

First we make the following claim: Let
M\in{\mathbb{N}}
be arbitrary,

(p_{t})_{t\in{\mathbb{N}}}
be a set of uniform draws from
[n]
and let

T^{*}_{M}
be the random function defined in (
8.38
).

Then, almost surely over the value of
T^{*}_{M}
, the sequence of observations
D^{\prime}_{t}
from (
8.2
), has the
following law when
(S_{t})_{p_{t}}\neq 0
:

(9.19)

(D^{\prime}_{t})_{t\in[T^{*}_{M}]}\overset{{\mathcal{L}}}{=}\left(f_{\theta,\lambda}(S_{t},p_{t},-(S_{t})_{p_{t}})+V(S_{t},p_{t},q_{t})M^{1/2}Z_{t}\right)_{t\in[T^{*}_{M}]},

where each
Z_{t}
is an independent standard Gaussian
independent of
(p_{t})_{t\in{\mathbb{N}}}
and thus
T^{*}_{M}
.

Now we prove Lemma
8.14
.
Notice, for each time
t\in[T^{*}]
, Algorithm
15
proposes
coordinates
i\in[n]
which, if accepted, would transition to
S_{t}-2(S_{t})_{i}e_{i}
. Therefore,
\|S_{t}\|_{0}=\|S_{1}\|_{0}
for the entire
run-time of Algorithm
15
. Then, by Lemma
7
7

7

Note, this is not
a circular argument as we do not use Lemma
8.14
in the proof
of Lemma
8.7
.

8.7
,
S_{\rm HOM}
has

\|S_{\rm HOM}\|_{0}=n
, giving that
\|S_{t}\|_{0}=n
for all
t\in[T^{*}]
. Thus, using the claim with
M=M_{1}
,
we have, almost surely over
T^{*}
, that

(9.20)

(D^{\prime}_{t})_{t\in[T^{*}]}\overset{{\mathcal{L}}}{=}\left(f_{\theta,\lambda}(S_{t},p_{t},-(S_{t})_{p_{t}})+V_{n}\sqrt{M_{1}}Z_{t}\right)_{t\in[T^{*}]},

where each
Z_{t}
is an independent standard normal
random variable independent of
(p_{t})_{t\in[T^{*}]}
and thus

T^{*}
. Dividing both sides of the above displayed equation by

V_{n}\sqrt{M_{1}}
gives the result.

It remains to prove the claim. Recall the difference of
H_{\frac{r+1}{2},\gamma}(S_{t}+e_{p_{t}}(q_{t}-(S_{t})_{p_{t}}))-H_{\frac{r+1}{2},\gamma}(S_{t})
from Remark
8.3
. We can see that
the difference
D^{\prime}_{t}
is equivalent to this difference plus the random threshold term
K_{p_{t},t_{p_{t}},q_{t}}
. Therefore, using the results of Lemma

8.13
for
D^{\prime}_{t}
(i.e. for
D_{t}
with
q_{t}=-(S_{t})_{p_{t}}
and

\gamma=0
), we have, almost surely over
T^{*}_{M}
, that

(9.21)

\left(D^{\prime}_{t}\right)_{t\in[T^{*}_{M}]}\overset{{\mathcal{L}}}{=}\left(f_{\theta,\lambda}(S_{t},p_{t},-(S_{t})_{p_{t}})+V(S_{t},p_{t},q_{t})M^{1/2}Z_{t}\right)_{t\in[T^{*}_{M}]},

where each
Z_{t}
is an independent standard Gaussian
independent of
(p_{t})_{t\in{\mathbb{N}}}
and thus
T^{*}_{M}
.
∎

9.3.
Proofs of Lemmas

8.18
,

8.19
,
8.20
And
8.21

Proof of Lemma
8.18
.

We have
g(\sigma,i,a)=\langle(\sigma+ae_{i})^{\otimes r}-\sigma^{\otimes r},W\rangle
. As each element of
W
is standard Gaussian,
we have the law equivalence

(9.22)

g(\sigma,a,i)\overset{{\mathcal{L}}}{=}{\mathcal{N}}(0,\|(\sigma+ae_{i})^{\otimes r}-\sigma^{\otimes r}\|_{F}^{2}).

This proved the first equality in law for
g(\sigma,a,i)
.
And thus, the statement is proven.
∎

Proof of Lemma
8.19
.

We have that
(\sigma+ae_{i})^{\otimes r}-\sigma^{\otimes r}=\sum_{k=1}^{r}\binom{r}{k}a^{k}e_{i}^{\otimes k}\otimes\sigma^{\otimes r-k}
.
As any two
k
summands have differing support, then we have that

(9.23)

\|(\sigma+ae_{i})^{\otimes r}-\sigma^{\otimes r}\|^{2}_{F}=\sum_{k=1}^{r}\|\binom{r}{k}a^{k}e_{i}^{\otimes k}\otimes\sigma^{\otimes r-k}\|^{2}_{F}=\sum_{k=1}^{r}\binom{r}{k}^{2}a^{2k}\|\sigma\|^{r-k}_{0}.

As each element of the summation is positive on the right-hand side above,
we have automatically the following upper bound:

(9.24)

\|(\sigma+ae_{i})^{\otimes r}-\sigma^{\otimes r}\|^{2}_{F}=\sum_{k=1}^{r}\binom{r}{k}^{2}a^{2k}\|\sigma\|^{r-k}_{0}\leq\|\sigma\|_{0}^{r-1}\sum_{k=1}^{r}\binom{r}{k}^{2}a^{2k}.

The proof follows from taking the square root and realizing the right-hand
side above is maximized when
a=2
.

∎

Proof of Lemma
8.20
.

Define the function
g(x)=x^{r}
. As both inner products in
f(\sigma,\theta,a,i)
have rank one structure, we equivalently have

(9.25)

f(\sigma,\theta,a,i)=g(\langle\sigma+ae_{i},\theta\rangle)-g(\langle\sigma,\theta\rangle).

Therefore, by the mean value theorem, we have that

(9.26)

f(\sigma,\theta,a,i)=g^{\prime}(c)a\theta_{i},

where
c\in[\min(\langle\sigma+ae_{i},\theta\rangle,\langle\sigma,\theta\rangle),\max(\langle\sigma+ae_{i},\theta\rangle,\langle\sigma,\theta\rangle)]
.
We then consider two cases:

First, when
a\theta_{i}\geq 0
, we have the bound

(9.27)

C_{\rm Signa\ell}\min(\langle\sigma+ae_{i},\theta\rangle^{r-1},\langle\sigma,\theta\rangle^{r-1})a\theta_{i}\leq f(\sigma,\theta,a,i)\leq C_{\rm Signa\ell}\max(\langle\sigma+ae_{i},\theta\rangle,\langle\sigma,\theta\rangle)^{r-1}a\theta_{i},

as the assumption on the non-negativity of the inner
products ensures that
g^{\prime}(c)=rc^{r-1}
is non-decreasing the interval
[\min(\langle\sigma+ae_{i},\theta\rangle,\langle\sigma,\theta\rangle)^{r-1},\max(\langle\sigma+ae_{i},\theta\rangle,\langle\sigma,\theta\rangle)^{r-1}]
.

Second, when
a\theta_{i}\leq 0
, we reverse the above bound. This leads to

(9.28)

C_{\rm Signa\ell}\max(\langle\sigma+ae_{i},\theta\rangle^{r-1},\langle\sigma,\theta\rangle^{r-1})a\theta_{i}\leq f(\sigma,\theta,a,i)\leq C_{\rm Signa\ell}\min(\langle\sigma+ae_{i},\theta\rangle,\langle\sigma,\theta\rangle)^{r-1}a\theta_{i}.

∎

Proof of Lemma
8.21
.

Let
g(x)=x^{\frac{r+1}{2}}
. We have that

(9.29)

h(s,a)=\gamma(g(s+a)-g(s)).

Therefore, by the mean value theorem, we have that

(9.30)

h(s,a)=\gamma g^{\prime}(c)a,

where
c\in[\min(s,s+a),\max(s,s+a)]
.
Since
g^{\prime}(\gamma c)=\gamma\frac{r+1}{2}c^{\frac{r-1}{2}}
is increasing
in c over the interval
[\min(s,s+a),\max(s,s+a)]
as each of
s
and

s+a
are non-negative, the result follows.
∎
